Chapter 1

Report Summary




01
                                                                                        Report Summary




1.1       IPART has set Sydney Water’s prices for the next 5 years
We have reviewed Sydney Water’s prices and have made decisions on the maximum prices it
can charge its customers from 1 October 2025 to 30 June 2030. This report outlines our
decisions and explains how and why we reached them.

Sydney Water is the largest water utility in Australia. It owns and operates the water, wastewater
and some of the stormwater infrastructure and systems that serve 5.4 million people and more
than 122,000 businesses in the Sydney Metropolitan, Illawarra and Blue Mountains regions. 1

Water and wastewater services are essential, and it is vital that Sydney Water’s services and
infrastructure keep pace with Sydney’s growth, meet community expectations on social and
environmental performance, and deliver value to customers. Sydney Water needs the capability
to maintain and replace its assets, deliver necessary infrastructure to meet the increasing
demands of population growth, and manage the challenges of climate change.

We consider prices and bills need to be higher to allow Sydney Water to fund the significant
increase in efficient costs of meeting those requirements. However, the prices we have set are
lower than Sydney Water proposed, as we consider Sydney Water’s efficient costs over the next
5 years are lower than it proposed.

IPART’s role is to set the maximum prices Sydney Water can charge for these services. In doing
so, we set maximum prices that mean customers would only pay for expenditure that is efficient.
Sydney Water may set prices below the maximum with the approval of the Treasurer, and it also
provides a range of hardship assistance for customers struggling to pay their bills.

Sydney Water is, like most other water businesses in the world, a monopoly. Most customers
cannot shop around for a provider which offers them better value, lower charges or better
services. Sydney Water is not, in the main, competing with any other businesses to attract and
keep customers. In a competitive market, businesses are compelled to adapt, innovate and keep
prices competitive. If they do not, they will not survive.

IPART seeks to set efficient prices which reflect the maximum that Sydney Water would need to
charge in a competitive environment. This means customers do not necessarily pay for what it
does spend or wants to spend, but what it should spend. It also means that it generates the
revenue that an efficient business needs to plan, construct and maintain infrastructure as well as
fund its day-to-day operations.

Our decisions, and the maximum prices, result in customers only paying what Sydney Water
requires to efficiently deliver quality water services.

In addition to our legislative responsibilities and our framework for regulating water businesses,
we have also considered the following factors when setting maximum prices as required by the
NSW Government:

•     the cost-of-living impacts of Sydney Water’s prices

•     the effectiveness of existing rebates to manage the social impacts of Sydney Water prices

•     opportunities to adjust project timelines to minimise price impacts and, if necessary, to
      reduce the proposed capital programs in line with least cost planning principles

•     deliverability of the proposed capital plans based on capability and market conditions.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 8
                                                                                                          Report Summary




1.2       Typical bills will increase by 11.8% plus inflation from 1 October,
          then from 1 July 2026 by around 5.1% a year plus inflation

Typical annual water and wastewater bills will increase by $144 (or 11.8%) plus
inflation, or $168 (or 13.8%) including inflation in 2025–26

In discussing typical residential bills, we refer to the combined water and wastewater bill a typical
residential house would pay. a Some Sydney Water customers also pay a stormwater drainage
charge to Sydney Water, which means their bills are higher.

Our maximum prices see typical household bills for water and wastewater services increase in
2025-26 by $144 (or 11.8%) before we add inflation. After we add inflation, the overall increase in
the typical household bill is $168 (or 13.8%) in 2025-26. This is lower than the increase proposed
by Sydney Water of $253 (or 20.7%), including inflation. b Typical bills then increase by an average
of $77 (or 5.1%) a year plus inflation, in each of the following 4 years. c This is lower than Sydney
Water’s proposed increases of $110 (or 6.8%) each year plus inflation.

The typical household bill will increase from $1,220 in 2024–25 to:

•     $1,388 in 2025–26 including inflation, which is $84 (or 5.7%) lower than proposed by Sydney
      Water in 2025-26
      — This is $1,363 before adding inflation, compared to Sydney Water’s proposal of $1,439

•     $1,695 in 2029–30 in the last year of the 2025 determination period, plus inflation, which is
      $218 (or 11.4%) lower than proposed by Sydney Water in 2029-30.


Price increases apply to variable usage charges more than fixed service charges

Household water bills include fixed water and wastewater service charges, and a variable water
usage charge. Sydney Water customers expressed a preference for higher variable water usage
charges relative to fixed service charges. This would allow them to make usage choices and
potentially exert more control over their bills. This change would mean relatively lower fixed
service charges, which would be set to generate the remaining revenue we estimate Sydney
Water will need to cover its efficient costs.

The variable water usage charge is important because it sends a signal to customers about how
much water not only costs to collect, make safe and distribute – but also how expensive it will be
to increase supply if needed. For Sydney Water we estimate this value to be around $3.90 per
kilolitre. This is a proxy for water scarcity and the value of water and promotes efficient water use.




a
    This is based on consumption of 200 kilolitres a year, which is the average amount of water an individually metered
    household in Sydney Water’s area of operations uses.
b
    We have applied the Australian Bureau of Statistics’ Australian All Capitals year to March 2025 inflation of 2.4% for
    2025-26 prices.
c
    We do not know what inflation will be in the future, so we set prices in each year from 2026-27 to 2029-30 in today’s
    dollars. We then calculate and add inflation to those prices on 1 July each year, when we know what the inflation rate
    has turned out to be.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                      Page | 9
                                                                                         Report Summary




Our decision is for the variable water usage charge to rise from $2.67 to $3.58 per kilolitre by
2029–30 (plus inflation). This is more in line with both costs and the scarcity value of water. It is
higher than Sydney Water’s proposed water usage charge of $3.12 in each year from 2025–26.

Households and businesses with low or moderate water usage may benefit from a higher
variable water usage charge (and lower fixed service charges). However, we note that higher
water users including some large families and industrial customers may face a higher percentage
increase in their bills.


We have balanced customer affordability with the need to invest to deliver safe
and reliable services

Many households and businesses are dealing with higher cost-of-living pressures. Affordability
was the key theme in the feedback we received on both our Issues Paper and Draft Report.
Sydney Water also stated in its pricing proposal it was a top priority for its customers. 2

Under our maximum prices, typical bills increase 11.8% plus inflation in 2025–26, the first year of
the price path. In each of the following years, they increase around 5.1% plus inflation. This price
path reflects, to an extent, how Sydney Water’s efficient costs increase across the 5 years to
2029-30.

We consider affordability is critical for essential services like water and wastewater. We are
acutely aware of current cost-of-living pressures, and the impact higher prices will have on
household budgets. We have sought, and will continue, to protect consumers from unjustified
price increases for water and wastewater services. However, increases in maximum prices are
required now to fund efficient expenditure to deliver reliable and safe water services and to
reflect a fair contribution between today’s customers and future customers.

While less than Sydney Water proposed, the revenue that our maximum prices will generate over
the next 5 years should allow Sydney Water to plan and construct new and replacement assets
to meet customer needs and performance standards. It should also allow Sydney Water to
replace assets at the end of their life, provide safe drinking water and meet its environmental
performance obligations.


Sydney Water should be able to manage uncertainties

We have increased the amount of forecast capital expenditure to $13.2 billion to be included in
Sydney Water’s prices over the 5 years to 2029-30. This is an increase of around $2.5 billion from
our Draft Report. We consider this expenditure is necessary and efficient so that Sydney Water
can continue to provide safe and reliable water services over the next 5 years.

We heard from many stakeholders including NSW Health, local councils and industry organisations
that they support increased capital expenditure to address aging infrastructure, servicing
population growth and to deliver safe drinking water and improved environmental outcomes.

This large increase in capital expenditure will facilitate Sydney Water continuing to provide safe
clean reliable drinking water, adapting to meet the NSW Government’s policies and regulatory
obligations including servicing population growth, and protecting our waterways and
environment.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 10
                                                                                                     Report Summary




After releasing our Draft Report the Secretary of the NSW Department of Planning Housing and
infrastructure wrote to IPART providing an update on the 2025 Sydney Housing Supply Forecast.
She advised that the updated 2025 Sydney Housing Supply Forecast for Greater Sydney is
161,687 dwellings expected to be completed by June 2029. 3 This represents an increase of
23,156 net dwellings, compared to the 2023 housing forecast. The letter also referred to the
various reforms that have recently been implemented by the NSW Government to increase
housing supply under the National Housing Accord which targets the delivery of 263,000 new
homes in Greater Sydney by June 2029.

The maximum price we have determined will facilitate Sydney Water being able to build the
infrastructure needed to support the 2025 Sydney Housing Supply Forecast. The higher target in
the National Housing Accord includes additional housing in transport orientated development
and small and medium rise developments which may use existing infrastructure. If Sydney Water
needs to invest more than we anticipate, under IPART’s water regulation approach, it would be
open to the Tribunal 2030 to include all efficient capital expenditure in its asset base when the
Tribunal sets prices in 2030.

In September 2025, we received information from Sydney Water about the potential impact of
data centres on future system costs and water demand. 4 Data centres are large-scale information
processing hubs that typically need high volumes of water for cooling. While the scale, number
and timing of such facilities are uncertain, the additional system infrastructure and water
resources needed may be significant. Sydney Water estimates the water needs of data centres
may be up to 250 megalitres a day by 2035. d Sydney Water did not include the additional costs
of servicing, or water demand from, data centres in its pricing proposal.

Due to both the preliminary information available and the considerable uncertainty of large-scale
data centre development, we have not included an allowance for additional efficient costs of
serving them in this determination. We also consider that Sydney Water would be able to levy
upfront charges on developers, subject to compliant Developer Servicing Plans, to recover the
costs of existing and new infrastructure it needs, as well as any higher operating costs it incurs, to
serve these customers. Sydney Water may also enter into a Negotiated Services Agreement with
large non-residential customers, including data centres. There may also be scope for these
services to be provided using recycled water, which could be delivered by Sydney Water or
third-party operators licensed under the Water Industry Competition Act 2006 (WIC Act).

Nonetheless, there is a possibility that Sydney Water may need to expand its system capacity
materially over the next few years, potentially at significant additional cost. It may also sell more
water than we have allowed for in setting prices. If the need to service data centres accelerates in
the next few years, and the efficient costs and sales volumes change materially from those we
have used to set prices in this determination, we may consider reviewing and setting new prices
before 2029-30.




d
    For comparison, we estimate Sydney Water’s average daily water sales in 2029-30 to be around 1,327 megalitres per
    day.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                Page | 11
                                                                                                        Report Summary




While we have used relevant current information in making our decisions, costs, demand and
service requirements may change as the needs of Sydney Water’s customers change. Sydney
Water’s licensed service standards may shift. Unforeseen events may occur. As Sydney Water’s
operating environment changes, it will need to reprioritise to drive efficiencies, and innovate to
deliver its customer services and outcomes at the lowest sustainable cost, in a manner that
promotes long-term customer value.

IPART does not approve individual projects or expenditure. IPART’s decisions provide Sydney
Water an overall efficient envelope of revenue to operate its business over the next 5 years. Our
final decisions result in a forecast average annual revenue requirement of $3.7 billion, which is
17% higher than the annual average revenue requirement in the 2020 determination.

Figure 1.1 Comparison of average annual revenue requirement ($million, $2024-25)




Note: Figures include the notional revenue requirement, including adjustments
Source: Sydney Water pricing proposal; Sydney Water submission to IPART Draft Report; IPART analysis.

We forecast that Sydney Water will receive around $548 million a year more than over the 2020
determination period. This level of revenue provides Sydney Water with the financial resources to
operate its business, adapt to changes and reprioritise its expenditure to deliver safe and reliable
water services that are in the long-term interest of consumers. If unplanned events occur which
mean Sydney Water needs to invest more than we anticipate, we would typically add all efficient
capital expenditure to its asset base when we set prices again in 2030.

Our final decisions on maximum prices will facilitate Sydney Water providing safe, reliable
drinking water, adapting to meet the NSW Government’s policies and regulatory obligations
including servicing population growth, and protecting our waterways and environment.

Typical bills are comparable to other Australian water utilities

As set out below, under our decisions Sydney Water’s typical bill is around the average of other
similar water businesses around Australia.

Figure 1.2 Typical annual bills for major water businesses in 2025–26 ($2025–26)




Note: The typical bill shown for Sydney Water reflects our decisions on prices as set out in this report. The typical bill for Hunter Water
reflects the prices set out in our Final Report for Hunter Water’s prices 2025-2030. Figures for other major water utilities are from the Bureau
of Meteorology 2023-24 and are CPI adjusted to $2025-26.
Source: IPART analysis using data from the Bureau of Meteorology.


Typical bills under our maximum prices will be higher for most customers. However, one of our
findings is that most residential customers should be able to afford the increases, albeit with
some financial impacts. We note that most pensioners receive a pensioner rebate off their
Sydney Water bill from the NSW Government. e

We have considered the issue of affordability carefully, knowing affordability concerns are
different for different customers and different households.

The United Nations suggests that water costs should not exceed 3% of household income. 5 While
we know that any price increases are unwelcome, our analysis suggests that under our prices,
the typical customer in almost all customer groups does not breach this benchmark.

However, there is a small subset of customers who may exceed the 3% threshold and may need
additional financial support. These are:

•    recipients of JobSeeker payments

•    single households receiving parenting payments

•    single households receiving the age pension, disability pension or carer payment.




e
    The rebate each pensioner household served by Sydney Water receives is currently applied to the fixed service
    charges on each bill. Currently, the maximum rebate is 100% of the quarterly service charge for water, 86% of the
    quarterly service charge for wastewater, and if applicable, 50% of the quarterly service charge for stormwater.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                        Page | 13
                                                                                                     Report Summary




The current water pensioner rebate in NSW generally assists single and couple pensioner
households to remain below the 3% threshold, but as highlighted above, certain households
would exceed the threshold and may be experiencing financial vulnerability.

In addition, we have made recommendations to the NSW Government on improving the
effectiveness of rebates to help moderate the impact on more adversely affected households.
These recommendations include that the NSW Government:

•     considers temporarily expanding the eligibility of rebates to households that hold either a
      Health Care Card or Low Income Health Care Card
•     considers temporarily maintaining the absolute value of the 2024–25 Sydney Water
      pensioner rebate in nominal terms over each of the next 5 years, specifically, $67 for water,
      $532 for wastewater, $44 for stormwater (house) and $14 for stormwater (apartment).


1.3       Bill increases reflect efficient costs
The bill increases arising from our maximum prices are lower than proposed by Sydney Water. 6
However, they will be higher than customers currently pay. This is primarily because of our
decisions on the efficient costs Sydney Water should incur in delivering its services over the next
5 years. Though lower than Sydney Water proposed, relative to what we used to set prices in
2020 we have made decisions to increase Sydney Water’s efficient:

•     operating expenditure per year by $179 million or 11% over the 5-year determination period

•     capital expenditure per year on infrastructure by $1.2 billion or 89% over the 5-year
      determination period.

We have also added around $581 million in adjustments to Sydney Water’s revenue requirement
in 2025-26 to account for efficient costs it incurred, but did not recover, over the 2020 period. f

Overall, our decision is that the revenue Sydney Water needs to cover its efficient costs is around
$3.7 billion a year on average across the 5-year determination period. This is around $346 million
or 8% lower than proposed by Sydney Water. However, it is $548 million per year or 17% higher
than we used when we set its prices in 2020, which reflects our view that efficient expenditure
needed to deliver services has increased.




f
    These include adjustments for the demand volatility adjustment mechanism (DVAM), and the efficient costs Sydney
    Water incurred in the 2024-25, the year we deferred the price determination.

Under our maximum prices the typical residential bill would be around:

•     $1,388 in 2025–26, which is $84 or 5.7% lower than the $1,472 proposed by Sydney Water.

•     $1,695 in 2029–30, which is $218 or 11.4% lower than the $1,913 proposed by Sydney Water.


1.4        We have considered all feedback received from stakeholders
We heard from a range of stakeholders over our consultation period including individuals,
industry organisations, the Energy and Water Ombudsman NSW and the Justice and Equity
Centre. We received 477 submissions to our Issues Paper g, 197 submissions and 200 survey
responses to our Draft Report h and held a Public Hearing i attended by 108 stakeholders who
provided feedback on various aspects of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal.

Many stakeholders raised issues relating to:

•     affordability and the impacts of proposed price increases on cost-of-living for different
      customers

•     the use of fixed service charges versus variable usage charges, and the impacts of increased
      variable water usage charges on water use
•     Sydney Water’s proposed spending, including spending to cater for growth

•     the importance of spending on water infrastructure

•     the transparency of spending.



g
    Issues paper - IPART is reviewing prices for Sydney Water and WaterNSW Greater Sydney - 1 November 2024 | IPART
h
    Draft Report - Review of prices for Sydney Water Corporation from 1 October 2025 - May 2025 | IPART
i
    Sydney Water and WaterNSW Greater Sydney Public Hearing | IPART



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                      Page | 15
                                                                                           Report Summary




Cost-of-living and the affordability of Sydney Water’s bills was the dominant theme of comments
from stakeholders, and individual customers. Many households and businesses indicated that
large increases in the price they pay for an essential service like water will be difficult to manage.

We also heard from Sydney Water and other stakeholders on the challenges Sydney Water faces
such as climate change, system capacity and a rapidly growing service area. They suggest a
need to invest in infrastructure and an expanded workforce to maintain high quality water
services now and into the future.

We value the feedback that stakeholders have provided, and we have considered all views in
reaching the decisions set out in this report. Chapter 3 of this report summarises what we heard
from stakeholders in our review.


1.5       Final prices are higher than in our Draft Report
We have made several changes to our decisions on efficient costs, prices and bills since we
published our Draft Report in June 2025. These changes are summarised below, and have led to
higher increases in the typical residential bill of $73 in 2025-26, and by $133 plus inflation by
2029-30 compared to our Draft Report.

The changes we have made include:

•     Setting efficient capital expenditure at $13.2 billion over the next 5 years, which is

      — $2.5 billion higher than we used to set prices in the Draft Report
      — $3.4 billion (or 20%) lower than proposed by Sydney Water

•     Setting efficient operating expenditure at $9.4 billion over the next 5 years, which is

      — $0.4 billion higher than we used to set prices in the Draft Report
      — $0.5 billion (or 5%) lower than proposed by Sydney Water

•     Increasing the rate of return (the weighted average cost of capital – or ‘WACC’) Sydney Water
      should earn on its regulated assets from 3.2% to 3.3% due to changes in financial markets’
      cost of debt and equity

•     Lowering the rate of inflation we use to:
      — increase the value of Sydney Water’s regulatory asset base on which it earns a return, and
        a depreciation allowance
      — convert prices into $2025-26.


1.6       We assessed Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard
Under the Independent Pricing and Regulatory Tribunal Act 1992 (IPART Act) 7 we are required to
consider a range of matters when setting maximum water prices. Our Water Regulation
Handbook was developed to assist us in considering these matters, focusing on: customers,
costs, and credibility. It is underpinned by 12 guiding principles which both IPART and water
businesses use to develop and assess pricing proposals. Our Handbook provides further
information on our water regulation framework.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                    Page | 16
                                                                                                    Report Summary




Under this framework, we ask each water business to self-assess its pricing proposal as either
Standard, Advanced or Leading using our 12 guiding principles. We then conduct our own
assessment on this grading using the same criteria. Our grading is an important element in
shaping the approach we take in each price review. We can conduct a more streamlined review
of pricing proposals that we assess as Advanced or Leading. Proposals that are graded Standard,
Advanced or Leading may qualify for certain allowances and/or incentives.

We assessed Sydney Water’s proposal against each of the matters set out in the IPART Act and
the letter from the Premier 8 and we used our Water Regulation Handbook to help us make our
assessment. In summary, our decision is to grade Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard,
reflecting our findings that:

•     Sydney Water has shown a commitment to extensive customer consultation, understanding
      customer preferences and adopted some of these into its decision-making.
•     Sydney Water developed clear accountability features to track and report progress against
      the customer outcomes, which could have been enhanced by including more granular
      performance measures.
•     Sydney Water’s cost forecasts were in some instances higher than we consider to be
      efficient, however it still presented a credible path towards cost-efficiency in its pricing
      proposal.
•     Sydney Water developed a long-term plan to support its investment decision making and
      proposed expenditure.

Chapter 2

Assessment of Sydney Water’s
pricing proposal




02
                                                             Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




   Summary of our decision on Sydney Water’s pricing proposal grading


   We decided to grade Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard
   Our decision is to grade Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard, as it met the guiding
   principles in our Water Regulation Handbook for this grade.
   This decision is consistent with our preliminary grading in the Issues Paper, draft grading in
   the Draft Report and with Sydney Water’s self-assessment.


IPART sets maximum prices that Sydney Water can charge its customers for water, wastewater
and stormwater services, under the IPART Act. In setting these maximum prices, we assess
Sydney Water’s pricing proposal and make decisions to protect customers from the abuse of
monopoly powers, so the prices customers pay are fair, efficient and promote their long-term
interests.

In our assessment of Sydney Water’s proposal, we carefully balanced the factors we are required
to consider under the IPART Act. Each of the chapters in this report explain how we took into
account these considerations in reaching our decisions on Sydney Water’s costs, price settings,
prices, and service standards.


The considerations under sections 14A(2) and 15(1) of the IPART Act have been central
to our approach in setting maximum prices
We applied the considerations in the IPART Act and the letter from the Premier when setting
Sydney Water’s maximum prices. Those considerations include affordability; Sydney Water’s cost
of providing water, wastewater and stormwater services; the need to protect Sydney Water’s
customers from abuses of monopoly power; the effect of our prices on general inflation over the
medium term; the need for Sydney Water to be more efficient so as to reduce costs for the
benefit of its customers and taxpayers; the social impacts of our prices; the need for ecologically
sustainable development, and standards of quality, reliability and safety.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                          22
                                                           Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




Matters for IPART to consider when setting water prices




We used our Water Regulation Handbook when we assessed Sydney Water’s proposal. Our
Water Regulation Handbook includes a water regulation framework based on customers, costs
and credibility and provides a useful system for analysing the considerations the Premier required
us to take into account as well as the considerations in the IPART Act we must or may take into
account.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                        23
                                                            Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




2.1       We applied our new water regulation framework
Our new water regulation framework, set out in our Water Regulation Handbook, aims to
encourage each water business to develop pricing proposals that:

•     promote their customers’ interests

•     keep their costs as low as possible

•     enhance their business’ credibility.

The framework is centred around water businesses developing pricing proposals that promote
customer value. The framework encourages water businesses to actively involve and engage
with their customers and bring customers into the decision-making process when they set
outcomes. This is essential if water businesses are to identify better ways of delivering their
services that align with their customers’ preferences.

The framework is underpinned by 12 guiding principles (see Figure 2.1 and our Water
Regulation Handbook).

Figure 2.1 The water regulation framework and the 12 guiding principles




Source: IPART, Water Regulation Handbook, July 2023, p 2.


We assess the quality of the water business’s pricing proposal based on the grading rubric
outlined in Appendix B, which informs how we undertake our price review. There are 3 possible
grades for the pricing proposal: Standard, Advanced or Leading (see Box 2.1).




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                         24
                                                                Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




        Box 2.1 There are 3 possible grades under the water regulation framework
        The grades for a water business’s pricing proposal are:

        •    Leading – for businesses that are industry leaders in understanding their
             customers, innovating to deliver services customers want and driving costs
             efficiencies. The business also demonstrates how it delivers significant
             improvement in customer value through a combination of quantitative and
             qualitative evidence.
        •    Advanced – for businesses that demonstrate very strong understanding of their
             customers, and are broadly at the cost efficiency frontier.
        •    Standard – for businesses that conduct meaningful customer engagement and
             have a credible path towards the cost efficiency frontier. This grade is consistent
             with good practice in the NSW water sector.
        Source: IPART, Water Regulation Handbook, July 2023.




2.1.1       Sydney Water self-assessed its proposal as Standard

Sydney Water self-assessed its proposal as Standard. In developing its proposal, it prioritised 3
focus principles from our water regulation framework that it considered reflected its customers’
priorities. These focus principles were given greater emphasis in our review of the pricing
proposal compared to the other principles. Sydney Water’s focus principles were:

•   customer outcomes.

•   balance risk and long-term performance
•   equitable and efficient cost recovery.

In making its self-assessment, Sydney Water told us it delivered a representative customer
engagement program that gave customers an opportunity to shape the services it plans to
deliver. Further, Sydney Water notes it has customer-led performance metrics and targets which
are regularly updated through multiple customer-facing channels (including website and bill
inclusions) to hold itself accountable. Sydney Water also considers it has a robust hardship
program that supports customers, adopts industry best practice and works with community
partners to improve processes.

On cost principles, Sydney Water told us its proposed expenditure reflects the minimum
expenditure required to deliver its service obligations and customer outcomes sustainably into
the long-term. Sydney Water considers it has moderated costs as far as practical and proposed
an ambitious efficiency target for its business (above measured economy-wide productivity
performance) to achieve affordable outcomes. Sydney Water notes it will accept more risk where
it benefits customers and it has resilience to absorb cost impacts arising from changes in its
operating environment.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                             25
                                                                        Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




On credibility principles, Sydney Water indicates it has a strong track record of delivering
infrastructure. Further, it outlines how it has expanded its delivery capacity and capability for the
proposed increase in capital expenditure. Sydney Water told us it is committed to continual
improvement, including through its corporate performance reporting and enterprise planning
processes.

More information on Sydney Water’s self-assessment is available in its pricing proposal. a


2.1.2      Our preliminary assessment informed our approach to the review

Our preliminary grading for Sydney Water was Standard (see our 2025 Sydney Water price
review - Issues Paper). As a result, we took a more forensic review of its expenditure.

To inform our decisions, we engaged independent experts, AtkinsRéalis, to review Sydney
Water’s proposed operating and capital expenditure. We asked AtkinsRéalis to specifically
examine Sydney Water’s:

•      strategic planning and risk
•      performance over the 2020 determination period
•      proposed forecast operating expenditure
•      proposed forecast capital expenditure
•      proposed water demand.

Our decisions on Sydney Water’s efficient expenditure are set out in Chapter 4 and Chapter 5 of
this report.


2.2        Our decision is that Sydney Water’s proposal is Standard

Our decision is:

                1.   To grade Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard.




a
    See Sydney Water, 2024 Pricing Proposal to IPART, September 2024, p319.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                     26
                                                                      Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




Our reasons for a Standard grading

                    Customers
                    Over a well-organised, multi-phased customer engagement program, Sydney Water developed
                    a comprehensive understanding of its customers’ key priorities. It then developed customer
                    outcomes based on these key properties, and included clear timeframes for achieving these
                    outcomes in its pricing proposal.

                    While many aspects of Sydney Water’s customer engagement were meaningful, some elements
                    could be improved for the next price review. These include ensuring that all topics consulted on
                    are well-explained to customers, adequate time is allocated to develop customers’ foundational
                    knowledge and deliberate on topics, and supporting materials are clear and tailored to the
                    audience.


                    Costs
                    Sydney Water demonstrated a commitment to improving its efficiency through integrating a cost
                    efficiency strategy into its pricing proposal. However, Sydney Water’s proposed costs were
                    materially higher than we considered efficient. We found they could be better supported by
                    cost-benefit analysis, reasonably deferred to a later period or lowered to optimise customer
                    value.


                    Credibility
                    The credibility of Sydney Water’s proposal is supported by a clear path towards meeting
                    customer outcomes and improving cost efficiency. Sydney Water showed a credible
                    commitment on areas of improvement that are of value to customers.


We took a holistic approach to assessing Sydney Water’s pricing proposal. We considered
Sydney Water’s self-assessment of its proposal against each of the 12 guiding principles.
However, we allocated a single grade to the pricing proposal as a whole, rather than allocating a
grade to each principle, consistent with our Water Regulation Handbook. This recognises that
each pricing proposal’s grading may not be a simple weighted average of the grades for each of
the 12 principles. It also reflects the importance of water businesses developing robust pricing
proposals that balance customer, cost and credibility outcomes according to customer
preferences.

Our draft decision was to agree with Sydney Water’s self-assessment of its pricing proposal and
maintain our preliminary Standard grading. This grading was supported by the Justice and Equity
Centre, which noted that Sydney Water demonstrated a genuine commitment to develop a
proposal shaped by consumers’ values and priorities. 9

We maintained our draft decision to grade Sydney Water’s pricing proposal as Standard. In reaching
this decision, we considered the matters set out in sections 14A(2) and 15(1) of the IPART Act.

Sydney Water demonstrated a Standard pricing proposal because it:

•   Undertook a multi-phase customer engagement program, which led to meaningful
    engagement from customers on several topics. Appendix C provides more information on
    Sydney Water’s customer engagement.
•   Used feedback from its customer engagement program to develop 3 outcomes, based on
    the priorities that mattered most to its customers:

    — deliver a great customer experience
    — provide safe, clean, reliable drinking water every day



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                      27
                                                              Assessment of Sydney Water’s pricing proposal




    — facilitate Sydney Water protecting waterways and the environment now and for the
      future.

    We note these outcomes generally align with Sydney Water’s ‘business as usual’ operations,
    as it needs to comply with drinking water guidelines and environmental obligations, as well as
    minimum service levels to protect customers in its Operating Licence.
•   Introduced clear accountability features to track and report progress against the customer
    outcomes, which could have been enhanced by including more granular performance
    measures. Sydney Water also linked proposed expenditure to the customer outcomes
    through targeted strategic investment plans.

•   Presented a credible path towards cost-efficiency in its pricing proposal, despite us finding
    Sydney Water’s cost forecasts to be overly risk-averse in some instances. It incorporated an
    annual productivity efficiency factor of 0.7%. Additionally, it originally proposed to introduce
    mechanisms which aim to incentivise Sydney Water to achieve efficiencies across its capital
    and operating expenditure, as well as deliver on outcomes for leakage reduction.
•   Developed a long-term plan to support its investment decision making and proposed
    expenditure, which broadly aligns with the NSW Government’s strategy to improve water
    security and resilience. 10 Its approach could be improved by Sydney Water more clearly
    setting out the service levels and risks it needs to plan for in the short-term. This would
    help it better identify costs that could reasonably be deferred until the next 5-year
    determination period.

3.1      We consulted with stakeholders to inform our decisions
We gave stakeholders several opportunities to have their say on this review:

•     1 November 2024, we published Sydney Water’s 2025 pricing proposal and an Issues Paper
      summarising the key aspects of the proposal. We invited stakeholders to make written
      submissions over 5 and a half weeks.

•     21 November 2024, we held an online Public Hearing which allowed the community to let us
      know what they thought about the pricing proposal and ask questions directly to
      Sydney Water and to IPART.

•     20 May 2025, we published our Draft Report outlining our draft decisions on Sydney Water’s
      proposed expenditure, prices and bill impacts. We invited stakeholders to make written
      submissions and complete a short customer survey over 5 weeks.

We heard from a range of stakeholders over the course of this review, mostly individual
stakeholders. We also received submissions from organisations including:

•     Council and council groups across the Sydney region
•     Urban Development Institute of Australia NSW and Meriton Properties Services Pty Ltd and
      others in the development and construction industry
•     Water Services Association of Australia (WSAA), Stormwater NSW, the Cooks River Alliance
      and other water and environmental groups

•     Justice and Equity Centre (JEC), coNEXA, Western Sydney Leadership Dialogue and other
      businesses and advocacy groups
•     Government organisations such as the Energy & Water Ombudsman NSW (EWON) and
      NSW Health.

•     Sydney Water.

We thank all stakeholders for their time and effort spent to provide us with feedback through
these avenues. We considered all feedback received to inform our analysis and decisions on
Sydney Water’s prices. Our consultation with stakeholders has helped us to consider the social
impacts of our determination and recommendations under section 15(1) of the IPART Act.

                                                                         What we heard from stakeholders




3.2      What stakeholders told us about our draft decisions
Throughout our consultation, stakeholders raised important concerns mainly around:

•     affordability and the impacts of price increases on cost-of-living for customers

•     capital expenditure and the trade-offs between bill impacts and pre-treatment and growth
      expenditure

•     operating expenditure allowance and the impacts on asset renewals

•     Sydney Water’s proposed acquisition of Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater business

•     variable and usage charges and the extent of rebates

•     risk and incentive mechanisms and performance measures and targets.


3.2.1    Many stakeholders commented on the draft prices and affordability

Most individuals were concerned about the draft bill impacts
Throughout our review, the affordability of bills has been a primary concern for stakeholders.

In response to our Issues Paper, over 200 individuals submitted that Sydney Water’s proposed
price rises were too high given the current cost-of-living crisis. Many felt that the proposed 18%
increase in the first year was excessive, noting that wages have not increased at the same rate.
Some suggested that the bill increases should be smaller, in line with inflation, 11 or phased in
gradually, and one suggested Sydney Water should seek a greater share of funding from the
NSW Government. 12

We received similar feedback in response to our Draft Report. Forty-nine individual stakeholders
commented on affordability, with most of those submissions indicating that draft prices were too
high. Some submissions described the proposed increase as "substantial" 13 and an "unnecessary
further burden". 14


Perceived affordability of draft bills varied by income level

We asked 2 questions related to affordability in our customer survey, which received mixed
responses. More than half (55%) of respondents said they somewhat or strongly disagreed that
draft prices are affordable with little or no impact on their living standard or budget. Around half
(53%) also said they somewhat or strongly disagreed that Sydney Water’s services represented
value for money given the service Sydney Water provides.

There was some relationship between income and the perceived affordability of the draft prices.
Notably, most respondents with household incomes below $180,000 a year said that draft prices
are unaffordable, while two-thirds of respondents with incomes above $180,000 a year said draft
prices were affordable. Other metrics such as age and household size did not correlate with
perceived affordability.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                          31
                                                                         What we heard from stakeholders




Some organisations were concerned about the impact of draft bills on vulnerable
groups
Justice and Equity Centre (JEC) supported IPART's focus on affordability during the review but
questioned whether it is "reasonable or sustainable" for customers to bear the full cost of major
infrastructure investments for growth and resilience in their bills. It noted rising community
concern that these are "government policy" costs that should be funded from the government
budget, not directly by households. 15

The JEC's main recommendation was for a comprehensive, NSW Government led review of all
water assistance and support measures. It argued for a consistent, statewide approach to ensure
support is equitable regardless of location or water provider. 16

Energy and Water Ombudsman NSW (EWON) raised that “the cost-of-living benchmark applied
to the draft decision does not account for the impact that an increase in water prices will have for
the total housing costs for each customer group". 17 It highlighted an alternative measure of
housing stress to our Draft Report, suggesting that water bills should be considered as part of a
household's total housing costs (rent/mortgage + rates), where "housing stress" is defined as
spending more than 30% of income on these costs. 18

Community Housing Industry Association NSW (CHIA NSW) pointed out that social and
affordable housing residents are ineligible for existing rebates and their incomes (often social
security) will not keep pace with the proposed increases. 19

A few submissions, mostly from organisations, commented on the bill increases being reasonable
and necessary, and called for reinstating some of Sydney Water’s proposed expenditure to
ensure critical works can proceed.


3.2.2   Some stakeholders argued that the proposed revenue allowance would
        be insufficient to deliver infrastructure for growth

In setting our draft revenue envelope, we used a lower level of efficient capital expenditure on
growth than Sydney Water proposed.

In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water stated that the overall revenue over the
2025 determination period would not provide sufficient funds to deliver all the growth
infrastructure it considers is needed. As such, it requested that we include around $2 billion in
additional growth capital expenditure when setting final prices. 20 It estimated that “IPART’s draft
decision would only enable the delivery of 120,000 new homes by 2030… which is up to
75,000 fewer homes than [Sydney Water’s] Price Proposal would support … and significantly
below the number of new homes required to meet Housing Accord targets”. 21 It also mentioned
that the Sydney Housing Supply Forecast (SHSF) 2023, which informed our draft decision, does
not include recent government policies that will speed up housing construction. 22

Sydney Water proposed that should the $2 billion of ‘other’ growth expenditure not be reinstated,
IPART should adjust its growth expenditure allowance to reflect the draft SHSF-2024 forecast. In
the absence of a draft SHSF-2024, it requested a regulatory mechanism to allow an in-period
true-up if growth occurs faster than predicted. 23




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                          32
                                                                          What we heard from stakeholders




Other submissions on growth expenditure from organisations echoed similar concerns:

•   Councils generally raised concerns about how the SHSF-2023 does not include policy
    changes such as the Transport Oriented Development (TOD) program and the Low and
    Mid-Rise Housing Policy. Campbelltown Council’s submission that “IPART’s … significant
    [proposed] reduction has been made at a point in time where the capital expenditure for
    critical infrastructure should be increased” and that the proposed capital expenditure profile
    “would adversely impact the timing of the supply of some 40,000 dwellings for NSW” was
    typical of council submissions. 24 In total we received submissions from 10 councils
    referencing the provision of growth infrastructure.
•   Business and property organisations generally contended that IPART’s lower than proposed
    expenditure allowance results in short-term water bill savings at the expense of long-term
    investment resulting in broader cost-of-living impacts including housing affordability. 25
•   The Housing Industry Association notes that the lower than proposed capital expenditure
    puts an expectation on developers to fill the funding void through higher infrastructure
    contributions and that there is limited scope for developers to step in and increase
    contributions as infrastructure contributions have hit their ceiling for the industry. It contends
    that every dollar charged in infrastructure contributions has a multiplier effect on house
    prices. 26


3.2.3   Stakeholders generally supported prioritising the pre-treatment program

In response to our Draft Report, most stakeholders supported prioritising capital expenditure on
Sydney Water’s pre-treatment program.

Sydney Water’s submission strongly advocated for reinstating most of its proposed
pre-treatment program expenditure, arguing that IPART's draft decision exposes customers to
significant public health and economic risks. 27 It noted:

•   “The quality of raw water from upstream dams has consistently fallen outside the design
    limits of our water filtration plants for much of the past decade” and that “on two occasions in
    2022 we asked customers in Sydney’s outer suburbs to limit their use of drinking water as a
    means of ensuring we could supply enough safe and clean drinking water” and a “third such
    request was narrowly averted in 2024”. 28

•   “Without essential upgrades, Sydney faces a much greater risk of emergency water
    restrictions (which have not been effective in the past) or boil water notices and loss of water
    supply during moderate to high rainfall events, bushfires and droughts, undermining public
    confidence and putting public health and wellbeing at risk”. 29

NSW Health advised the narrow avoidance of boiled water alerts and challenges in maintaining
water quality during recent extreme weather events. It said in its submission that “in order to
manage the public health risks in the supply system, NSW Health notes the importance of
pre-treatment at Cascade, Nepean, Orchard Hills and Prospect to ensure effective control of raw
water quality. Some critical assets, including water filtration plants and treated water reservoirs
also need renewal”. 30




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                         33
                                                                         What we heard from stakeholders




Some Industry and council stakeholders strongly supported including the costs for the broad
program in setting the revenue envelope, raising the following concerns about our draft decision
to rephase expenditure on the program when setting prices:

•   SUEZ Australia and New Zealand, the Prospect operator, contended that the program is well
    justified as it reflects planning to support growing population and climate change. It stated
    that the risks of deferring expenditure include reduced plant output under stress, rising costs
    from short-term reactive strategies, and the eventual erosion in public trust. 31
•   Arup Australia Pty Ltd’s submission did not support the deferral of pre-treatment program
    expenditure, noting “the risk of not proceeding with the Prospect Pretreatment project cannot
    be robustly quantified”. 32
•   Other industry organisations and councils argued that there are significant social, health,
    and economic risks for the community from poor water quality, potential boil water notices,
    and crisis management.
•   Some organisations, such as Aurecon argued that the material difference in expenditure
    between Sydney Water’s proposal and IPART’s Draft Report “represent a significant
    difference in risk profile, for which customers have not been specifically engaged”. 33
•   Infrastructure Partnerships Australia raised concerns that “the decision sends a negative
    signal to market participants whereby a project which has progressed to the point of
    appointing a contractor is no longer deemed as sufficiently necessary to proceed”. 34
Of the 3 individual stakeholders who discussed the pre-treatment program in their submissions,
one supported deferring expenditure for the pre-treatment program. They felt it was not well
justified, and that customers should not pay for "speculative" projects that add to cost-of-living
pressures. 35 Another individual supported the program, agreeing that future growth would make
it harder to manage water quality and that deferral would reduce the reliability of Sydney's
water supply. 36
In the survey, we asked respondents how much they agreed with the statement: “Sydney Water
should invest in pre-treatment facilities now to reduce future water quality risks, noting that this
could add $10 to $20 a year to current bills”. Results showed that almost twice as many
respondents somewhat or strongly agreed (60%) that Sydney Water should invest in
pre-treatment in this review compared to those that somewhat or strongly disagree (31%).


3.2.4   Sydney Water requested higher funding for operating expenditure to fund
        growth, higher real prices, and its asset renewals

Our draft decision was to set Sydney Water’s core operating expenditure (day-to-day running
costs) at $6.5 billion, which is 6% lower than proposed ($7.0 billion). Sydney Water has asked for
this to be increased to $6.9 billion in its submission to the Draft Report. 37


Sydney Water proposed that we accept its proposed ‘trend’ increases
On growth, Sydney Water contended that its proposal to include operating expenditure growth
based on the number of new properties connecting to its network (about 1.4% per year) was
reasonable and based on bottom-up and top-down IPART analysis. 38 It submits that our draft
decision is not enough to cover the growth-related costs and changes in input prices. 39



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                        34
                                                                        What we heard from stakeholders




Our trend allowance also includes a component for expected ‘real price effects’, which are price
increases higher than inflation. In setting our draft revenue envelope, we included less than
Sydney Water proposed for labour, contractor, and material costs based on analysis by
AtkinsRéalis. Sydney Water asked that IPART reconsider the proposed reduction in real price
effects, noting that it faces guaranteed cost increases from staff pay agreements and
superannuation changes. 40


Sydney Water proposed that we accept the full maintenance ‘step change’ increases
Sydney Water’s revised position also includes the full reinstatement of proposed increases for
maintenance on its water and wastewater systems ($224 million combined). 41 Our draft decision
was lower than the proposed AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound recommendation. 42 Sydney Water
contended that if it receives less capital funding to replace old assets it would need more
operating funding to maintain existing assets and prevent failures. 43


Critical sewers program

Sydney Water also identified critical sewers, the broader asset renewals expenditure, and
operating expenditure as priority issues in its submission:

Our draft decision was to accept AtkinsRéalis’ lower range scenario for the critical sewers
program ($80 million per year over the regulatory period) based on the Sydney Water’s average
historical expenditure over the 2020-2024 period.

Sydney Water stated the 2020-2024 period was impacted by exceptional circumstances,
including COVID-19, extreme weather, and supply chain disruptions. It said it has “significantly and
demonstrably enhanced its delivery capacity” of the program by pointing to its current delivery of
$150 million in upgrades this year. 44 Its revised proposal was to sustain the 2025 level of
expenditure for the program with a 5% annual increase ($174 million per year). 45


Asset Renewals

Our draft decision was to accept AtkinsRéalis’ lower range scenario given that we considered that
Sydney Water had not strongly justified that current risk levels of assets were too high or that
customers were willing to pay more to reduce them.

Sydney Water reasoned that a lower than proposed allowance for asset renewals expenditure
will result in a "reactive" approach to renewals once an asset has completely failed. 46

Several councils and industry groups argued for more funding for Sydney Water on critical
sewers and asset renewals, warning that underfunding these programs increases the risk of
sewer overflows and environmental harm from aging infrastructure, and that a lower than
proposed allowance for the program poses a risk to public health. 47




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       35
                                                                                    What we heard from stakeholders




3.2.5    There was significant support to spread the costs of Sydney Water
         acquiring Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater assets

In our Draft Report, we made no provision in Sydney Water’s notional revenue requirement (NRR)
for acquiring Hawkesbury City Council’s (HCC’s) wastewater assets because of the uncertainty in
timing and costs. We have since received significant feedback on this matter.


Strong community and council support for the acquisition
In response to the Draft Report, HCC wrote to its ratepayers encouraging them to make a
submission to IPART in support of Sydney Water taking over HCC’s wastewater assets. a
We received 66 written submissions and several verbal submissions from HCC residents, who all
strongly supported Sydney Water taking over HCC’s wastewater assets. Stakeholders mentioned
that the acquisition would lead to lower bills for HCC residents and better environmental
outcomes for the Hawkesbury-Nepean River. “The cost of extending Sydney Water's wastewater
services to the Hawkesbury area should be shared amongst all of Sydney Water's customers ...
[which] would be a fair solution and not financially disadvantage Hawkesbury customers” was a
typical response. 48


Sydney Water also supports the acquisition
Sydney Water’s submission to our Draft Report supported integrating HCC wastewater customers
into its broader network. It proposed to recover these costs through increased regulated revenue
and suggested adjusting revenue in the next regulatory period if the acquisition is delayed. 49

It estimated the takeover would increase the average annual bill for a typical residential customer
by around $1-2 over the 2025 determination period. The NSW Government has the power to
direct Sydney Water to acquire the assets. However, this does not automatically mean the costs
would be passed on to customers unless the government also directs IPART to do so.


3.2.6 Stakeholders also commented on a variety of other key issues

Tax on Assets Free of Charge (AFOC)

Our draft decision was to set Sydney Water’s tax allowance to zero based on a ruling in the case of
Victoria Power Networks Pty Ltd v Commissioner of Taxation in the Full Federal Court of Australia. 50

In its submission to the Draft Report, Sydney Water mentioned that the court case did not apply
to its situation. It explained that it receives around $250 million in AFOC each year, largely from
developers, and that the Australian Taxation Office requires it to pay tax on those assets. 51

Consistent with our recent decision on Hunter Water’s prices for the 2025 determination period,
we have included a tax allowance for Sydney Water of $137.6 million over the
2025 determination period (see section 6.6.).




a
    Hawkesbury City Council, Council seeks Sydney Water acquisition of wastewater system - have your say



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                   36
                                                                        What we heard from stakeholders




Variable usage and fixed service charges
Our draft decision was to gradually increase the variable water usage charge from its current
level of $2.67/kL to $3.50/kL by 2029-30, based on Sydney Water’s long-run marginal cost
(LRMC) estimate and avoiding a negative water service charge. Feedback on our draft decision
was mixed:

•   Some individuals and industry groups supported increasing the variable usage component to
    manage customer impacts, promote efficient water use, and align with user-pays principles.
    Meriton Property Services suggested a variable usage charge as high as $6/kL, but it did not
    clearly explain how this value was derived. It suggested that IPART should allow
    Sydney Water to charge commercial prices to building owners that provide rental apartments
    in multi-unit buildings. 52
•   One individual noted that the variable usage charge should be capped at current levels, given
    that water is not currently scarce. 53 Another preferred a fully fixed charge structure. 54
•   Sydney Water supported our draft price structure “to better reflect the long-run marginal
    cost and customer preferences for more of the costs to be put on the variable usage
    charge”. 55 It agreed with our approach to set water usage charges in a way that does not drive
    negative service charges. 56

Responses to our customer survey showed a slight preference for higher variable charges
(46% for vs 40% against). Around 44% of respondents somewhat/strongly agreed that a higher
usage charge would lead them to use less water.


Rebates

In our Draft Report, we made recommendations to the Minister about the effectiveness of the
current rebates and potential improvements. 57 There was broad support from stakeholders for
affordability concerns to be addressed through targeted rebates to those most in need.

•   Justice and Equity Centre highlighted that increasing water prices will disproportionately
    impact large households and the growing number of long-term renters. It urged
    Sydney Water to consider expanding its own programs to provide more assistance to these
    groups. 58
•   The Community Housing Industry Association noted that social housing residents are hit
    hard by price increases but are not eligible for existing water rebates or hardship programs. It
    supported our rebate recommendations and suggested that the NSW Government directly
    subsidise measures that improve water efficiency in existing homes. 59
•   The Energy & Water Ombudsman NSW (EWON) stated that current rebates are "not fit for
    purpose" given the rise in usage charges and potential impact of drought pricing. It
    recommended that affordability must be considered alongside an alternative measure of
    housing stress rather than in isolation with water bills relative to income. 60

•   Bayside City Council considered that protecting vulnerable customers from utility cost
    increases should be addressed through targeted government policies, such as rebates or
    concessions - not by compromising critical infrastructure. 61




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       37
                                                                         What we heard from stakeholders




•   Sydney Water noted that for the purpose of setting pensioner concessions, it is limited by its
    existing billing system whereby rebates can only be set as a percentage of the service charge
    to whole numbers. 62

Our survey found strong support for making rebates available to more people. Fifty-five per cent
of respondents either strongly or somewhat supported expanding rebates to include Health Care
Card and Low-Income Health Care Card holders. There was also support for offering broader
rebates to any household experiencing financial difficulty, though respondents acknowledged
this would need extra taxpayer funding.


Revenue and incentive mechanisms

Sydney Desalination Plant cost pass-through

Our draft decision was to remove the existing Sydney Desalination Plant cost pass-through. Both
Sydney Water and Sydney Desalination Plant (SDP) disagreed with this decision. 63 Sydney Water
stated that removing the pass-through would “provide a disincentive for Sydney Water to
efficiently balance these costs of restrictions in managing our water supply network”. 64 It also
contended that SDP costs meet the criteria for a pass-through. 65 As an alternative, Sydney Water
suggested raising the trigger for drought pricing from the current 60% dam level to 75%. 66

One individual was in favour of removing the SDP pass-through, considering that it would push
Sydney Water to find cheaper water sources and the plant has barely been needed since 2020. 67

Incentive mechanisms

Sydney Water initially proposed to have the 3 incentive mechanisms (capital expenditure savings
scheme (CESS), the efficiency benefits sharing scheme (EBSS) and outcome delivery incentives
(ODIs) applied with some ‘carve-outs’. Our draft decision was to not accept these ‘carve-outs’,
which stakeholders commented on:

•   Sydney Water stated that it no longer supports applying the EBSS and CESS schemes. It
    suggested that the lower than proposed expenditure allowance in our draft decision would
    make it highly likely it would incur the 1% financial penalties to meet its essential compliance
    obligations. It also suggested that our draft decision would impact its ability to meet its
    leakage targets, leading to a penalty under the ODI scheme. 68
•   Sydney Desalination Plant supported the general principle of allowing exemptions for costs
    that are material and outside a regulated business’s control. It contended that this would
    prevent large gains or losses and supports efficient expenditure. 69
•   Justice and Equity Centre agreed with our decision to apply the incentive schemes without
    exemptions. However, it expressed concern about the leakage ODI, suggesting it should have
    larger penalties for failure compared to the rewards for success. 70




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                        38
                                                                          What we heard from stakeholders




Performance measures and targets

Our draft decision was to accept Sydney Water’s proposed performance goals, with some
proposed additional measures for a more holistic view of its performance.


Sydney Water revised some performance measures and targets, but disagrees with
reporting others
•     Sydney Water changed 10 of its proposed measures in response to our Draft Report,
      including updating how it reports on drinking water quality and the quality of treated
      wastewater. It proposed to publish data for 8 of our suggested measures as "supplementary
      information" without any targets. 71
•     It contended against some additional new measures we suggested (such as time in payment
      assistance programs or the time customers are restricted from supply), suggesting that it
      would make the public reporting inaccessible or the costs of tracking the measure would
      outweigh the value it provides. 72

•     Sydney Water proposed reviewing most targets after the final expenditure outcomes. It
      considered that its proposed targets were at risk from lower than proposed investment and
      may not be achievable. This includes targets for drinking water quality, leakage, reliability,
      quality of treated wastewater and others. 73


Feedback from other stakeholders
•     Justice and Equity Centre supported our draft decisions on this matter and recommended
      adding more measures related to customer service and affordability. It also suggested that
      Sydney Water and Hunter Water should report on a core set of consistent metrics to allow for
      better comparison. 74

•     Colliers Engineering and Design urged that Sydney Water be held accountable for its
      performance, delivery and cost-efficiency while enabling it to meet long-term infrastructure
      needs. 75


3.3       We have considered all stakeholder feedback
Consultation with the community is an important part of our water pricing review process. We
have considered all feedback provided on Sydney Water’s proposed prices in making our
decisions on maximum prices to apply from 1 October 2025.
The following chapters explain our decisions including our considerations of stakeholder
feedback.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                          39
Chapter 4



Operating expenditure




04
                                                                                  Operating expenditure




    Summary of our decisions on operating expenditure


    Our operating expenditure is $9.4 billion for 5 years

    At around $1.9 billion per year, this is:

    •   $86 million higher than our draft decision

    •   $105 million per year (or 5%) lower than Sydney Water’s proposal
    •   $179 million per year (or 11%) higher than we used to set prices in the 2020
        Determination.


    Core expenditure is set at $6.7 billion
    ‘Core operating expenditure’ is the everyday running costs of the business excluding bulk
    water costs. For the first time, we assessed this using our base-trend-step approach. Our
    decision includes lower trend and step components compared to Sydney Water’s
    proposal, and includes ongoing efficiency challenges to Sydney Water, to encourage it to
    continue to find and implement more efficient ways to operate, to manage costs in
    customers’ long-term interests.


    Bulk water expenditure is set at $2.6 billion
    This represents a significant increase compared to the bulk water cost used last time we
    set prices. The key change from Sydney Water’s proposal is lower prices from WaterNSW.

    Overall demand is relatively stable compared to the last time we set prices. However, we
    have set higher bulk water prices from WaterNSW, and for the first time, bulk water costs
    now include an annual allowance for the usage costs of desalinated water purchases from
    the Sydney Desalination Plant (SDP). Our final decision includes an allowance for bulk water
    purchases from SDP which is similar to the levels of Sydney Water’s purchases of bulk
    water from SDP in recent years. This is 7% lower than the amount proposed by Sydney
    Water. This represents about 7% of total purchases, and SDP total costs (fixed and variable)
    represent 46% of bulk water costs.



This chapter sets out our assessment of the level of operating expenditure Sydney Water
requires to operate its business efficiently over the 2025 determination period. Sydney Water’s
operating costs are:

•   the day-to-day expenses involved in running its business and maintaining the infrastructure
    and equipment it uses to provide services. This includes costs such as staff wages, electricity,
    contractors, maintenance, treatment operations and insurance. We refer to this as the ‘core
    operating expenditure’

•   the cost of bulk water, which is purchased from WaterNSW or the Sydney Desalination Plant
    (SDP) at prices set by IPART.

We will discuss these 2 types of expenditure separately.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                 Page | 41
                                                                                                  Operating expenditure




In May 2025, we released our Draft Report, which included $6.5 billion in core operating
expenditure and $2.4 billion in bulk water costs, 6% and 18% lower than Sydney Water’s proposal
respectively. 76 In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water made a revised proposal with core
expenditure of $6.9 billion (0.9% lower than its original proposal), and accepted our bulk water
costs but with a minor change to volumes from the Sydney Desalination Plant ($2.4 billion). 77

We have carefully reviewed Sydney Water’s proposed operating costs using a base-trend-step
approach, as outlined in our Water Regulation Handbook. 78 In reaching our decisions, we
considered independent expert advice from AtkinsRéalis, and additional supporting
documentation provided by Sydney Water. AtkinsRéalis’ report on its assessment of Sydney
Water’s expenditure proposal is available on our website here, and its supplementary report on
the revised proposal is here.

Our assessment of Sydney Water’s operating expenditure balances the considerations set out in
sections 14A(2) and 15(1) of the IPART Act. This chapter examines the economic costs of Sydney
Water’s production and services, and assesses the efficiency in its supply of its services. We have
made decisions on items to increase efficiencies and an annual cost efficiency target that allows
Sydney Water’s ability to deliver high-quality, reliable and safe services is not compromised. Our
decisions on step changes factor in the costs to Sydney Water of complying with environmental
regulations to support ecologically sustainable development, and customer expectations on
service standards. We also compared Sydney Water’s proposed future costs to current and past
levels of expenditure to inform our decisions.


4.1      Sydney Water spent $31 million (0.4%) less over the last period
         than we had allowed for
In 2020 we set the operating expenditure for a 4-year period up to and including 2023–24. Due
to an extension to the determination period, we did not set an allowance for 2024–25. In the 4
years of the last determination period, Sydney Water spent 0.4% less operating expenditure than
the amount we used to set the prices. (See Table 4.1 further below)


Core operating expenditure

‘Core operating expenditure’ refers to Sydney Water’s operating expenditure excluding the bulk
water costs. This was about 72% of the total spend and is mostly controllable, with some non-
controllable expenditure such as licence fees a.

Sydney Water spent $4.9 billion on its core operating expenditure (excluding bulk water) over the
4-year period from 2020, an annual average of $1.2 billion. This is 1% less than the allowance of
$5.0 billion used to set the prices in 2020. The 2024–25 actual expenditure is expected to be
slightly higher than the 2023–24 spend. 79




a
    Uncontrollable costs to Sydney Water include licence fees and land taxes. While bulk water prices are beyond
    Sydney Water’s control, it has some ability to manage the volumes purchased. As these are significant costs, we
    consider them separately in this review.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                   Page | 42
                                                                                      Operating expenditure




Sydney Water’s operations were impacted in the first 2 years by COVID-19 restrictions, while in
the latter 2 years expenditure increased slightly. AtkinsRéalis found that Sydney Water was able
to meet our 0.8% pa efficiency target applied to the latter 3 years of the determination period. 80


Bulk water costs

Sydney Water’s bulk water costs made up about 28% of total operating expenditure. These costs
were 2% higher than we used to set prices in 2020. This was driven by the greater use of
desalinated water from the Sydney Desalination Plant (SDP), largely in response to poor water
quality from Warragamba Dam following periods of bushfire and several heavy rainfall events. 81

Total bulk water volumes were 7.6% lower than forecast in the 2020 determination period. 82
