4.2         Sydney Water proposed higher operating expenditure, partly
            driven by assumed bulk water cost increases
Sydney Water proposed $9.9 billion in operating expenditure over the next 5 years, a 20%
increase to its annual operating expenditure by 2030, compared to its 2023-24 spend. In its
submission to our Draft Report, it revised its proposal to $9.3 billion, with the bulk of the reduction
derived from the lower bulk water prices we used in our Draft Report. 83

Under the revised proposal, the annual average operating expenditure would be 10% greater
than actual spend in 2023-24, driven by:

•      a 12% increase in core operating expenditure compared to 2023-24, and

•      a 4% increase in bulk water costs driven primarily by an assumed increase in prices.

Sydney Water adopted IPART’s base-trend-step approach to forecast its operating expenditure
for the 2025 determination period. In its revised proposal, Sydney Water rejected some of our
draft decisions and sought:

•      the full base and trend costs per its original proposal

•      some of the step changes in full, including for water and wastewater maintenance,
       digitalisation and stormwater remediation, and it reduced the ‘step efficiencies’ it originally
       proposed by $63 million

•      the addition of pre-treatment plant and digital metering costs along with capital expenditure,
       or alternatively, some additional operating expenditure in the absence of those capital
       expenditures

•      additional costs that were not in its original proposal, including for mandatory contributions to
       the NSW Climate Change Fund and the operating costs of Hawkesbury City Council
       wastewater assets that it might acquire b

•      a small additional amount of bulk water purchases from the Sydney Desalination Plant to
       cover for operational outages. 84

Our decision is to set Sydney Water’s total operating expenditure at $9.4 billion in total, or an
average of around $1.9 billion per year over the next 5 years. This is:

•    $105 million (5%) lower per year than Sydney Water’s original proposal, and $10 million (0.5%)

•    $179 million (11%) higher per year than the allowance we used to set prices in 2020

•    $86 million (5%) higher per year than our draft decision.
On a per customer basis, this is $14 (2%) more per water and wastewater customer (annual
average) than we used to set prices in 2020.

This reflects our estimate of the efficient level of operating costs Sydney Water should incur in
providing its services over the determination period. However, it is not a budget or an amount that
Sydney Water is required to spend over the period. Forecasts, costs and unexpected events can
change how much Sydney Water needs to spend, and what the priorities of the business are.
Sydney Water should focus on continuing to provide value to customers.

Our key changes compared to the proposal are:
•    A $237 million or 3% lower core operating expenditure. This is largely based on AtkinsRéalis’
     recommendations and includes some new step change expenditure that Sydney Water
     included in its revised proposal.
•    $287 million or 10% lower bulk water costs, mostly caused by lower prices for water
     purchased from WaterNSW than Sydney Water used in its proposal. c

4.4         Core operating expenditure should be lower than proposed
Our decision is to set core operating expenditure at $6.7 billion over 5 years. Comparatively, our
draft decision was to set core operating expenditure at $6.5 billion, and Sydney Water’s revised
proposal was for $6.9 billion. 85

We used the base-trend-step approach to assess Sydney Water’s efficient operating
expenditure, as outlined in our Water Regulation Handbook. 86 We also engaged AtkinsRéalis to
inform our decisions regarding the efficient level of expenditure.

Core operating expenditure is all of Sydney Water’s operating expenditure except the bulk water
costs. This includes some controllable and some non-controllable expenditure such as licence fees.

We have set core operating expenditure higher than our draft decision. The key changes are to:

•      accept some additional growth costs, but reallocate some of these to the base and step
       components
•      accept some new costs that were not originally proposed for NSW Climate Change Fund
       contributions and Hawkesbury City Council wastewater asset operating costs.

•      increase some step change costs based on new and updated information, including adopting
       a different approach to the step efficiencies.


4.4.1       We have made minor changes to the proposed base expenditure

We have decided to set a base component of $1,199 million. This is based primarily on 2023-24
actual operating costs with -$7 million in adjustments made by Sydney Water. Our final decision
is around $5 million higher than our draft due to:

•      a $5 million adjustment to account for an electricity price increase in 2024-25, reflecting a
       recommendation by AtkinsRéalis, and

•      the reversal of a draft decision related to water conservation management costs ($1 million).


4.4.2 Our trend component is lower than Sydney Water’s revised proposal

Our final decision is for a 2.5% cumulative impact on the base allowance by 2030 as shown in
Table 4.3. Below that, Figure 4.2 shows a comparison of the cumulative effects of Sydney Water’s
proposal with our decision and AtkinsRéalis’ revised upper and lower bound recommendations.

Source: IPART calculations.


Our draft decision on trend increases was significantly lower than Sydney Water’s proposal.
Sydney Water’s revised proposal sought to re-instate the full proposed trend increases. It
considered that AtkinsRéalis had not adequately considered its bottom-up assessment of cost
increases that Sydney Water had developed to support the growth and real price effect
proposals.

The total trend allowance includes:

•    Growth factors of 0-1.2%, for a weighted average of 0.85% per annum

•    Real price effect factors of -0.2% to 0.5%, for a cumulative 1.6% increase
•    An offsetting 0.7% for ongoing efficiency gains.


Growth factor
Our decision is to apply growth factors of 0% to 1.2% annually across the 4 business components.

Sydney Water’s proposed growth factor was based on forecast growth in connections, averaging
1.4% per annum, which it applied to all costs. Our draft decision was to halve this based on
AtkinsRéalis recommendations and finding that growth drives some specific but not all operating
costs, and that water demand is relatively flat. 87 In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water
argued that the draft decision did not adequately consider the complexity, scale and growth
expected of the Sydney Water servicing area, and our decision to include a growth factor lower
than connections growth is out of alignment with regulatory precedent. 88




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                 Page | 47
                                                                                                  Operating expenditure




AtkinsRéalis updated its recommendations after closely reviewing Sydney Water’s bottom-up
analysis of cost changes. Its recommendations included to re-classify some of the ‘growth’
increases as a base or real price increase adjustments, and a step increase, and some
adjustments for costs that were not well justified or aligned with other evidence. It found 2
treatment plants were no longer expected for the upcoming period. It also recommended
applying growth rates more aligned to the service driving it, which included no growth-related
increase for corporate services. 89 This resulted in a weighted average growth rate of 0.16% per
annum at the lower bound and 0.86% per annum at the upper bound.

We accepted AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound recommendations except for stormwater, including its
reclassification of some costs as base and step increases.

We also disagree with Sydney Water’s assertion that the draft decision is out of alignment with
other regulators, for reasons set out in Box 4.1.



       Box 4.1 Other regulatory decisions on growth trend factors
       •    Our recent Hunter Water decision was based on a different approach to the
            review than undertaken for Sydney Water. While Hunter Water’s proposal was
            assessed as Advanced, Sydney Water’s proposal was assessed as Standard, as
            explained further in Chapter 2. In addition, Sydney Water was seeking larger
            expenditure increases.

       •    The Essential Services Commission notes in its guidance that “customer
            connections growth rate may not directly correlate with the forecast expenditure
            growth rate” and expects businesses to justify their proposed growth rate. 90 It
            generally accepted the proposed growth rates but some of these were based on
            a bottom-up growth costs or a reduced connection growth rate, rather than
            simply connections forecasts. d, 91. Melbourne Water’s proposed growth rate was
            not accepted. 92 Many Victorian water businesses proposed efficiency factors
            exceeding or close to their growth rates, leading to net efficiencies applied to
            their base expenditure. e, 93

       •    the Tasmanian Economic Regulator effectively halved the connections growth
            rate by applying it to 50% of operating expenditure only. Taswater’s proposal was
            derived bottom-up and Taswater considered that not all of its operating
            expenditure was impacted by growth. 94




d
    For instance, South-East Water found that controllable operating expenditure had grown at a lower rate (1.77% on
    average) than residential water customer numbers (1.90% on average) as had bulk water consumption (explained by
    COVID-19 and weather). It applied an ‘uncertainty discount’ of 0.22% on average each year on expected customer
    growth. Gippsland Water had econometric analysis undertaken which showed a negative relationship between
    growth and operating expenditure, however this finding was heavily qualified by issues with the underlying data. It
    instead proposed a bottom-up assessment of growth costs to add to its baseline. Yarra Valley Water also did a
    bottom-up assessment, reflecting the marginal cost of growth rather than the rate of growth. Goulburn Valley Water
    concluded that the growth rate is a “near enough” proxy for the year-on-year increase in operating expenditure.
e
    In their last determinations (occurring 2021-2024), South-East Water (metro), Melbourne Water (bulk water), Yarra
    Valley (metro), Greater Western Water (metro), Gippsland Water (urban), Wannon Water (urban) all proposed
    efficiency adjustments greater than growth rates.

       Box 4.1 Other regulatory decisions on growth trend factors
       •    The Australian Energy Regulator has a methodology that considers customers
            numbers as well as circuit length and ratcheted maximum demand to
            understand growth’s impact on operating expenditure.




Real price increase
Our trend allowance includes a component for expected real price increases, that is, price
increases higher than inflation. We decided to apply a real price increase of 1.6% cumulative,
higher than our draft decision of 1.2%.

Sydney Water’s proposal was for an effective increase of about 3.8% cumulative based on
expected changes in 8 indices reflecting its costs. f In its revised proposal it sought this be
reinstated in full. g Where AtkinsRéalis had found that the indices used applied to 71% of operating
costs, Sydney Water argued that there are no available indices for the remaining 29% of costs
(biosolid, build-own-operate and insurance costs), and it is fair to extrapolate its weighted
average to those cost items. It stated its experience is those cost increase by more than inflation.
It also sought labour costs be re-instated in full, noting it has a large proportion of staff on
enterprise bargaining agreements, and there are upcoming superannuation increases.

AtkinsRéalis’ final recommendations were slightly higher than the draft recommendations. It
maintained that the real price effect adjustment should apply to 71% of operating expenditure for
the upper bound, and 50% for the lower bound, but extended this to all components.

We have increased our draft decision to account for superannuation guarantees. We have
adopted the full proposed labour increase for 2024-25 and 2025-26 and maintained our draft
decision to halve it in the future years. While Sydney Water also argued that a high proportion of
its staff are on Enterprise Bargaining Agreements (EBA), it did not articulate how this relates to the
above CPI labour cost increases. We accept that the agreements are less dynamic than hiring
without one, but an efficient business would negotiate the EBA to provide for efficient costs in the
interests of customers. We have applied the remaining components to 71% of costs, in line with
AtkinsRéalis’ upper recommendation.




f
    This is a weighted average accounting for Sydney Water having applied only the labour component to its corporate
    costs, with a weighted average of other cost components to the remaining costs.
g
    The proposal was based on forecast increases of 8 cost components, with labour and contractors representing 84%
    and increasing higher than forecast inflation. The remaining components were expected to rise slower than or close to
    inflation.

Ongoing efficiency factor
We have maintained our draft decision to accept Sydney Water’s proposed annual efficiency
factor of 0.7% per annum.

The ongoing efficiency factor is to account for economy-wide efficiency gains that a
sophisticated and agile business should be able to embrace and incorporate. This applies in
addition to ‘step’ efficiencies proposed by Sydney Water or efficiencies that we have accepted
based on AtkinsRéalis’ review.

We have assessed the Australian multi-factor productivity since 1996 to understand the
economy-wide efficiency gains. We undertook our analysis across a group of 16 industries and
also tested 12 industries. This analysis showed an average multi-factor productivity between 0.7%.
and 0.9%.

Based on this, we consider Sydney Water’s proposal of 0.7% is reasonable while noting it is at the
lower end of the range.

In reaching this decision we considered that that this efficiency target meets the need for greater
efficiency in the supply of services to reduce costs for the benefit of consumers and taxpayers, as
required under section 15(1)(e) of the IPART Act.


4.4.3 We have not accepted all of Sydney Water’s step change costs

Sydney Water had proposed about $797 million in step increases with a $413 million ‘step
efficiency’. 95 Its revised proposal included 2 new step increases – for Climate Change Fund
contributions, and operating costs associated with Hawkesbury City Council wastewater assets. 96

Our final decision is for step increases of $316 million – a $666 million increase with a
$339 million efficiency adjustment, higher than our draft decision.

Sydney Water accepted most of our draft decisions on step changes. AtkinsRéalis reviewed
those that Sydney Water disputed as well as its approach to the ‘step efficiency’ adjustments.
Table 4.5 shows our final decisions on those step changes.

Summary of key adjustments
Compared to our Draft Report, our final decision includes higher costs for:

•      Sydney Water’s two new step increases that were not in the original submission.

•      Pre-treatment plant operating expenditure, as we have decided to include capital
       expenditure for 3 pre-treatment plants. Our decision includes AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound
       recommendation for these 3 plants, plus $14 million due to a delay in expected operating
       time, which was part of Sydney Water’s revised proposal.
•      Digital metering operating expenditure, as we have decided to include capital expenditure
       for the project. We accepted AtkinsRéalis’ lower bound which includes some further
       expected efficiencies compared to the upper bound.
•      Water and wastewater maintenance, and digitalisation costs, based on AtkinsRéalis’
       revised upper bound recommendations. This approach is in line with our draft decisions to
       accept the original upper bound recommendations.

       — For maintenance expenditure, the revised recommendations take into account new
         information about performance deterioration, and for wastewater, acknowledges that
         there would be greater maintenance pressures in response to reduced renewals
         expenditure. 97
       — For digitalisation, the increase reflects new market information provided by Sydney
         Water. Sydney Water suggests actual costs may exceed its proposal, but it was willing to
         accept the difference. 98




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                     Page | 51
                                                                                  Operating expenditure




•     Stormwater remediation, we have adjusted our decision be more in line with historical
      expenditure and include AtkinsRéalis’ recommendation for increased dam costs. 99
•     Increased water treatment (BOO) costs related to the shift in bulk water sourced from the
      Sydney Desalination Plant to WaterNSW.

We have also accepted AtkinsRéalis’ lower bound recommendation for a new step change for
the Upper South Creek treatment plant. During the review of growth costs this was identified as
a cost better classified as a step increase. 100

We also amended our approach to the ‘step’ efficiencies. Our draft decision had been to adopt
AtkinsRéalis’ approach to pro-rate the step efficiencies based on our adjustments to step
changes. However, it is apparent that the proposed efficiencies would be derived from
adjustments to the entire operating expenditure not just the relatively small portion of step
changes. We asked AtkinsRéalis to review its initial approach. It noted that the efficiencies were
derived from a combination of approved programs and some top-down/bottom-up
consolidations and that applying the full amount may double-count some other adjustments it
made. 101

While Sydney Water’s initial proposal was for a $413 million ‘step efficiency’, it revised this based
on having applied the pro-rata approach but with no further reason for the reduction. 102 We
decided to accept Sydney Water’s revised efficiency proposal, but we also accept that the item-
specific changes we made in other areas may result in double counting. We pro-rated the revised
proposal by the difference between our decision on core operating expenditure before efficiency
adjustment compared to the revised proposal. Sydney Water states that it is committed to
improve efficiency and challenge itself to drive better customer value. 103


4.4.4 Corporate cost allocation

Corporate costs are allocated to the three different services (water, wastewater and stormwater)
to be recovered through prices. We have maintained our draft decision to allocate corporate
costs proportional to the total operating expenditure of those services, however, we excluded
bulk water costs from the water service.


4.5      Our changes to bulk water volumes purchased and total costs
Bulk water costs made up around 30% of the total proposed operating expenditure.
Comparatively, over 2020–21 to 2023–24, it made up 28% of actual operating expenditure. The
main difference was an assumed increase in the price of bulk water from WaterNSW over the
next 5 years.

Our final decision includes:
•     minor changes to overall demand and Sydney Desalination Plant purchase volumes, and

•     the final prices that we have determined in our concurrent review of WaterNSW, which are
      higher than we used in our draft decisions.

AtkinsRéalis did not review total water demand from customers in detail, but it considered the
efficiency of Sydney Water’s proposed bulk water purchase volumes and where savings could
be made, including the efficient use of bulk water from different sources. It made 3
recommendations relating to the proposed purchase volumes.

The prices Sydney Water pays are determined by IPART and are not within Sydney Water’s
control.

Total bulk water volume is 1.4% lower than proposed, based on 3 adjustments.


Price elasticity of demand adjustments

As discussed further in Chapter 8, we have made a decision for lower water sales than Sydney
Water proposed and were used in our Draft Report. This is because we applied a price-elasticity
of demand to a higher water usage price.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                    Page | 53
                                                                                     Operating expenditure




Savings from digital metering
AtkinsRéalis had recommended 17.8 GL of expected savings from Sydney Water’s proposed
expansion of digital metering to be reflected in the expected demand from WaterNSW. 104 We had
not accepted this in our Draft Report consistent with our decision to adopt AtkinsRéalis’ lower
range for capital expenditure on major asset renewals. Our final capital expenditure decision
includes additional renewals funding, and we understand that Sydney Water intends to pursue
the digital metering roll-out. Accordingly, we have incorporated this bulk water recommendation.


Volume of water sourced from the Sydney Desalination Plant (SDP)

Bulk water is purchased primarily from WaterNSW, with some additional supply from SDP. SDP is
a more expensive source than the dams that WaterNSW manages, so SDP supply should be
used only as needed.

In 2022 the SDP was moved to a new ‘always on’ operating model. The Greater Sydney Operating
Strategy rules mandate a minimum purchase of 50 ML/day by Sydney Water and depending on
dam levels, this may increase to around 125ML/day or 250ML/day. The proposed purchase
volumes from SDP were based on probabilistic modelling for the percentage of time the dams
will be at different storage levels. This combined with the operating rules forms the basis of SDP
volumes. AtkinsRéalis found this to be appropriate and we agree. 105

Sydney Water can also request additional water for ‘emergency events’ such as poor raw water
quality and to support operational purposes such as maintenance. It did this on 17 occasions in
the last determination period. 106

Sydney Water’s proposal had included an additional 7 GL per year or 8% of full production over a
year to support operational events. We found Sydney Water’s proposal to be risk averse and that
a more appropriate allowance, having regard to our statutory factors, would be 2 GL per annum.

In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water requested that 15.7GL of desalinated water be
included to support planned critical maintenance, accepting an off-setting amount from the
water to be sourced from WaterNSW. This would occur in the first 3 years. 107 We have accepted
this proposal.

Future changes to bulk water prices will directly impact Sydney Water’s costs. We have decided

the final year of the Sydney Water Determination. The SDP is also due to have a new
determination in place from 2027–28. To balance out any difference in prices, it will be open to a
future Tribunal to consider a true-up of revenue when making its next determination. This is
discussed further in Chapter 7.


4.6        Sydney Water’s expenditure for the deferral year
In November 2021, we approved the extension of Sydney Water’s current determination period
by one year, to 2024–25. This meant that prices remained constant at 2023–24 levels, and no
operating expenditure allowance was set for 2024–25. IPART agreed to true-up the efficient
costs of the deferral year that Sydney Water did not recover through prices and consider adding
this to prices in the next determination period (explained further in section 6.7.3).

We have assessed Sydney Water’s operating expenditure for 2024–25 for efficiency. In response
to our Draft Report, Sydney Water sought more information about our draft decision, having
expected us to apply a bottom-up approach to the efficiency assessment, rather than the top-
down base-trend-step approach. 108 We applied the base-trend-step approach for this
assessment as an approach to assess an efficient level of expenditure.

Sydney Water’s forecast operating expenditure was $1.7 billion for 2024–25 (i.e., to June 2025),
consisting of $1.28 billion for core operating costs and $470 million for bulk water costs.

AtkinsRéalis assessed Sydney Water’s operating expenditure over 2024–25 in the base-trend-
step analysis and concluded that efficient core expenditure was in the range of $1.3 billion for the
upper bound and $1.2 billion for the lower bound. The differences primarily are for changes to the
base and trend components. It did not recommend changes for bulk water costs. 109




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 56
                                                                                      Operating expenditure




Our draft decision was that the efficient level of expenditure was $1.25 billion. This was used to
calculate a draft true-up. Our final decision is that the efficient level of expenditure is $1.26 billion.
This is the result of some changes to the base and trend components as discussed earlier in this
chapter, adding the Climate Change Fund contribution, and correcting for some errors.


Usual treatment of the final determination year
Sydney Water also sought greater clarity on whether the final year of the 2025-2030 price period
will be assessed, noting that with the penultimate year assessed for the base year, some material
expenditures in the final year can be lost. 110

We understand Sydney Water’s premise. However, we do not typically make ex-post changes to
operating expenditure in the final year. The treatment of the 2024-25 year as a ‘deferral year’ was
an anomaly. At this stage, without further deferrals, it would be open to a future Tribunal to review,
in the next pricing review, the expenditure in the final year of this determination, against efficiency,
output measures and the applicable statutory criteria.
If, in preparing the next pricing proposal, Sydney Water encounters new expenditures in the final
year that do not occur in the penultimate year but it expects to apply in the future determination
period, the business can explain these in its proposal as adjustments to the base year, trend or
step changes compared to the penultimate year, as appropriate.

   Summary of our decisions on capital expenditure


   The efficient level of capital expenditure from 2025–26 to 2029–30 is $13.2 billion
   We have increased the amount of forecast capital expenditure to $13.2 billion to be
   included into Sydney Water’s notional revenue requirement build up for the 5 years to
   2029-30. This is an increase of $2.5 billion from our Draft Report.

   Our allowance for capital expenditure is $1.2 million per annum higher than the allowance
   included in current water prices. We consider this expenditure is necessary and efficient so
   that Sydney Water can continue to provide safe and reliable water services over the next 5
   years.

   We heard from many stakeholders including NSW Health, local councils and industry
   organisations that they supported increased capital expenditure to address aging
   infrastructure, to service population growth, for safe drinking water and improved
   environmental outcomes. 111

   IPART does not approve individual projects or expenditure. IPART’s decisions provide
   Sydney Water an overall efficient envelope of revenue to operate its business over the next
   5 years. Over the next 5 years inevitably costs, demand and service requirements will
   change as the needs of Sydney Water’s customers change. Unexpected events may occur.
   This will require Sydney Water to reprioritise to drive efficiencies and innovate to deliver its
   customer services and outcomes at the lowest sustainable cost, in a manner that promotes
   long-term customer value.

   This large increase in capital expenditure will facilitate Sydney Water providing safe
   reliable drinking water, adapting to meet the NSW Government’s policies and regulatory
   obligations including servicing population growth, and protecting our waterways and
   environment.

   The Tribunal may consider and adjust for any additional efficient expenditure in the next
   review of Sydney Water’s prices.


   Sydney Water’s actual capital expenditure over the 2020 determination period
   is efficient
   We reviewed Sydney Water’s historical capital expenditure over the 2020 determination
   period to determine the efficient level of expenditure to include in the RAB roll-forward.

   Our view is that Sydney Water’s actual capital expenditure of $7.2 billion from 2019–20 to
   2023–24 was efficient and variances between allowed and actual expenditure were
   justified.

   We have set 2024–25 expenditure at $2.6 billion based on Sydney Water’s actual 2024–25
   expenditure with revisions based on the independent expert advice from AtkinsRéalis,
   specifically, the lower range recommendation for renewals expenditure and upper range
   for compliance expenditure.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                  Page | 59
                                                                                                      Capital expenditure




This chapter sets out our assessment of Sydney Water’s capital expenditure required to deliver
good quality services and promote long-term customer outcomes. Sydney Water’s capital costs
are the investments it makes to buy, build and renew the infrastructure and equipment it uses to
provide its services (e.g. water mains and pipelines, wastewater treatment plants, IT systems, etc.).

We have carefully reviewed Sydney Water’s proposed capital costs in light of its long-term
investment plan, a the impacts of climate change on its assets and planning, growth in the Greater
Sydney region including supporting the delivery of NSW Government’s housing targets, and the
need to address customer priorities and outcomes and deliver value for money.

In reaching our decisions, we considered independent expert advice from AtkinsRéalis 112,
additional supporting documentation provided by Sydney Water 113 and comments from
stakeholder consultation which is on our website. In providing their advice to IPART, AtkinsRéalis
reviewed a sample of projects and forecast expenditure. This review assessed Sydney Water’s
approach to long term planning, optioneering and adaptive planning, asset management
practices and processes, risk management, procurement and deliverability. AtkinsRéalis’ reports
on its assessment of Sydney Water’s expenditure are available on our website 114.

Our assessment of Sydney Water’s capital expenditure carefully considers the requirements set
out in sections 14A(2) and 15(1) of the IPART Act. In this chapter, we assess the costs of Sydney
Water’s production and services and the efficiency of its historical and proposed capital
investments. Our assessment also compares Sydney Water’s proposed future costs to current
and past levels of expenditure to inform our decisions. It focuses closely on cost efficiency and
considers how Sydney Water’s proposed capital investments would deliver upon its standards of
quality, reliability and safety. These include capital investments aimed at meeting environmental
regulations. The social impacts of major capital investments, including the servicing population
growth expenditure, are also considered in our assessment of Sydney Water’s proposed capital
costs.


5.1      Sydney Water’s spending over the last 5 years

Our decision is:

              5. To set the efficient capital expenditure of $9.8 billion over 2019–20 to 2024–25, as
                 shown in Table 5.1.


Our decisions on capital expenditure reflect our assessment of the prudent and efficient level of
expenditure on capital works that should be included in a business’s regulatory asset base to be
recovered through prices. When we assess historical capital expenditure, we look at spend over
the current determination period (2020–24), as well as spend over the final year of the 2016
determination period (i.e. 2019–20). b




a

b

    for the 2020 determination period we would not have had a complete year of actual expenditure data from 2019-20
    to assess its efficiency.

Sydney Water provided a detailed explanation for the variance in its allowed and actual
expenditure, including:

•      the circumstances in place at the time of its 2019 submission, including changing climatic,
       economic and social circumstances
•      changes in conditions between its 2019 submission and the Final Determination, including the
       drought breaking, followed by extended and extreme periods of wet weather, plus the
       COVID-19 pandemic

•      new delivery arrangements in place that enabled a higher level of capital delivery. 115

Sydney Water also provided a detailed assessment of variances at a project and program level to
explain the differences in allowed and actual expenditure. 116

Based on the detailed explanation of variances and the variance amount, we propose to include
all of Sydney Water’s actual capital expenditure over the 2020 determination period into the RAB.


5.2         Sydney Water proposed higher capital expenditure to address
            customer growth and renew ageing assets
Sydney Water proposed capital expenditure of around $3.3 billion per year (or $16.6 billion in
total) over the 2025 determination period and $2.7 million for the 2024–25 deferral year. 117 As
shown in Figure 5.1, this is:

•      $1.9 billion (137%) per year higher than the average forecast expenditure used to set
       prices in 2020
•      $1.7 billion (101%) per year higher than the average of Sydney Water’s reported actual
       expenditure per year over the 2020 determination period.

In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water submitted a revised draft capital expenditure
program requesting an additional $3.0 billion per year (or $14.8 billion in total) over the 2025
determination period. 118 This is approximately $1.8 billion lower than its original proposal but
$4.1 billion higher than our draft decision.

Sydney Water accepted some of our draft capital expenditure decisions but proposed higher
expenditure allowances for the following items: 119

•     the full amount requested in its original pricing proposal for growth servicing arguing that it
      places at risk of nearly 75,000 new homes across Sydney
•     a partial amount requested in its original pricing proposal for the Resilient and Reliable Water
      Supply program to continue to progress design and planning to accommodate an expanded
      Sydney Desalination Plant
•     a partial amount requested in its original pricing proposal for the Pretreatment Program for
      projects that are contractually committed and in delivery

•     a partial amount requested in its original pricing proposal for critical sewers renewal in line

•     the full amount requested in its original pricing proposal for digital meters on the basis that it
      offers better value for money than like-for-like meter replacements

•     maintain the relative portfolio adjustment at 8% for the infrastructure capital portfolio

We engaged independent experts AtkinsRéalis to undertake a supplementary review on Sydney
Water’s revised capital expenditure program and additional information provided by Sydney Water. 120


5.3       We have set capital expenditure to be $13.2 billion over the 2025
          determination period

Our decision is:

              6. To include $13.2 billion of capital expenditure into Sydney Water’s notional revenue
                 requirement for the 2025 determination period, as shown in Table 5.2.


The capital expenditure allowance we set for Sydney Water represents our view on the overall
envelope of capital expenditure that we consider reasonable to maintain or improve Sydney
Water’s assets and services over the upcoming determination period, and that should be
recovered through prices. It does not signal the amount Sydney Water is required to spend on
specific capital projects, or discrete allowances for specific works, projects or programs. We
expect Sydney Water to prioritise its planned prudent and efficient capital works within the
envelope of capital expenditure that we consider reasonable to recover through customer prices.
This means that Sydney Water can be dynamic in its spending and make investment and
business decisions that are in customers’ interests.

determination period (i.e. around $2.6 billion per year). Our decision on efficient capital
expenditure for the 2025 determination period is set out in Table 5.2 below, and is:

•      $1.2 billion (89%) higher per year than the allowance we used to set prices in 2020

•      $1.0 billion (60%) higher per year than Sydney Water’s actual capital expenditure over the
       2020 determination period

•      $0.3 billion (11%) lower per year than Sydney Water’s revised proposal (see Figure 5.1).

This large increase in capital expenditures will facilitate Sydney Water providing safe clean
reliable drinking water, adapting to meet the NSW Governments policies and regulatory
obligations including servicing population growth, and protecting our waterways and
environment.

In the following sections, we step through our analysis and explain how we reached this decision
for the key capital cost drivers and changes from our Draft Report.


Growth capital expenditure

Sydney Water proposed growth expenditure of $10.1 billion over the 2025 determination period,
representing $2 billion per annum which is near trebling of the current level of expenditure. The 2
largest projects relate to the Aerotropolis/Mamre Road Stormwater ($1.4 billion) and Resilient and
Reliable Water Supply (RRWS) ($1.3 billion). 121

In our Draft Report, we proposed growth expenditure of $1.3 billion each year over the 2025
review period, which is an increase of $0.9 billion each year over the current allowance for
growth but less than Sydney Water’s proposal.

In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water requested additional funding for RRWS and the
original proposed amount for other growth expenditure. 122

Having considered all submissions and undertaken further analysis our decision is to increase the
growth capital expenditure by $1.3 billion compared to our draft decision. This will provide
Sydney Water with the flexibility to service population growth and meet government policy
requirements including servicing the Sydney Housing Supply Forecast 2025 and to deliver
planning and design works to accommodate any future capacity of Sydney Desalination Plant.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                 Page | 64
                                                                                   Capital expenditure




Resilient and Reliable Water Supply
Given current uncertainty about the timing of any expansion of the Sydney Desalination Plant
(SDP), our draft decision was to defer Sydney Water’s costs associated with its network
expansion. The network expansion is not essential to enable the expansion of SDP but
accompanies it to enable Sydney Water to service additional demand and address drought risks
when the expanded SDP comes online.
If the SDP expansion does occur within the 2025 determination period and Sydney Water does
incur costs associated with the expansion, then the efficient costs would be considered as part of
the 2030 price review to be included into the RAB.
Sydney Water partially accepted our draft decision, and explained that deferral of some projects
to only commence when drought occurs increases risk. Sydney Water requested $58 million of
the original $828 million to complete all necessary planning and design to allow shovel-
readiness for both contractual and drought readiness. 123 This work was also supported by the
Sydney Desalination Plant in its submission to our Draft Report. 124

Having further considered this we now agree to including additional expenditures for planning
and design works to accommodate an any future capacity of Sydney Desalination Plant.

Co-regulators in the Regulators Advisory Panel (RAP) acknowledged that purified recycled water
may have broader benefits for both the environment and supply reliability.

We further considered the proposed funding of purified recycled water projects (PRW). We note
the potential benefits of PRW, but we consider that more work is required to build community
acceptance for drinking purified recycled water. If the need to develop PRW projects becomes
urgent, the funding envelope we have allowed for Sydney Water should provide it with sufficient
flexibility to prioritise the projects. Should that occur, this will be considered at the next
determination or it would be open to Sydney Water to ask IPART to review the determination
early and replace the determination with a new one.


Other growth expenditure
In response to our Draft Report Sydney Water and multiple stakeholder submissions argued that
our draft decision would impact housing supply and that basing growth expenditure on the
Sydney Housing Supply Forecast (SHSF) 2023, would not provide adequate funding for serving
population growth. 125 Some of these submissions also noted that the SHSF 2023 does not include
non-residential development or residential development in Illawarra. 126 Sydney Water notes that if
IPART were to accept the SHSF as the best forecast, then it should be based on more up-to-date
version of the SHSF with adjustments for growth in the Illawarra region, non-residential
development, and an allowance for planning work. 127

We note that the SHSF is a point-in-time forecast and may not include all current government
housing policies. 128 The SHSF is updated annually to reflect new planning reforms over the
previous 12 months. However, we also agree with AtkinsRéalis that Sydney Water’s UGI forecasts
may not be appropriate for decision making as they are based on developer information which
may be “optimistic about the scale and pace of development”. 129




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 65
                                                                                    Capital expenditure




After releasing our Draft Report, the Secretary of the NSW Department of Planning Housing and
Infrastructure wrote to IPART providing an update on the 2025 SHSF. She advised that the
updated 2025 SHSF for Greater Sydney is 161,687 dwellings is expected to be completed by
June 2029. 130 This represents an increase of 23,156 net dwellings, compared to the 2023 housing
forecast. The letter also referred to the various reforms that have recently been implemented by
the NSW Government to increase housing supply under the National Housing Accord which
targets the delivery of 263,000 new homes in Greater Sydney by June 2029.

The maximum price we have determined will facilitate Sydney Water being able to build the
infrastructure needed to support the 2025 SHSF. The higher target in the National Housing Accord
includes additional housing in transport orientated development and small and medium rise
developments which may use existing infrastructure. If Sydney Water needs to invest more than we
anticipate, under IPART’s water regulation approach, it would be open to the Tribunal 2030 to
include all efficient capital expenditure in its asset base when the Tribunal sets prices in 2030.

While we have used relevant current information in making our decisions, costs, demand and
service requirements may change as the needs of Sydney Water’s customers change. Sydney
Water’s licensed service standards may shift. Unforeseen events may occur. As Sydney Water’s
operating environment changes, it will need to reprioritise to drive efficiencies, and innovate to
deliver its customer services and outcomes at the lowest sustainable cost, in a manner that
promotes long-term customer value.

If the efficient costs and sales volumes change materially from those we have used to set prices
in this determination, we may consider reviewing and setting new prices before 2029-30.

AtkinsRéalis also reviewed the issues raised in submissions to our Draft Report including whether
Sydney Water requires more capital expenditure to service population growth. AtkinsRéalis
considered the additional information on the NSW housing accord. 131 We note that AtkinsRéalis
amended its recommendations for growth expenditure upper and lower bounds to reflect the
increases 132.

Our independent expert AtkinsRéalis’ advice on growth expenditure is summarised below.




      Box 5.1 Summary of AtkinsRéalis’ review of growth expenditure
      AtkinsRéalis undertook a top-down assessment of need and cost to determine a
      reasonable estimate of the cost required for future growth investment and found that
      costs could be 12% lower than Sydney Water’s proposal. We consider its approach
      and assumptions are reasonable as they are based on the ‘run rate’ of expenditure
      from better developed projects in later stages of planning and delivery, and its
      assessment of historical and forecast growth capex and new connections.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 66
                                                                                      Capital expenditure




      Box 5.1 Summary of AtkinsRéalis’ review of growth expenditure
      We note that AtkinsRéalis also undertook a bottom-up assessment of some strategic
      schemes which supported its top-down assessment and finding for the upper bound.
      Specifically, the bottom-up assessment identified that costs could be reduced by 9%
      compared to Sydney Water’s proposal. The bottom-up approach identified: 133

      •   potential scope deferrals and cost efficiencies for costs that are not well justified
      •   potential scope deferral if growth is aligned with the Sydney Housing Supply
          Forecast (SHSF) as opposed to Sydney Water’s ‘high confidence’ Urban Growth
          Intelligence (UGI) forecasts
      •   other scope deferrals to reflect a lower growth rate or high-risk approach to the
          management of growth, and

      •   additional delivery cost that could be lower, above and beyond those identified
          by Sydney Water.

      In its Supplementary Report, AtkinsRéalis considered the updated information on
      housing supply projections for dwelling completions and amended both its upper
      and lower bound to account for additional forecast aggregate total housing supply.
      The upper bound was also adjusted to include an estimate for non-residential
      demand, but excluded from the lower bound due to uncertainty associated with the
      amount of non-residential demand that might be reflected in the SHSF2025. 134




Renewals expenditure

Renewals expenditure represents Sydney Water’s second biggest driver for its proposed capital
expenditure. Sydney Water has proposed $5.5 billion over the 2025 determination period which
represents a 93% increase in real terms. Renewals expenditure is primarily comprised of major
asset renewals, property and digital capital expenditure.

Based on AtkinsRéalis’ independent expert advice, our draft decision was to set the efficient level
of renewals expenditure at $3.7 billion over the 2025 review period. This is 33% ($1.8 billion) lower
than Sydney Water’s proposal.

In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water partially accepted our decisions and its revised
proposal included the full amount of its pricing proposal for digital smart meters, and additional
expenditure for the Pretreatment Program, critical sewers and other asset renewals. 135

Our decision is to increase the capital expenditure by $617 million for renewals expenditure
compared to our draft decision. This will provide Sydney Water with the necessary efficient
expenditures for renewals including its digital smart meters program, efficient assets renewals,
pretreatment and critical sewers. In making our final decision on renewals expenditure we
considered submissions, advice from AtkinsRéalis’ and our legislative obligations.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 67
                                                                                       Capital expenditure




Prospect Pre-treatment
The Pretreatment Program is one of the largest programs of works in Sydney Water’s proposed
capital program, with more than $1 billion proposed over the next 7 years. The program of works
is across 8 water filtration plants and is to bring in an additional stage of treatment to remove
more contaminants from raw water. The Prospect Pretreatment project is the single plant with the
highest proposed cost ($697 million over the 2025 determination period). 136 The drivers for the
project identified by Sydney Water include:

•   increased risk of flooding at Prospect water filtration plant due to changes in the Australian
    Drinking Water Guidelines, with more stringent requirements for turbidity

•   expected population growth
•   increased frequency of poor raw water quality events that are impacting capability, quality
    and supply. 137

AtkinsRéalis reviewed the project in detail and found that the Pretreatment Program does not fall
into the ‘very well justified, clearly has to happen now’ category. Specifically, it notes that Sydney
Water survived historical adverse events without boil water notices, and that the assumptions in
the cost-benefit analysis lean to favouring the pretreatment plant. Nevertheless, AtkinsRéalis
acknowledges the potential benefits of the project for future adverse water quality events, and if
climate change makes these events more likely, then it could be more useful in the longer run
than historical precedent would suggest. 138

Our draft decision did not include the majority of the Pretreatment Program, except for the
ongoing Nepean water filtration plant program. We agreed that carrying out the Pretreatment
Program projects in stages would be beneficial and would allow lessons learned to be applied
throughout the delivery of the program. 139

We also sought feedback from customers and stakeholders on our draft decision, including
whether customers are comfortable with the trade-off in costs and benefits or if customers would
prefer to pay higher water prices that will facilitate higher water quality in exceptional or unusual
events.

Sydney Water’s submission to our Draft Report included a lower proposal for the Pretreatment
Program, comprising projects that are currently contractually committed and in delivery. This
includes Nepean and Prospect which are now in the construction phase and Cascade, to allow it
to continue to comply with Australian Drinking Water Guidelines. 140

In response to our Draft Report stakeholders supported increasing expenditure on pretreatment
over the next 5 years. 141 NSW Health noted the importance of pre-treatment at Cascade, Nepean,
Orchard Hills and Prospect to facilitate effective control of raw water quality. 142 We agree that it is
in the public interest to reduce water quality deterioration and to improve resilience and reliability
of the system.

Our decision is to accept Sydney Water’s revised expenditure for the Pretreatment Program.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 68
                                                                                  Capital expenditure




Digital smart meters
As part of our broader decision for renewals expenditure, we accepted AtkinsRéalis’ lower bound
for digital smart meters. The lower bound excluded the smart meters program which was
identified as not essential and could be deferred to the next period. The lower bound
represented an estimate of like-for-like renewals.

In response to our draft decision, Sydney Water argued that smart meters offer better value for
money than like-for-like meter replacements and is in line with the same level of customer
service of comparable cities. It notes that the program complements its water efficient programs
by enabling faster and more accurate identification of leakages, and reduced downstream costs
such as remedying reading errors and improving network management. 143

Our decision is to accept Sydney Water’s proposal and include the full amount for the digital
smart meter program. Based on the information presented by Sydney Water and AtkinsRéalis’
findings and recommendations, 144 we agree that there are minimal benefits to deferring the
program to 2030 when it would otherwise be rolled out, and the program will be cost-beneficial
for customers over the medium term.


Critical sewers
AtkinsRéalis’ expenditure review noted that the critical sewers work program is prudent and likely
to continue over many years. 145 However, due to concerns around deliverability challenges in
deep ocean outfall sewers, our draft decision was to accept AtkinsRéalis’ lower bound which was
based on historical average spend over the last period.

In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water partially accepted our decision and our concerns
around deliverability but advised that previous delays in 2020-24 were driven by extraordinary,
one-off events (such as the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, multiple 1-in-100 year flood
events, and complexities in establishing the new Regional Delivery Partner contract) which have
passed or been resolved and that it has addressed any on-going deliverability concerns. It
highlights that it is on track to deliver over $150 million in works across the program, and
proposed a revised expenditure for the program based on this level of spend, plus an annual
increase of 5%. 146

We also considered advice from co water regulators (NSW Health and the EPA) at recent Regulars
Advisory Panel meetings which highlighted support for prioritising expenditure on critical “avoid-
fail” assets, such as the ocean outfall sewers to protect public health and the environment.

In AtkinsRéalis’ supplementary review, AtkinsRéalis maintained its concerns around the
challenges in working in deep sewers, and the effects of adverse weather and other unforeseen
events. It revised its upper and lower bounds to reflect the new information provided by Sydney
Water. The upper bound is based on Sydney Water’s revised proposal. It also revised up its lower
bound based on the assumption that 50% of the proposed major outfall sewer works in the
program are completed in the 2025 period. 147




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                              Page | 69
                                                                                    Capital expenditure




We acknowledge AtkinsRéalis’ concerns around deliverability challenges with deep ocean
outfalls which can be impacted by adverse and unforeseen events. Our decision is to include an
additional $180.2 million for the major outfall sewers program. It will be open to the Tribunal to
consider and potentially adjust for any additional efficient expenditure in the next review of
Sydney Water’s prices.


Other asset renewals
In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water refuted the characterisation that its approach
to asset renewal aims to significantly reduce risk and raised concerns about the linkage between
renewals and maintenance spend, and AtkinsRéalis’ expenditure review approach. 148 It put
together a revised higher-risk program that is nearly $500 million less than its original proposal,
but higher than our draft decision, which includes the following: 149
•   increasing the proposed amount for wastewater pumping stations and water reservoirs
•   accepting our draft decision on property, water network and wastewater network renewals
•   a revised expenditure amount for water filtration plans, wastewater resource recovery
    facilities and stormwater.

AtkinsRéalis supplementary review considered renewals of wastewater pumping stations, water
reservoirs, water filtration plants, wastewater resource recovery facilities and stormwater assets.
Based on the information presented by Sydney Water, it maintained its upper and lower bound
for water reservoir, water filtration plant and wastewater resource recovery facility renewals
largely as it was not convinced that additional expenditure is required over the next 5 years. 150

AtkinsRéalis did, however, revise its lower bound for wastewater pumping station based on a
longer time series to inform its recommendation for the lower bound, and stormwater renewals
to account for the effects of COVID-19 and weather on delivery over the last determination
period. This resulted in higher lower bound estimates for both wastewater pumping stations and
stormwater renewals. 151

We have considered all submissions, AtkinsRéalis’ supplementary review and undertaken further
analysis. Our decision is to adopt AtkinsRéalis’ revised lower bound for asset renewals (aside from
the Pretreatment Program, critical sewers and digital smart meters as discussed above). We
consider the lower bound to be more reflective of the efficient costs to deliver service outcomes,
including reliable and safe water to customers.


Compliance expenditure

Our draft decision for compliance expenditure was based on accepting AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound
for wet weather overflow, which reflects Sydney Water’s revised lower costs and will enable
Sydney Water to meet the current requirements of its Environment Protection Licence. 152

Sydney Water accepted our draft decision but commented that AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound
underestimated expenditure. 153 AtkinsRéalis’ Supplementary Report amended its upper and lower
bound to reflect the clarification in cost estimates. 154

Our decision is to maintain our draft decision to accept AtkinsRéalis’ upper bound which reflects
an increase in compliance expenditure of $589 million compared to our draft decision, consistent
with the advice from AtkinsRéalis’ Supplementary Report. 155

   Summary of our decisions on revenue requirement and adjustments


   Set Sydney Water’s notional revenue requirement at $18.7 billion over the 2025
   determination period

   This is $1.7 billion (or 8.5%) lower than Sydney Water’s proposal. This difference reflects our
   decisions on:

   •   efficient operating expenditure, which is $524.1 million (or 5.3%) lower than Sydney
       Water proposed

   •   tax allowance, which is $52.1 million or 27.5% lower than Sydney Water proposed

   •   return on assets, which is 14.0% lower than Sydney Water proposed because of a:

       — lower WACC of 3.3% compared to Sydney Water’s proposed 3.6%, and
       — lower capital expenditure allowance of $13.2 billion compared to Sydney Water’s
         proposed $16.6 billion.

   The revenue decision also includes revenue adjustments for demand volatility, true-up of
   debt costs and the additional efficient costs Sydney Water incurred in 2024–25.


   Exclude Sydney Water’s proposed RAB adjustments for historical Rouse Hill
   developer charges and Blue Mountains Tunnel lease payments
   Sydney Water proposed increasing the value of its regulatory asset base (or ‘RAB’ on which
   it earns a return) by:

   •   $485 million for past costs it suggests have been excluded in delivering services in
       Rouse Hill
   •   $140 million for the Blue Mountains Tunnel (BMT) finance lease, which it argues we
       previously undervalued.

   We do not agree. We consider the previous efficient costs associated with the BMT have
   been included in the maximum wastewater prices we set in previous determinations. We
   also found that Sydney Water did not clearly demonstrate that the Rouse Hill claim reflects
   an error rather than a past regulatory decision, nor did it justify the size of the adjustment or
   ruled out financial benefits it may have received from its treatment in the interim. Sydney
   Water accepted our draft decision in its revised proposal and so we have maintained it.


Consistent with our requirement under section 15(1) of the IPART Act to have regard to the cost of
providing Sydney Water’s services and the need to ensure an appropriate rate of return on public
sector assets, we continue to use the building block approach to calculate the notional revenue
requirement, as outlined in the Water Regulation Handbook. 156

6.2        Rolling forward the regulatory asset base (RAB)
The regulatory asset base (RAB) represents the value of Sydney Water’s assets on which it should
earn a return on capital and an allowance for depreciation. We calculated the opening RAB for the
2025 determination period by ‘rolling the RAB forward’ from the previous determination period.

To do this we:

•     added $9.0 billion of historical capital expenditure from the 2020 determination period, as
      discussed in Chapter 5

•     deducted $11.3 million in asset disposals

•     deducted $3.0 billion for regulatory depreciation of assets
•     added $4.8 billion to account for annual indexation.

To calculate the RAB for each year of the 2025 determination period we then:




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 76
                                                                               Other costs and notional revenue




•      added $10.7 billion of forecast capital expenditure, which is based on the efficient capital
       expenditure allowance set out in Chapter 5, net of cash contributions
•      deducted $55.1 million in asset disposals

•      deducted $3.2 billion for regulatory depreciation of assets.

Our calculations result in the RAB increasing from $28.8 billion on 1 July 2025 to $36.2 billion by
30 June 2030. Our full RAB roll forward calculations are shown in Appendix E.


Stormwater RAB is negative in 2029–30

For allocating costs, Sydney Water’s RAB is broken down into a separate water, wastewater and
stormwater RAB with costs associated with each service separated so that customers pay cost-
reflective service prices.

One observation is that the stormwater RAB’s closing value becomes negative in the last year of
the determination period. This is mainly due to front-loaded developer contributions for the
Mamre/Aerotropolis growth project, while capital works roll out more gradually. As a result, cash
contributions to the RAB exceed costs during this period (see Table 6.2).

6.2.1      We did not make any RAB adjustments for Rouse Hill developer charges
           and Blue Mountains Tunnel finance lease payments

Our decision is:

                 8.   To exclude from the RAB, Sydney Water’s proposed adjustment of:
                      a. $485 million for historical Rouse Hill developer charges between 2000
                          and 2009
                      b.   $140 million for historical Blue Mountains Tunnel finance lease payments
                           between 1990 and 2016.


In its revised proposal, Sydney Water accepted our draft decisions 157 and so we have maintained
our decision to exclude these proposed adjustments from Sydney Water’s RAB.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                         Page | 77
                                                                                         Other costs and notional revenue




There is not sufficient evidence to substantiate Sydney Water’s claim regarding the
regulatory treatment of Rouse Hill developer contributions
Previously, Rouse Hill’s integrated water cycle management (IWCM) facility costs were ringfenced
from regulated bill setting. These assets were paid for through capital contributions from
developers and not included in the RAB. Sydney Water claimed that between 2000 and 2009, all
developer contributions, including those related to ring-fenced assets in Rouse Hill, were deducted
erroneously from the RAB. 158 This reduces the revenue available to fund regulated water and
wastewater services, at the expense of taxpayers who implicitly pick up the shortfall.

Sydney Water proposed to add $485 million ($2024–25) to the RAB ($90 million related to water
assets and $395 million related to wastewater assets) to correct this error, which it claims it found
in 2010. 159 However, it was unable to establish that the treatment of developer contributions was an
error, as opposed to a regulatory decision at the time and that its $485 million estimate was robust
and would not confer a windfall gain on Sydney Water.

Our review of Sydney Water’s $485 million calculation found that over 50% of the claimed error
included contributions for regulated water and wastewater assets, not just ring-fenced assets as
claimed. We have not been able to verify that Rouse Hill capex was not rolled into the RAB over the
same period that cash contributions were deducted.

We are not convinced that it is in the long-term interests of water and wastewater customers to
pay higher prices over the 2025 determination period for an unsubstantiated benefit previous
customers may have derived at the expense of taxpayers up to 25 years ago.


All relevant Blue Mountains Tunnel finance lease costs have been accounted for in
Sydney Water’s regulatory accounts
Sydney Water proposed to add $140 million ($2024–25) to the wastewater RAB to address an
historic error that it claims was made in relation to the treatment of the Blue Mountains Tunnel
(BMT) finance lease charges between 1990 and 2016. 160 These lease payments relate to the Blue
Mountains Sewage Transfer Scheme, which was developed in the 1990s to transport wastewater
from the upper Blue Mountains to the treatment plant at Winmalee via a 39 km tunnel.

Sydney Water claims that while maintenance and inspection costs were recorded in Sydney
Water’s regulatory accounts as operating costs and recovered through the annual revenue
requirement, no provisions were made for principal and interest payments.

We have excluded this proposed adjustment from the RAB because we consider that all relevant
finance lease costs were accounted for in Sydney Water’s regulatory accounts at the time.

While early reporting from 2008 lacked granularity, we are convinced Sydney Water’s BMT lease
costs were included in our assessment of efficient operating expenditure. a In 2013, Sydney Water
sought to classify them as finance leases and we changed our regulatory approach to separate
these costs at our 2016 price review.




a
    In 2010, we restructured the annual information return templates to separately identify BMT lease costs as 100%
    operating costs and backward extending data collection indicates they were treated the same way back in 2008.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                    Page | 78
                                                                            Other costs and notional revenue




We would not make an adjustment for additional expenditure that arises because of
policy and regulatory changes
In its proposal, Sydney Water claimed $229 million in unfunded Rouse Hill stormwater and
recycled water expenditure, which it does not intend to claim at this time. 161 In further
correspondence with IPART, Sydney Water noted that this stemmed from previous policy settings
and regulatory decisions rather than an error. 162

In general, we do not consider it appropriate to make RAB adjustments for expenses incurred from
changes to policy and regulatory settings. This would effectively involve a retrospective re-opening
of those decisions and would contradict our role as a regulator to provide a benchmark maximum
revenue allowance for Sydney Water to deliver its service obligations, rather than review specific
project costs. It would also be difficult to determine in retrospect how a past Tribunal would have
assessed these costs and their efficiencies under the new policy setting.

We consider that having greater regulatory certainty about the future treatment of expenditure
would be beneficial to Sydney Water and its customers.


6.3     Return on assets

Our decision is:

            9.   To set an allowance of $5.4 billion for the return on assets component of the
                 notional revenue requirement, noting that:
                 a. the opening RAB on 1 July 2025 is $28.8 billion
                 b.   we added $10.6 billion in capital costs, net of disposals and depreciation
                 c.   we used a real post tax WACC of 3.3% as the efficient rate of return.


We include an allowance for return on assets in the revenue requirement to account for the
opportunity cost of capital invested to provide regulated services. This allows businesses to
continue to make efficient capital investments in the future. We calculate the return on assets by
multiplying the value of the regulatory asset base (RAB) over the determination period by an
efficient rate of return.

We calculated a return on assets allowance of $5.4 billion for Sydney Water over the 2025
determination period.


6.3.1   We used a real return on capital (post-tax real WACC) of 3.3%

As in previous reviews, we determined the rate of return using a weighted average cost of capital
(WACC). We used our standard WACC approach 163 to calculate a WACC of 3.3% for Sydney Water’s
prices. This is lower than the 3.6% WACC that Sydney Water used to calculate the revenue
requirement in its pricing proposal, due to changes in market conditions since Sydney Water’s
proposal in September 2024.
The equivalent pre-tax real WACC is 4.1%.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                      Page | 79
                                                                            Other costs and notional revenue




A full step-through of our WACC calculation is provided in Appendix C.


6.4     Return of assets (regulatory depreciation)

Our decision is:

            10. To set the return of assets (regulatory depreciation) at $3.2 billion



We include an allowance for depreciation in the notional revenue requirement so that the
capital invested by Sydney Water in its regulatory assets is returned over the useful life of
each asset.

Consistent with our usual approach, we used the straight-line depreciation method to calculate
regulatory depreciation. Under this method, the assets in the RAB are depreciated by an equal
value in each year of their economic life. We consider this method balances the need for simplicity,
consistency and transparency.

We did not make changes to underlying asset lives for any asset types. Appendix E shows our
decisions on asset lives for the 2025 determination period.


6.5     Return on working capital

Our decision is:

            11. To set the return on working capital as $76.3 million over the 2025 determination
                period.


The working capital allowance component of the NRR represents the return the business could
earn on the net amount of working capital it requires each year to meet its service obligations. It
allows the business to recover the cost it incurs due to the time delay between providing a service
and receiving the money for it (i.e. when the bills are paid).

In 2018, we developed a standard approach to calculate the working capital allowance.

The amount we allowed for the 2025 determination period represents the holding cost of net
current assets.


6.6     Tax allowance

Our decision is:

            12. To set the tax allowance as $137.6 million over the 2025 determination period.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 80
                                                                                           Other costs and notional revenue




We include an explicit allowance for tax because we use a post-tax WACC to estimate the
allowance for a return on assets in the revenue requirement. This tax allowance reflects the
regulated business’s forecast tax liabilities. The tax allowance is not intended to recover Sydney
Water’s actual tax liability over the determination period. Rather, it reflects the liability to which a
comparable commercial business would be subject.

We applied our standard method to set the tax allowance, with an adjustment for franking credits
to the business’s (nominal) taxable income. This sets aside 22.5% of cash contributions for income
tax purposes, consistent with the parameters we use to set the WACC, where imputation credits
are valued at 0.25. b



       Box 6.1 Tax implications of assets free of charge
       Regulated businesses can receive contributions from developers towards
       infrastructure for new development in 2� forms: as cash from developer charges or as
       assets constructed by the developer and gifted to the regulated business called
       Assets Free of Charge (AFOC) or gifted assets.

       We do not include the value of these assets in a business’ RAB because the business
       has not made any investment in them. By extension, customers should not have to
       pay for a return on (or of) assets for which a business has not incurred costs.

       However, businesses are required to pay income tax on assets received free of
       charge. To account for this income tax liability, we include a tax allowance for these
       assets in the NRR.

       In our Draft Report, we excluded allowances for tax on AFOC (and associated
       depreciation allowances) in the NRR based on a recent ruling in the case of Victoria
       Power Networks Pty Ltd v Commissioner of Taxationa, The Full Federal Court of
       Australia ruled that certain assets gifted to certain regulated businesses had an
       assessable income of $0.

       However, in its response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water maintained that
       circumstances and conditions of the Victoria Power Networks case are different to
       Sydney Water’s existing regulatory regime.b Sydney Water advised that it had
       received tax advice that there were insufficient factors to support a positive outcome
       in a Private Binding Ruling. It asked IPART to reinstate the tax allowance on AFOC
       because it would create a financial incentive to collect developer contributions
       through cash rather than AFOC.c




b
    Under IPART’s framework, this represents the amount by which the total allowed return on equity is reduced to reflect
    the imputation credits that investors will receive. Our standard value is 0.25. See IPART, Review of our WACC method –
    Final Report, February 2018, p 82.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                      Page | 81
                                                                                         Other costs and notional revenue




        Box 6.1 Tax implications of assets free of charge
        We have considered Sydney Water’s submission and agree that at present, there are
        significant uncertainties surrounding the tax implications of AFOC. We consider that
        these uncertainties are too large to warrant a pre-emptive removal of the tax
        allowance from the NRR. As such, we have maintained our usual approach of
        including a tax allowance for AFOC in Sydney Water’s prices.

        We will continue to monitor the Australian Tax Office’s review of the issue over this
        pricing period and will consider whether any changes might be warranted to our
        approach to setting tax allowances at the 2030 price review. If the ATO clarify any
        changes to the tax treatment of AFOC during this determination period, it would be
        open to a future Tribunal to true-up any resulting over-recoveries of the tax
        allowance from customers’ bills in Sydney Water’s next price review.

        a Victoria Power Networks Pty Ltd v Commissioner of Taxation [2020] FCAFC 169.
        b Sydney Water submission to IPART Draft Report, July 2025, p 47.
