6.7       Revenue adjustments

Our decision is:

               13. To make the following revenue adjustments to Sydney Water’s notional revenue
                   requirement over the 2025 determination period:
                   a. $314.0 million for the Demand Volatility Adjustment Mechanism (DVAM)
                     b.   -$69.0 million for the cost of debt true-up
                     c.   $336.0 million for the deferral year true-up.




6.7.1     We have included $314.0 million to compensate Sydney Water for under-
          recovered revenue because of lower than forecast demand

Under the price cap approach, we use a Demand Volatility Adjustment Mechanism (DVAM), to
adjust for any over- or under-recovery of revenue resulting from actual demand being different to
forecasts. The DVAM protects businesses from under-recovery due to lower than forecast water
sales and protects customers in the case of any over-recovery through bills.

In 2020, we set the DVAM threshold at ±5% for Sydney Water. This means Sydney Water is only
able to recover the difference between its actual sales and the 5% ceiling, if the difference is
greater than ±5% over the price determination period. This 5% threshold incentivises businesses to
accurately forecast and manage water sales. We make DVAM adjustments in the determination
period after the differences have occurred.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                   Page | 82
                                                                          Other costs and notional revenue




Between 2019 and 2024, Sydney Water’s actual demand was lower than its forecast demand. As a
result, it under-recovered revenue compared to what was initially forecast. We applied our DVAM
calculation method to calculate a revenue adjustment of $314.0 million to account for demand
volatility over the 2020 determination period. This is consistent with what Sydney Water calculated
in its pricing proposal, adjusted for actual 2023–24 volumes.


6.7.2 We have trued-up Sydney Water’s cost of debt to -$69.0 million

Our 2018 WACC methodology introduced a trailing average cost of debt. Under this method the
WACC changes every year as new tranches of debt are introduced to the trailing averages and the
oldest tranches drop out. At each price review we would consider whether to:

•   update prices annually to reflect the updates in the WACC annually, or

•   use a regulatory true-up at the next period, which we would pass through to prices at the
    beginning of the next period.

We have decided to use a true-up approach for changes to the cost of debt, consistent with our
approach in Sydney Water’s 2020 Determination. We consider this reduces price fluctuations
within price periods for customers while ensuring that businesses are adequately compensated for
changes in the cost of debt that occur within each price period.

We have calculated a cost of debt true-up for the 2020 determination period of -$69.0 million,
which is consistent with Sydney Water’s pricing proposal. Our decision is to include this true-up as
an adjustment to Sydney Water’s 2025–30 NRR.


6.7.3 We have trued-up the cost of Sydney Water’s deferral year to $336.0 million

In 2021, we agreed to defer the scheduled 2023–24 water price review for Sydney Water by one
year. This meant that the 2023–24 prices set out in the 2020 Determination remained constant in
nominal terms in 2024–25. As a result, Sydney Water under-recovered its efficient costs over
2024–25. IPART agreed to true-up the efficient costs of the deferral year that Sydney Water did not
recover through prices and consider including those costs in setting prices for the 2025
determination period.

We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal that it should recover deferral year costs and calculated
the amount to be $336.0 million. To do this, we calculated the NRR for one year based on 2024–25
parameters and our standard building block approach. The true-up amount is the difference
between our calculation of the NRR for 2024–25, and the revenue the business expects to receive in
2024–25, based on actual prices and forecast volumes. This is not a true-up to the actual costs
incurred by the business, but a true-up compared to if we had set prices in our usual way for 2024–
25. This differs from Sydney Water’s proposal of $433 million because we used a lower WACC of
3.1%. We calculated the WACC as though a new 4-year determination period would start in 2023–24,
whereas Sydney Water used the WACC from the 2020 determination period of 3.4%.

Appendix E provides further explanation and calculation of the deferral year true-up.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                    Page | 83
                                                                        Other costs and notional revenue




6.7.4 We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to share 100% of recycled
      water revenue with customers

In its revised proposal, Sydney Water proposed to share 100% of recycled water usage revenue
from least-cost Integrated Water Cycle Management (IWCM) schemes with its wastewater and
stormwater customers. Sydney Water claimed that least-cost schemes are essential services, not
optional revenue-raising. The revenue exists only because Sydney Water must provide wastewater
and stormwater services, and it does not reduce drinking water sales revenue. 164

Historically, we either let businesses keep this revenue, or decided to share it 50:50 with
customers, depending on the extent to which it replaced drinking water. We treated it like non-
regulated revenue and shared it so both customers and the business benefited. 165

We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to return 100% of this revenue to customers based on
the service it relates to – wastewater or stormwater. This change means Sydney Water’s customers
will share around $70 million in recycled water revenue over 5 years. It reduces:

•   water and wastewater bills by about $5 by 2029-30, or $1 per year on average
•   combined water, wastewater and stormwater bills by around $17 by 2029-30, or $12 per year
    on average.

                                                  Risk allocation




s




                                Chapter 7

                                Risk allocation




                                07
Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                          Page | 85
                                                                                         Risk allocation




   Summary of our decisions on risk sharing


   Maintain the Shoalhaven Transfer fixed service charge cost pass-through
   The existing Shoalhaven Transfer fixed service charge cost pass-through largely meets our
   cost pass-through principles. The mechanism allows Sydney Water to pass through to
   customers the additional cost it incurs from WaterNSW when the Shoalhaven Transfer
   Scheme is operating.


   Replace the SDP fixed service charge cost pass-through with an allowance for
   Sydney Water's efficient SDP bulk water purchases
   The existing SDP fixed service charge cost pass-through does not meet our cost pass-
   through principles. The mechanism was originally introduced when SDP operated in either
   drought response or shutdown modes, and allowed the costs of these different operating
   modes to be passed through to customers. However, SDP’s role has now expanded beyond
   drought response. It operates flexibly and is required to respond to production requests
   from Sydney Water. This means there is no single trigger event to operationalise SDP.
   Instead, it is within Sydney Water’s influence.

   The cost pass-through may also influence decision making by inhibiting cost transparency.
   The mechanism will mean that the costs are automatically passed through to customers
   which may bias decision making on when and how to operationalise SDP for the purposes
   of water security management.

   While Sydney Water is required to increase its production requests so SDP operates at
   maximum capacity when water storage levels fall below 75%, we have factored a forecast
   of these costs into the funding envelope for operating expenditure. We consider the
   funding envelope better balances revenue risk for Sydney Water under drought conditions
   with incentives to pursue least-cost bulk water purchases, compared with the existing cost
   pass-through.


   Maintain drought water usage pricing
   The existing drought water usage charge meets our cost pass-through principles. The
   mechanism is dynamic and sets the water usage price higher when water storage levels fall
   below 60% and reverts back to the original variable water usage price when storage levels
   rise above 70%. The mechanism is an equitable and cost-reflective way to recover Sydney
   Water’s additional costs for managing drought and to provide cost-reflective signals to
   customers about the additional costs of water supply during drought.

   We did not accept Sydney Water’s proposal – if there is no SDP cost pass-through – to
   trigger drought pricing when water storage levels fall below 75%, rather than the current
   60% trigger. The funding envelope already accounts for forecast SDP purchase volumes
   with dams operating at different storage levels, including below 75%. Further, the Greater
   Sydney Drought Response Plan does not envisage the water restriction regime (and
   associated costs) being activated until storage levels fall below 60%.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 86
                                                                                           Risk allocation




    Consider an SDP expansion true-up for efficient costs in the next review

    Given the uncertainty in the timing and costs, we see merit in introducing a true-up for
    efficient costs associated with an expanded SDP. While we cannot bind a future Tribunal, a
    true-up for efficient costs, rather than actual costs as proposed by Sydney Water, plus
    holding costs, in the next price review will result in more cost-reflective prices and
    equitable risk sharing between customers and Sydney Water.


    Consider a true-up to manage changes in bulk water prices in the next review

    While we cannot bind a future Tribunal, a true-up for changes in bulk water prices may
    be appropriate. We expect changes in bulk water prices over the 2025 determination
    period, and the materiality of these changes is currently unknown. A true-up would
    enable the Tribunal to consider the implications of the changes in bulk water prices at
    the next price review.


    Not include a regulatory mechanism to true-up growth-related expenditure
    We did not accept Sydney Water proposal for an in-period true-up to recover growth costs
    that exceed its funding envelope for capital expenditure. Sydney Water has flexibility to
    service population growth, meaning it has influence over what expenditure to include in the
    true-up. This may incentivise Sydney Water to prefer investments it can true-up, rather than
    the mix of infrastructure priorities that optimise customer value.




7.1      Price control

Our decision is:

              14. To allow a price cap approach to regulation



In line with our Water Regulation Handbook, water businesses can propose a form of price control
that is in their customers’ interest. Also in our framework is that the default regulatory period lasts
for 5 years. Sydney Water proposed to maintain its current form of control, which is a price cap. The
Justice and Equity Centre supported the current price cap approach. 166

A price cap approach has some important benefits such as:

•     maintaining consistent revenue streams to support the business’s operations

•     providing predictable, transparent, and stable prices to customers.

Further information on price controls and the different forms of price control is available in section
4.7.3 of the Water Regulation Handbook.

We accept Sydney Water’s proposal to continue with a price cap approach for the 2025
determination period.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 87
                                                                                                                Risk allocation




7.2       Risk sharing
The water regulation framework seeks to promote the long-term interest of customers, identifying
and rewarding businesses that sustain better customer outcomes and cost efficiencies. However,
we recognise that within a determination period there are inherent uncertainties that may require
additional costs (or avoided costs) to be shared between customers and the business if they arise.

This section outlines how we assessed Sydney Water’s requests for revenue risk mechanisms.
Specifically, how we balance the needs of businesses to manage revenue risks (from unforeseen or
uncertain large step changes in costs) with consumer protection and independent scrutiny.

In doing so, we considered whether the risk mechanisms would lead to prices being more closely
aligned to Sydney Water’s cost of providing services, in accordance with sections 14A(2)(a) and
15(1)(a) of the IPART Act. We also had regard to the potential social impacts from the proposed risk
mechanisms on customers, as well as the need for greater efficiency in the supply of services to
reduce costs for the benefit of consumers and taxpayers, as required under sections 15(1)(e) and
15(1)(k) of the IPART Act.

Information on our revenue risk sharing framework and principles is set out in Chapter 5 of the
Water Regulation Handbook.


7.2.1     Cost pass-throughs

Our decisions are:

               15. To continue the Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-through mechanism.

               16. To replace the SDP cost pass-through mechanism with an allowance for Sydney
                   Water's efficient SDP bulk water purchases.

               17. To maintain drought water usage pricing.


The prices we set provide a funding envelope for the water business over the next 5 years. That
funding envelope is built on our decisions of the efficient costs it should incur in providing services
over that period.

However, there are inherent uncertainties around future needs, costs and events that might mean a
water business needs to spend more (or less) than we expect when setting prices. Sometimes,
when there is a known, material cost that the business cannot control, we can include a cost pass-
through (up front) in the determination. If we do, the costs are incurred within the determination
period, and the business can automatically pass the costs through to customers. c




c
    Further information of our approach to cost pass-throughs and our principles are available in section 5.1.1 of the Water
    Regulation Handbook.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                        Page | 88
                                                                                                        Risk allocation




Cost pass-throughs are intended only for large step changes in costs with material impact on a
business. In setting a cost pass-through, we would review the efficient cost of managing an event
and set a price or a methodology for calculating the price. This provides an appropriate balance of
revenue risk management with oversight for consumer protection.

Sydney Water originally proposed to maintain 3 cost pass-throughs in its pricing proposal, all
relating to costs associated with bulk water. It proposed to:

•   increase fixed water service charges to cover the additional costs of buying water pumped
    from the Shoalhaven River

•   change fixed service charges to recover the costs of the Sydney Desalination Plant

•   increase the variable water usage charge per kilolitre during droughts, when its costs increase
    and customers’ water use is restricted. 167

These cost pass-throughs are set out in more detail below.


Shoalhaven Transfer fixed service charge cost pass-through (existing mechanism)
During periods of low water availability, WaterNSW can pump water from dams in the Shoalhaven
River catchment which it sells to Sydney Water. Pumping is triggered when Sydney dam levels are
below 75% and continues until storage levels reach 80%. 168 WaterNSW can pass through these
additional costs it incurs from pumping to Sydney Water. d

Sydney Water proposed to maintain its existing Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-through
mechanism. The mechanism passes through to customers the difference in Sydney Water’s
forecast and actual bulk water costs from WaterNSW as a result of the Shoalhaven transfers.
Sydney Water would increase the fixed water service charge paid by households and businesses in
the year after costs are incurred.

Sydney Water stated that the mechanism means:

•   customers only incur the higher price from the cost pass-through when the service is
    required, and

•   Sydney Water recovers the higher costs when the pumping occurs. 169

Our decision is to maintain the mechanism. We found the mechanism meets our cost pass-
through principles. In particular, Sydney Water does not have control over the trigger event and
the resulting efficient costs can be fully assessed before they are passed through to customers.
(see Box 7.1).




d
    We have made a draft decision to maintain the Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-through in the prices WaterNSW charges
    Sydney Water (IPART, Review of prices for WaterNSW’s services in Greater Sydney from 1 October 2025, Draft Report,
    July 2025, p 73).




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                Page | 89
                                                                                              Risk allocation




      Box 7.1 Assessment of the Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-through
      We consider Sydney Water’s proposal for the Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-
      through meets our cost pass-through principles.

      •    Trigger event: There is a clear trigger event for Shoalhaven transfers as set out in
           the Water Sharing Plan for the Greater Metropolitan Region Unregulated River
           Water Sources 2023.

      •    Efficient cost: The resulting efficient forecast electricity costs associated with the
           transfers can be fully assessed before they are passed through to customers.
      •    Materiality threshold: There is no applicable materiality threshold, however, the
           Shoalhaven Transfer Scheme is energy intensive and energy prices are volatile
           and can change significantly which can have a material impact on costs to
           WaterNSW and subsequently Sydney Water.

      •    Efficient and equitable: A cost pass-through mechanism is the most efficient
           and equitable way to deal with costs associated with the Shoalhaven Transfer
           Scheme as it means that customers will not incur costs until the event is
           triggered and only the efficient forecast, rather than actual, costs are passed
           through.

      •    Symmetric: There is no symmetric treatment for over- or under-recovery of
           actual costs relative to forecast costs. However, this principle is not applicable as
           we set the cost pass-through mechanism based on forecast costs.

      •    Efficient cost of service: The Shoalhaven Transfer cost pass-through will result
           in customer prices that better reflect the efficient cost of service as it signals to
           customers the higher costs of supply augmentation during times of increased
           water scarcity.
      Source: IPART analysis.




SDP fixed service charge cost pass-through (existing mechanism)

Sydney Water proposed to maintain the existing SDP cost pass-through in its pricing proposal. The
SDP cost pass-through was introduced in the 2012 Determination to address uncertainty around
SDP’s operating schedule and its impact on Sydney Water’s costs. The mechanism accounts for
differences in SDP’s forecast and actual charges to Sydney Water. Similar to the Shoalhaven
Transfer cost pass-through mechanism, Sydney Water considers the existing SDP cost pass-
through to be fit-for-purpose. 170




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       Page | 90
                                                                                             Risk allocation




Our decision is to remove the mechanism. We found it does not meet our key cost pass-through
principles (see Box 7.2). In particular:

•    The trigger event is less clear as SDP’s role has expanded beyond drought response. Sydney
     Water has more flexibility and discretion in whether, and if so when, it buys bulk water from
     SDP. In 2012, we set Sydney Water’s maximum prices based on SDP being in water security
     shutdown mode throughout the period. We included a cost pass-through so that Sydney Water
     could recover the additional efficient costs if a drought triggered SDP’s operation. The trigger
     for SDP was defined using the rules set out in the Metropolitan Water Plan.

     However, SDP’s water production use has changed, and it has remained operational since 2019.
     Under its current Network Operator’s Licence, SDP is required to operate on a flexible full-time
     basis so that it can be operated (as requested by Sydney Water) as part of Greater Sydney’s
     total water system and maximise its contribution to water security for the region. 171
     Sydney Water has significant discretion and influence over when, and how much, it purchases
     water from SDP. Sydney Water’s Decision Framework for SDP Operation (the Decision
     Framework) 172 sets out the framework for how Sydney Water makes production requests to
     SDP. e
•    The operation of SDP is not outside of Sydney Water’s influence. Sydney Water can influence
     the NSW Government’s decisions on water security management, through providing input into
     the Greater Sydney Water Strategy and the Greater Sydney Drought Response Plan. A key
     direction of the Greater Sydney Water Strategy was to change the operation of SDP from
     ‘drought response’ to flexible full-time operation. Additionally, Sydney Water developed the
     Decision Framework (which is based on satisfying the Greater Sydney Water Strategy
     objectives) that sets out the framework for it making production requests to SDP.

•    The SDP cost pass-through may not result in the efficient cost of service. Sydney Water
     should have the right incentives to optimise where it sources its bulk water. The SDP cost pass-
     through mechanism reduces, to an extent, its financial incentive to find the right balance
     between water security and its own bulk water costs. It makes Sydney Water financially
     indifferent as to whether it purchases water from SDP, or from alternative and cheaper sources
     of water (such as from WaterNSW). This could result in decision-makers favouring purchasing
     water from SDP, as well as over-valuing the benefit customers get from water security.
     Removing the cost pass-through would provide an incentive for Sydney Water to purchase
     bulk water from SDP when it is efficient and cost-effective to do so. It would also promote an
     appropriate balance between cost and water security outcomes for customers.




e
    SDP is required to comply with any such requests under its Network Operator’s Licence.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 91
                                                                                               Risk allocation




      Box 7.2 Assessment of the SDP cost pass-through
      We consider Sydney Water’s proposal for the SDP cost pass-through does not meet
      most of our cost pass-through principles.

      •    Trigger event: SDP now operates flexibly and must comply with production
           requests from Sydney Water under its Decision Framework for SDP Operation
           (prepared by Sydney Water). When storage levels are:

           — above 75%, there is no clear trigger event in the Decision Framework and
             Sydney Water has influence over the operation of SDP.
           — below 75%, the Decision Framework requires Sydney Water to set its
             production requests for SDP to operate at full capacity.

      •    Efficient cost: The mechanism results in efficient costs as it adjusts the fixed
           water service price, in the following year, for the difference between SDP’s actual
           charges to Sydney Water, compared to the forecast revenue that we have
           already included in customer prices.

      •    Materiality threshold: There is no applicable materiality threshold. However,
           costs associated with SDP can have a material impact on Sydney Water, which it
           estimates to be around $54.9 million per year.

      •    Efficient and equitable: The mechanism is lagged so that the costs are only
           passed through to customers in the following year. This means customers will
           not incur costs until the event occurs.

      •    Symmetric: The mechanism is symmetrical as it calculates the actual costs, less
           the expected revenues and avoided costs, to be recovered (or returned) from all
           water customers through the mechanism.

      •    Efficient cost of service: The mechanism may disincentivise decision makers to
           fully factor in the full costs of when and how to operationalise SDP as the costs
           are automatically passed through to customers. This may unintentionally result in
           sub-optimal decisions on water management.
      Source: IPART analysis; Sydney Water, Price Proposal 2025–2030, September 2024, p 229.




Stakeholders had mixed views on removing the SDP cost pass-through

In response to our Draft Report, Sydney Water and the Sydney Desalination Plant disagreed with
our draft decision to remove the SDP cost pass-through. Sydney Water stated that:

•   The Decision Framework is designed to balance the cost of time spent and severity of water
    restrictions with the cost of producing more desalinated water. Removing this cost pass-
    through would provide a disincentive for Sydney Water to efficiently balance these costs of
    restrictions in managing its water supply network.

•   The mechanism meets the cost pass-through principles in the Water Regulation Framework. 173




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       Page | 92
                                                                                         Risk allocation




SDP also disagreed with removing the cost pass-through, in combination with the draft decision to
include a SDP bulk water allowance in operating expenditure, noting that customers might pay for
more desalinated water than they receive. 174

An individual stakeholder was in favour of removing the SDP pass-through, considering that it
would push Sydney Water to find cheaper water sources and the plant has barely been needed
since 2020. 175

If we remove the SDP cost pass-through, Sydney Water requested we amend the drought price
trigger so drought water usage pricing would apply when water storage levels fall below 75%. We
discuss its revised proposal in the section on drought water usage pricing below.

SDP’s function has changed since the last price review; it is now in flexible full-time
operating mode
Previously, the purpose of the SDP was to provide additional water supply during drought. It would
operate in shut-down or standby mode, with the requirement to switch on when directed - around
the point dam storage levels reached 60%. The SDP cost pass-through mechanism would
reconcile any difference between forecast and actual SDP bulk water purchase costs. This would
typically occur if SDP changed its operating mode during the period (from water security shutdown
to drought response).

The SDP is now in flexible full-time operating mode. The Decision Framework sets out the basis for
Sydney Water’s production requests to SDP (see Box 7.3). Sydney Water’s Operating Licence
requires it to purchase water from SDP consistent with the Decision Framework. 176



      Box 7.3 Decision Framework for SDP Operation
      The Decision Framework sets out operating rules for 3 distinct operating phases,
      depending on storage levels. They are:

      •    Ready to respond (>90%): A minimum daily purchase volume of 50ML/day, to
           maintain a state of readiness but also minimise dam spills when dams are above
           90% capacity.

      •    Operation flexibility (75% to 90%): Purchase volumes are optimised both to
           minimise dam spills and slow dam depletion rates during drought, based on
           additional weather and inflow indicators.

      •    Sustaining dam storage (<75%): Purchase volumes are set so SDP operates at
           maximum capacity when dam levels are 75% or below. Maximum capacity is
           250ML/day, unless there is maintenance required or other operational reason
           that supply would be reduced.
      Source: Sydney Water, Decision Framework for SDP Operation. June 2022.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                 Page | 93
                                                                                                             Risk allocation




Under SDP’s new operating mode, the funding envelope better balances revenue risk with
incentives to pursue least-cost bulk water purchases
Compared with the existing SDP cost pass-through, we consider the funding envelope provides
Sydney Water with incentives to efficiently manage its water supply network and pursue least-cost
bulk water purchases, while being able to manage its revenue risks during drought.

Data is available to model the probability of Sydney Water purchasing bulk water from SDP at
various storage levels under SDP’s full-time flexible operating mode. As discussed in Chapter 4, we
used this probabilistic dam level modelling to set Sydney Water’s funding envelope. It means that
SDP bulk water costs are now treated like other costs incurred by Sydney Water that we have
robust data to forecast and can factor into the funding envelope.

Under our approach, Sydney Water is compensated for higher SDP costs during drought. That is,
the funding envelope includes the probability of storage levels being below 75% for 15% of the time
(see Table 7.1).

Table 7.1 Storage level probabilities used to forecast bulk water purchase costs
 Dam level                                                   SDP usage (ML/d)           Proportion of time at that level
 Storage >90%                                                                   50                                  62%
 Storage 90% to 75%                                                            125                                  24%
 Storage <75%                                           250 (current full capacity)                                 15%
Note: Under the Decision Framework, Sydney Water must purchase a minimum of 50ML/day from SDP.


While Sydney Water bears some revenue risks under our approach compared with the existing
SDP cost pass-through, a business of its scale and maturity should be able to manage its risk in this
respect. We note that in the long-run, the risk is symmetric and Sydney Water should be no better
or worse off under these settings.

By contrast, given SDP’s new operating mode, maintaining the existing SDP cost pass-through
would mean Sydney Water effectively bears no risk on SDP purchases in non-drought conditions.
This would make it indifferent to the higher price of SDP water compared to least-cost water from
WaterNSW, which may lead to Sydney Water inefficiently operating its water supply network to
the detriment of its customers.

In addition, if there is no incentive for Sydney Water to purchase bulk water from WaterNSW where
it is the most efficient source (given factors such as price and water quality), this could have an
adverse effect on WaterNSW’s financial position.

Finally, exposing Sydney Water to a manageable level of revenue risk improves its incentives to
regularly review the Decision Framework to allow it to effectively balances water security with total
costs to customers.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                     Page | 94
                                                                                              Risk allocation




There may be merit in a separate mechanism to address changes in bulk water prices

The existing SDP cost pass-through mechanism adjusts Sydney Water prices for changes between
forecast and actual bulk water costs from SDP – i.e. both prices and volume. Our rationale for
discontinuing the cost pass-through relates to the impact of the mechanism on when and how
much Sydney Water sources bulk water volume from SDP. We recognise that SDP’s bulk water
prices are determined by IPART and outside of Sydney Water’s control. We separately discuss
mechanisms to address changes in bulk water prices below in section 7.2.2 on true-ups.


Drought water usage pricing (existing mechanism)

We introduced dynamic water usage pricing in our 2020 Sydney Water price review to reflect that
water businesses faced additional costs during drought and to send a stronger signal to customers to
conserve water in periods of scarcity. Under the mechanism, the water usage price increases when
water storage levels are low. Box 7.4 explains how the dynamic drought pricing mechanism works.



      Box 7.4 The drought pricing mechanism has rolling triggers (60% ‘on’
      and 70% ‘off’)
      The dynamic drought pricing mechanism means that the variable water usage
      charge varies between a non-drought price and a higher drought price, based on
      dam storage levels. The mechanism includes a ‘rolling’ trigger where the drought
      water usage price will apply from 31 days after dam levels fall below 60% and return
      to the base price 31 days after dam levels exceed 70% again (i.e. 60% ‘on’ and 70%
      ‘off’ triggers).

      The rolling trigger has various advantages:
      •    The ‘on’ and ‘off’ triggers are asymmetric so only a significant increase in water
           storage levels will turn off the drought price. This will minimise price volatility due
           to small fluctuations in dam levels and provide the water business with greater
           certainty of its funding for drought management projects.
      •    The drought price only applies for a limited time and is closely related to dam
           levels to reflect the water business’s costs.
      •    By lagging the trigger by one month, a water business is able to communicate
           with customers about price changes, which would provide a better opportunity
           for customers to adjust their behaviour.

      The variable water usage price is calculated by starting with the non-drought water
      usage charge, and then:
      •    Adding the efficient operating costs of responding to drought, including: costs for
           implementing water conservation programs; costs incurred in enforcement or
           communications during water restrictions; or drought management overheads.
      •    Reducing water sales forecasts to reflect the impact of water restrictions.
      Source: IPART analysis.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                        Page | 95
                                                                                               Risk allocation




Our decision is to maintain the drought pricing mechanism. Setting a dynamic drought water usage
price is an efficient mechanism to signal to customers the higher costs for water businesses to
manage drought, incentivises customers to manage their water usage during drought conditions,
and that the higher costs incurred by businesses during droughts are uncontrollable and should be
recovered via a pass-through. We found that the drought pricing mechanism meets our key cost
pass-through principles (see Box 7.5).



      Box 7.5 Assessment of drought water usage pricing
      We consider drought water usage pricing meets our cost pass-through principles.

      •    Trigger event: A rolling trigger is adopted based on defined water storage levels.

      •    Efficient cost: Drought water usage price and sales forecasts are designed to
           recover Sydney Water’s efficient additional drought costs and account for the
           effect of water restrictions on demand.

      •    Materiality threshold: There is no applicable materiality threshold. However,
           periods of drought heighten Sydney Water’s risk due to the compounding effect
           of higher costs (e.g. administering drought restrictions) and lower revenue (from
           reduced water usage from customers complying with water restrictions). Sydney
           Water estimates that drought non-bulk water costs could be potentially $52.3
           million per year (on top of baseline water conservation activities).

      •    Efficient and equitable: Drought is an uncontrollable and uncertain event.
           Drought water usage pricing is the most efficient and equitable mechanism as it
           only passes the efficient forecast costs through to customers when the event
           occurs and costs are incurred. Recovering estimated drought costs through
           higher usage price statically (rather than dynamically) risks over-recovery.
      •    Symmetric: The cost pass-through will be forecast costs. There is no symmetric
           treatment of over- or under-recovery of actual costs relative to forecast costs.
      •    Efficient cost of service: Drought water usage pricing will provide a signal to
           customers about the increased costs of supplying water. In theory, a more cost-
           reflective pricing approach would be to apply a separate price uplift for each
           different drought trigger or action (e.g. dam levels, implementation of water
           restrictions, water conservation projects, etc.). However, in practice, these
           individual uplifts would be small and only provide a minor price signal to
           customers as to the short-term impact of their water usage.
      Source: IPART analysis. Sydney Water, Price Proposal 2025–2030, September 2024, p 229.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       Page | 96
                                                                                             Risk allocation




A few stakeholders commented on drought water usage pricing

An individual stakeholder considered that people should be encouraged to use more water when
dams are close to full (to reduce the risk of spills), and less water when dam levels have dropped to
a point where it increases the risk of water restrictions. 177 We note that having a higher water usage
pricing during drought – which occurs under the existing drought pricing mechanism – sends a
clear message to customers about the need to change behaviour and conserve water while it is
most scarce.

The Justice and Equity Centre submitted that the current approach to drought pricing is not in
consumers’ best interests or well supported by consumers. Further, it does not regard drought
pricing as a fair or effective way to encourage water conservation. The Justice and Equity Centre
recommended an alternative price structure such as an inclining block tariff (IBT) where the
marginal water usage price increases as customers use more water. 178

We have considered this feedback on drought pricing and our decision is to maintain the cost pass-
through for drought water usage prices. In our view, the drought pricing mechanism allows Sydney
Water to recover the increased cost of providing its water services during droughts.

We have maintained our view about an IBT as expressed in the 2020 Final Report on Sydney
Water’s prices. 179 We consider that it would penalise higher water use regardless of storage levels,
disadvantaging larger families. It would also distort consumption and investment decisions given, at
any point in time, a price tier within an IBT would be either too low or too high. Further, in setting the
tiers, it would be difficult to establish an essential or base level consumption that could apply to
both residential and non-residential customers.

Sydney Water proposed triggering drought water usage pricing when storage levels fall
below 75% (rather than the current 60% trigger)
Sydney Water originally proposed maintaining the drought pricing mechanism with the existing
60% ‘on’ and ‘70’ off triggers. In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water subsequently
proposed that – if the SDP cost pass-through was not retained – then the drought price triggers
should change.

Sydney Water considered that the existing 60% ‘on’ trigger for drought water usage pricing
reflected settings in the 2017 Metropolitan Water Plan which were no longer NSW Government
policy. It noted that, under the Greater Sydney Water Strategy and Greater Sydney Drought
Response Plan, SDP is operating at maximum capacity when dam levels fall below 75%. Therefore,
this 75% storage level should also be the on/off trigger for drought water usage pricing. 180

Moving to a symmetric 75% drought price trigger may over-compensate Sydney Water for
its drought costs and introduce greater bill volatility
We have decided to maintain the existing triggers for drought water usage prices. Sydney Water’s
proposed modification to the drought pricing mechanism would not meet one of our key cost pass-
through principles – efficient cost of service. Changing the on/off drought triggers to 75%, without
also adjusting the value of the drought price uplift, may over-recover Sydney Water’s costs.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 97
                                                                                               Risk allocation




Drought water usage pricing is intended to provide a signal to customers about Sydney Water’s
increased costs of supplying water in drought conditions, as well as the revenue it forgoes due to
restrictions on customer water use. Under the Greater Sydney Drought Response Plan, the water
restriction regime is not anticipated to be activated until storage levels fall to 60%. 181 If the drought
price trigger changes to 75%, without modifying the value of the drought price uplift ($0.41/kL),
there would be less alignment with Sydney Water’s drought costs from water restrictions
compared with the existing trigger. In particular:

•   The drought price uplift factors in a revenue adjustment for reduced demand, based on
    expected demand under Level 2 water restrictions. By assuming Level 2 water restrictions
    (previously Level 3 under the 2020 drought price), Sydney Water considers it is taking on
    greater risk in the case that a higher, more severe level of water restrictions is reached.

•   While this is true, we note that Sydney Water would likely spend relatively more time at no
    water restrictions (or less severe Level 1 water restrictions) under a 75% trigger compared with a
    60% trigger. Therefore, Sydney Water may effectively be over-compensated during this time,
    as the initial demand reductions would be relatively less under the modified drought trigger.

Apart from looking at the drought price through the lens of efficient cost recovery for Sydney
Water, we also considered the customer perspective. When Sydney Water tested its drought price
options with customers during its customer engagement, the consultation was based on a 60%
trigger. It may have received different feedback from customers with a drought price using a 75%
trigger. We agree with the Justice and Equity Centre’s submission that Sydney Water devise a
robust engagement process to address the question of drought pricing in advance of the next price
review. 182

The current 60% ‘on’ and 70% ‘off’ triggers are asymmetric to minimise price volatility due to small
fluctuations in dam levels. Sydney Water proposed that the revised drought price turn on at below
75% and then off at 75%. This symmetric trigger may introduce greater bill volatility compared with
the existing asymmetric triggers. Sydney Water did note customers may be subject to bill instability
if dam levels hover around 75% storage levels. However, it also found that historical data on storage
levels suggests they do not typically rebound quickly after depleting below 75%, potentially
reducing the risk of bill volatility. 183


7.2.2   True-ups

Our decisions are:

            18. To not allow a Sydney Desalination Plant expansion true-up mechanism for actual
                costs incurred.

            19. To ask that the Tribunal consider at the next determination of Sydney Water’s
                prices whether a true-up of revenue is merited over the 2025 determination period
                due to the efficient costs associated with an expanded Sydney Desalination Plant.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                       Page | 98
                                                                                             Risk allocation




            20. To ask that the Tribunal consider at the next determination of Sydney Water’s
                prices whether a true-up of revenue is merited over the 2025 determination period
                due to changes in bulk water prices resulting from future bulk water price
                determinations.

            21. To not allow a within Determination Period true-up for growth-
                related expenditure.


While in general we expect businesses to manage within the expenditure envelope we set at the
start of a determination, there are circumstances where this is not possible. If costs change
materially during a determination period, businesses can apply for a true-up of costs at the next
price review. The costs that the business will incur can then be recovered from customers in the
following period.

Further information on our approach to true-ups, including what criteria they need to meet as part
of our assessment, is available in section 5.1.2 of the Water Regulation Handbook. 184

Sydney Water originally proposed a true-up for SDP expansion costs for the upcoming 2025
determination period. 185 In its submission to our Draft Report, it also proposed a regulatory
mechanism to true up growth-related capital expenditure that exceeds the funding envelope. 186

In addition, we have decided to include a true-up for changes in bulk water prices arising from
changes in future price determinations related to sources of bulk water supply.


SDP expansion true-up

Sydney Water expects to incur additional bulk water costs from 2028–29 ($185 million each year)
related to the expansion of SDP. 187 Sydney Water has proposed to not recover the forecast costs
over the 2025 determination period but to recover the actual operating costs incurred over the
2025 determination period relating to the expanded SDP, plus holding costs, as a true-up over the
2030 determination period.

Sydney Water notes that there is uncertainty with the timing of the expansion of SDP and a true-up
will provide that customers do not pay for costs that do not materialise, and if the project does start
and costs are realised, it will provide customers with additional time to adjust to bill increases. 188 We
note that the expansion of SDP is a decision for the NSW Government and there is uncertainty on
the timing and scope of costs for the project.

We see merit in considering a true-up mechanism for the efficient costs associated with the
expanded SDP, rather than actual costs as proposed by Sydney Water (see Box 7.6). It will be open
to the Tribunal at the time to consider and potentially adjust for any over or under-recovery due to
an expanded SDP in the next review of Sydney Water’s prices. This true-up could be implemented
by conducting an ex-post review of the costs incurred by Sydney Water and true-up the efficient
costs, plus holding costs, in the 2030 price review. This would also enable us to make decisions on
more robust data and information, and mitigate potential impacts on customers and Sydney Water.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 99
                                                                                               Risk allocation




      Box 7.6 Assessment of the SDP expansion true-up
      We applied cost pass-through principles to assess Sydney Water’s proposal for an
      SDP expansion true-up mechanism for actual costs incurred. While we found a true-
      up for actual costs would not meet most of our cost pass-through principles, it may
      be more appropriate to have a true-up for efficient costs associated with an
      expanded SDP.
      •    Trigger event: The expansion of SDP is a decision for the NSW Government.
           However, the operation of the expanded SDP will be subject to the expanded
           SDP operating licence. If it is similar to the current SDP operating licence and the
           expanded SDP operates flexibly and must respond to requests from Sydney
           Water, then there is no clear trigger event.
      •    Efficient cost: Sydney Water’s proposed true-up would be based on actual costs
           incurred by Sydney Water from the expanded SDP over the 2025 determination
           period. However, actual costs may not be efficient. An ex-post review of costs
           should determine what the efficient costs are for any true-up.
      •    Materiality threshold: There is no applicable materiality threshold. Sydney Water
           estimates that the potential costs could be $370 million over the determination
           period.a
      •    Efficient and equitable: A true-up of efficient costs will provide that customers
           only pay for the efficient costs actually incurred and the inclusion of holding
           costs will be equitable for customers and Sydney Water.
      •    Symmetric: Not applicable as the proposed true-up will be determined after
           costs are incurred.
      •    Efficient cost of service: The true-up for actual costs may influence decision
           making as the true-up will mean that the costs of when and how to
           operationalise SDP are automatically passed through to customers. This may
           unintentionally result in sub-optimal decision making on water management.
           However, a true-up for efficient costs, based on an ex-post review, would
           support more cost-reflective prices.
      a Sydney Water, 2024 Pricing Proposal to IPART, September 2024, p 225.
      Source: IPART analysis. Sydney Water, Price Proposal 2025–2030, September 2024, p 225.




Bayside Council noted its support for expanding the SDP. 189 In its submission to the Draft Report, the
SDP supported a true-up for costs related to this expansion. 190


Bulk water price true-up
Sydney Water primarily sources bulk water from 2 sources – WaterNSW and SDP – which
represent over a quarter of its operating expenditure. Our current methodology incorporates
forecast bulk water costs from WaterNSW and SDP over the 2025 determination period.
Specifically, forecast bulk water costs will be based on:

•   forecast volumes from each of WaterNSW and SDP

•   current prices for SDP as under the current SDP 2023 Determination, and




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                     Page | 100
                                                                                                              Risk allocation




•    new prices for WaterNSW based on the WaterNSW Greater Sydney 2025 Determination.

However, we recognise that over the 2025 determination period, there may be new price
determinations related to sources of bulk water supply. For example, the next SDP price review is
expected to begin in 2026, with a new determination and prices from 1 July 2027.

Bulk water prices are determined by IPART and Sydney Water will have to bear any change in
prices within a determination period. Given the uncertainty in the timing, materiality and price
structures arising from future bulk water price determinations, changes can impact:

•    Sydney Water’s financial performance and position, and

•    incentives on where, and how much, it sources bulk water.
As such, there is merit in considering a true-up for differences in forecast and actual bulk water
prices over the 2025 determination period. f

We preferred a true-up over a cost-pass through mechanism, since the quantum of the change in
bulk water prices is currently not known. A true-up would enable the Tribunal at the time to
consider the implications of changes in bulk water prices and make decisions to manage any bill
increases at the next review of Sydney Water’s prices.

A cost pass-through would reduce the risk of Sydney Water bearing potentially large bulk water
price increases that may impact its financial performance and position. However, it would limit a
Tribunal’s decision on price structures in a future price determination for bulk water supply
authorities and delay Tribunal consideration of whether the additional costs represent an efficient
use of bulk water and fair prices for customers.

It will be open to the Tribunal to consider the potential adjustment under a true-up. We note any
adjustment would likely be limited to the difference in forecast and actual bulk water price per
kilolitre, rather than an adjustment for differences in forecast and actual bulk water volume.
Otherwise, this could distort Sydney Water’s incentives to optimise how it sources bulk water.
Additionally, changes in volumes due to demand would be accounted for in the Demand Volatility
Adjustment Mechanism. Therefore, a separate true-up for changes in volume would be provided if
the actual demand changes by more than 5% than what is set in Sydney Water’s 2025
Determination.

In their submissions to the Draft Report, Sydney Water and SDP supported a true up for bulk water
price changes. 191


Regulatory mechanism to true-up for growth-related expenditure
In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water sought an in-period true-up to recover growth
costs that exceed its funding envelope for capital expenditure. g




f
    While Sydney Water could be impacted if bulk water prices materially increase, this risk could be mitigated as the water
    regulation framework allows the Tribunal to consider an earlier review of all or part of a price determination.
g
    Sydney Water considered an in-period annual true-up or cost pass-through, or an end of term true-up to address
    growth-related capital expenditure. Sydney Water assessed these options in terms of bill impacts, allocations of
    financing risk, incentives to invest efficiently and transparency and simplicity. It considered that the in-period annual
    true-up was the preferable option. Sydney Water submission to IPART 2025 Sydney Water price review – Draft Report,
    June 2025, pp 24-25, 55, 130-131




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                      Page | 101
                                                                                          Risk allocation




Our decision is to not accept this regulatory mechanism. When we set prices, we make a forecast
of efficient future capital expenditure over the price path (the funding envelope). Usually, outturn
costs will be different than we forecast. When the time comes to set prices at the next review, we
look backwards and include actual efficient capital expenditure over that period in the Regulatory
Asset Base.

This means that if a water business spends more (or less) than we forecast at the previous
determination, we include the actual efficient amount in the asset base so it can earn a return on it
going forward. However, there is a financial penalty (reward) when a business invests more (less)
than we forecast, as it is not compensated for the holding cost (or the notional return on assets) of
the difference between what we forecast and what it actually spent within a period.
The Justice and Equity Centre considered Sydney Water was the party best placed to carry and
manage any uncertainty risk related to growth expenditure in the short-medium term. It noted that
any necessary expenditure not accounted for in the 2025 Determination would still be added to
Sydney Water’s capital base and recovered over the long-term as a return on Sydney Water’s assets.
Should the NSW Government consider this insufficient to enable growth, the Justice and Equity
Centre considered it retains scope to contribute to Sydney Water’s funding for this purpose. The
Justice and Equity Centre thought this was not an appropriate risk for consumers to assume in full. 192

Sydney Water’s revised funding envelope for capital expenditure in this Final Report factors in
higher growth scenarios than our draft decision. We consider this reduces the revenue
uncertainty – and materiality of any potential holding cost risks – arising from Sydney Water
servicing new developments over the 2025 determination period, removing the need for its
proposed regulatory mechanism.

In any case, we do not consider there is merit in a true-up mechanism for efficient growth costs.
Sydney Water has flexibility to service population growth, meaning it has influence over what
expenditure to include in the true-up. This may incentivise Sydney Water to prefer investments it can
true-up, rather than the mix of infrastructure priorities that optimise customer value. It would also be
difficult to define ‘growth costs’ in advance without effectively locking-in specific types of projects.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                 Page | 102

                                Price setting

Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                     Page | 103
                                                                                          Price setting




   Summary of decisions on price setting


   We have accepted Sydney Water’s demand forecast method, adjusted for the
   price elasticity of demand and expected water leakage savings

   Our demand decision is based on Sydney Water’s forecasting method, adjusted for
   elasticity response to higher prices and lower water leakage. We expect sales volumes and
   total demand to decrease marginally in 2025-26 and 2026-27 and remain relatively stable
   over the remaining period.


   We have maintained Sydney Water’s ±5% demand volatility adjustment
   mechanism (DVAM) materiality threshold
   A ±5% DVAM threshold strikes a fair balance in sharing revenue risk between Sydney Water
   and customers, while providing incentives for accurate demand forecasts.


   We have accepted Sydney Water’s price structures for water, wastewater,
   stormwater and other charges
   Sydney Water retained its single tariff structure for water prices, setting the variable usage
   charge with respect to long-run marginal cost, after customer consultation showed
   customers preferred simplicity and equity. Some stakeholders suggested wastewater
   usage charges should also reflect long-run marginal cost, but we agree with Sydney Water
   that this is impractical since discharge volumes are not measured and cost drivers are
   external.


   We have set the variable water usage charge to increase incrementally each
   year to better reflect the long-run marginal cost (LRMC) by 2029–30
   We have maintained our draft variable water usage charge that increases over the 2025
   determination period to better reflect the revised LRMC by 2029–30, keeping the fixed
   service charge constant to recover remaining efficient costs — achieving a similar outcome
   to Sydney Water’s revised glide path.


   Stormwater charges should reflect full costs of providing stormwater services
   We did not accept Sydney Water’s proposal to introduce a uniform ‘postage stamp’
   stormwater charge for flood protection while shifting other stormwater costs associated
   with waterway health to wastewater customers. Stormwater services primarily manage
   increased runoff from urban development to protect local waterways from erosion and
   pollution and do not deliver waterway health benefits beyond statutory requirements.
   Shifting these capital costs to wastewater customers would misalign costs and benefits,
   distorting price signals over time, especially when developer contributions cease.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 104
                                                                                           Price setting




    We have spread income tax payable on developer contributions across
    wastewater customers
    We accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to recover income tax on developer contributions
    from wastewater customers. These taxes are indirect costs that could otherwise have
    unintended, distortionary impacts on stormwater prices.


    All remaining Rouse Hill Land Charges should cease on commencement of the
    new determination

    We did not accept Sydney Water’s proposal to phase out the Rouse Hill Land Charge
    gradually. Instead, we have taken a forward-looking approach and removed the charge
    from the start of the 2025 determination period. We have included the outstanding
    payments in the notional revenue requirement to be recovered from stormwater and
    wastewater customers, consistent with our view that this is a least cost facility and costs
    should be funded by all customers and not ring-fenced to the Rouse Hill area.


    We have adjusted prices for the costs of deferring the determination start and
    removing the Sydney Desalination Plan (SDP) pass through mechanism

    To make neither Sydney Water nor customers financially better or worse off from the
    delay, we have trued-up the difference between revenue collected under current prices
    and what would have been collected under the new determination. This adds around 1.3%
    to prices across the determination period. Similarly, we have added an uplift to the fixed
    water service charge in 2025-26 and 2026-27 to recover SDP pass through costs incurred
    in the 2020 determination period.


This chapter sets out our approach to assessing and decisions on Sydney Water’s:

•   forecast demand

•   demand volatility adjustment mechanism
•   long-run marginal cost of water

•   proposed price structures and cost allocation for water, wastewater and stormwater services

•   discharge factors.

It also discusses price adjustments to make Sydney Water and customers no better or worse off
for our deferred determination start date.

These elements inform the prices that we set in Chapter 9.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 105
                                                                                           Price setting




8.1      Demand

Our decisions are:

             22. To adopt Sydney Water’s water demand forecast over the determination period,
                 adjusted for the price elasticity of demand and water leakage savings.



             23. To maintain Sydney Water’s ±5% demand volatility adjustment mechanism (DVAM)
                 materiality threshold.


Understanding past and future demand for water services is important for setting prices. As
required under sections 14A(2)(i) and 15(1)(j) of the Independent Pricing and Regulatory Tribunal Act
1992 (IPART Act), we set prices using forecasts of:

•     the number of customers we expect would receive water services in each year of the
      determination period (forecast customer numbers)
•     the volume of water we expect a water business would provide for each of those years
      (forecast water sales volumes).

Further information on demand forecasts and what businesses are required to do to justify their
forecasts is available in section 4.7.2 of the Water Regulation Handbook.

There are a lot of factors that impact water demand. The most important factors are:

•     the population mix, number of dwellings, and mix of residential property types

•     water efficiency schemes influencing adoption of water saving technologies
•     changing consumption behaviours, including the influence of water conservation campaigns

•     demographics of customers, including age and socioeconomic status, and

•     a changing and more variable climate.

Wastewater volumes depend largely on water demand forecasts as they are based on a
proportion of water discharged.

Over the 2020 determination period, Sydney Water’s water sales were 9.0% lower than forecast.
Sydney Water stated that this was driven by weather variability, lasting impacts from water
restrictions between June 2019 to November 2020 and the impact of COVID-19 on population
growth and tourism. 193




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 106
                                                                                                      Price setting




8.1.1       We accepted Sydney Water’s forecast demand and sales volumes
            with adjustments

Sydney Water forecasts that demand would decrease from 546 GL to 542 GL in 2025–26, then
increase by 5 GL each year until 2030. Key drivers of this were customer growth, the proposed
increase in the variable water usage price, and the savings due to water conservation activities
and water recycling. Sydney Water noted that climate change and weather are key sensitivities. 194
In its forecasts, Sydney Water included a price elasticity of demand of -0.2 for single dwellings
and -0.07 for multi-dwellings. 195

Our decision on forecast water sales volumes and total demand largely accepts Sydney Water’s
method, but adjusts volumes to reflect:

•      an elasticity response to our maximum prices

•      our decision to include capital expenditure for digital meters, which is likely to result in water
       leakage savings.

This results in sales volumes and total demand decreasing marginally in 2025-26 and 2026-27
and remaining relatively stable over the period, compared with Sydney Water’s original forecast
that volumes would marginally increase from 2026-27 (see Table 8.1).

Table 8.1 Decision on forecast water sales volumes

    Demand (GL)b                     542.4                 544.4                 550.7        554.3         559.1
