While some stakeholders raised concerns about the demand and pricing impacts from the rapid
growth of data centres, we consider this requires no adjustment to Sydney Water’s demand
projections at this time (see Box 8.1). While data centres may source bulk water from Sydney
Water, they may also be able to access recycled water from either Sydney Water or third-party
provider. Sydney Water can enter into unregulated pricing agreements and development
servicing plans with to recover any additional costs of providing water services to data centres
and similar large industrial customers. However, it would be open to Sydney Water to consider
whether to introduce a bespoke price structure for data centres in the 2030 determination period.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                           Page | 107
                                                                                                                          Price setting




      Box 8.1 The impact of data centres on demand and price setting
      Growing demand for new technologies, including AI, increases the need for large-
      scale data generation and storage facilities. Data centres may place substantial
      pressure on water infrastructure, by consuming vast quantities of water for cooling
      purposes.

      Several submissions raised concerns about the demand and pricing impacts of data
      centres. The City of Sydney and Bayside Council stated that data centres require up
      to 12.5 GL per year more water than currently anticipated in Sydney Water’s plan.a
      The City of Ryde stated that data centres require vast quantities of water for cooling
      systems and operational efficiency adding substantial demand to Sydney Water’s
      capacity. It expressed concern that Sydney Water had paused new development
      connections in Macquarie Park – an area with a high concentration of data centres -
      because of water supply constraints. It recommended introducing a differentiated
      cost structure for data centres that considers land use.b

      Large industrial customers like data centres source bulk water from Sydney Water.
      There may also be scope for these services to be provided using recycled water,
      which could be delivered by Sydney Water or third-party operators licensed under
      the Water Industry Competition Act 2006 (WIC Act). This could reduce the impact on
      demand for drinking water from Sydney Water. Nonetheless, this may still require
      Sydney Water to provide back-up capacity if recycled water is unavailable or offline.

      Our price structures, outlined in section 8.2, provide appropriate signals for efficient
      and equitable water provision to large customers like data centres. This applies
      whether Sydney Water is the primary provider, or provides latent capacity as a back-
      up. Data centres pay the same usage charge based on the long-run marginal cost of
      supply and a service charge based on meter size, which reflects peak demand. This
      encourages them to locate in areas with lower supply costs and encourages
      competition from recycled water suppliers.

      Our developer charges determination sets out a method that allows new or
      expanded infrastructure costs to be recovered from developments that create them.
      Data centres that use excess capacity or require upgrades pay the difference
      between system-wide average costs and the higher costs of servicing their area.
      Note: Sydney Water can also negotiate bilateral, unregulated pricing agreements with individual high-demand customers,
      if they comply with IPART’s developer charging framework and are adequately ring-fenced. This allows bespoke, cost-
      reflective pricing to match the specific infrastructure and service needs of the customer while protecting the broader
      customer base from bearing significant costs from others.

      a. City of Sydney submission to IPART Draft Report, June 2025, p 2; Bayside Council submission to IPART Draft Report,
      June 2025, p 1.
      b. City of Ryde submission to IPART Draft Report, June 2025, pp 1-2.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                 Page | 108
                                                                                             Price setting




8.1.2   We propose to maintain Sydney Water’s ±5% demand volatility adjustment
        mechanism (DVAM) materiality threshold

In the 2020 determination, we used the DVAM as a tool to account for water sales uncertainty
(see Chapter 6). The DVAM allows for an adjustment to a business’s NRR to account for over- or
under-recovery of revenue due to material differences between forecast and actual water sales.

Sydney Water proposed to continue the DVAM mechanism for the 2025 determination period,
but with a zero per cent materiality threshold. 196 It claimed that this would avoid windfall gains or
losses and better incentivise water conservation by limiting the connection between water sales
and revenue, aligning with its customers expressed priorities of affordability and water
conservation. 197

Sydney Water claimed that the zero per cent threshold would allow it to manage external risks
more flexibly, or to pursue further opportunities to improve customer value. 198 However, it did not
provide any examples of other risks or opportunities to support this claim, and it does not
logically follow that reducing one risk means risk appetite for another is increased.

We consider that a zero per cent threshold is not in the interests of customers, as it does not
share revenue risk between customers and the business, and does not incentivise Sydney Water
to forecast demand accurately. We considered other approaches to incentivising more accurate
demand forecasting, such as excluding holding costs from the DVAM true-up. However, in
practice this would have a relatively minor impact on the overall true-up value.

Our decision is to continue with the current arrangement of a ±5% threshold for Sydney
Water’s DVAM.


8.2     Price structure

Our decisions are:

            24. To approve Sydney Water’s proposed price structures including:
                a. setting the variable water usage charge with respect to long-run marginal cost
                    of water supply
                 b.   setting the wastewater usage charge based on deemed usage, updated for
                      inflation
                 c.   setting fixed service charges to recover remaining efficient costs.



            25. To increase the variable water usage charge over the 2025 determination period
                from $3.17/kL to $3.58/kL to better reflect the long-run marginal cost and
                customer preferences for increased control over bills.



            26. To set stormwater charges so they reflect full-service costs, including residual
                scheme costs over time.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                  Page | 109
                                                                                          Price setting




            27. To recover income taxes on developer contributions for stormwater services from
                wastewater customers to minimise any distortionary impacts they may have on
                stormwater prices.



            28. To cease all remaining Rouse Hill Land Charge payments from the
                commencement of the 2025 determination period.


For the 2025 determination period, we have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to retain its
current price structures of:

•   Water: a single tier tariff with a variable usage and fixed service charge

•   Wastewater: a fixed service charge based on deemed usage and service for residential, and
    a fixed service charge with a single tier variable usage charge for non-residential customers
•   Stormwater: a fixed service charge based on single or multi dwelling for residential, and lot
    size and network impact for non-residential customers
•   Drought charge: an uplift price per kilolitre added to the variable water usage charge (see
    Chapter 7)

•   Unfiltered water charge: a flat discount on the variable water usage charge.

We have made decisions to increase the variable water usage charge incrementally to a level
that better reflects the long-run marginal cost of water supply and set a constant water service
charge over the 2025 determination period.

Our price structure decisions balance our requirements under section 15(1) of the
IPART Act to consider:

•   the need for greater efficiency in the supply of services so as to reduce costs for the benefit
    of consumers and taxpayers (section 15(1)(e)
•   the need to promote competition in the supply of the services concerned (section 15(1)(i)

•   the social impact of the determinations and recommendations (section 15(1)(k).

Chapter 9 outlines the fixed and variable charges a customer would have to pay for the 2025
determination period. This section discusses our decisions on the proposed split between fixed
and variable charges put forward by Sydney Water.


8.2.1   Stakeholders’ views on price setting

Sydney Water consulted customers on price structures in Phase 6 of its ‘Our Water, Our Voice’
engagement process. Sydney Water found that customers value affordability and ‘user pays’
most highly preferred to retain the current flat tariff pricing mechanism over other alternatives
canvassed, including an inclining block tariff. 199




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 110
                                                                                          Price setting




Customers generally perceived the current bills they receive to be ‘fair’ in that you pay for what
you use. However, customers also expressed a view that a larger portion of the bills should be
variable with usage, allowing them more control over their expenses. They thought this would
increase fairness, particularly for low water users who currently face bills dominated by fixed
service charges. 200

However, Sydney Water considered this to be impractical for wastewater because:

•   discharge volumes are not measured

•   the chemical concentration and stormwater infiltration are major wastewater cost drivers,
    which are largely outside of customers’ control. 201

On stormwater pricing, Sydney Water found that customers strongly supported waterway health,
but had mixed views on funding stormwater services. Overall, they preferred everyone to pay the
same regardless of location, citing:

•   stormwater services that provide waterway health outcomes are essential, like healthcare,
    public transport and education

•   people move in and out of different areas

•   everyone should contribute to Sydney’s liveability

•   stormwater services should be more streamlined and transparent. 202

During our consultation process through the Public Hearing, submissions to our Issues Paper and
Draft Report and customer survey responses, stakeholders told us that Sydney Water’s proposed
price increases were too high and were concerned about affordability and cost-of-living impacts.
For this reason, they stated a preference for cost increases to be put on the variable usage
charge, rather than the fixed service charge to support bill control and incentivise water
conservation. More than half of respondents to our customer survey on the Draft Report were
either in favour of, or neutral to, our proposal to increase the variable usage charge over the
determination period. A few stakeholders did not support having more of the cost increase being
put on the variable usage charge, because:

•   water is not currently scarce (hence, setting a higher variable usage charge may send an
    inappropriate price signal) 203
•   certain customer groups, such as low income renters, may experience disproportionate
    affordability impacts. 204

Sydney Water accepted our draft price structure in its revised price proposal. While it considered
that it is more economically efficient to set the variable usage charge equal to the LRMC estimate
for the entirety of the regulatory period, it acknowledged that a more customer aligned outcome
would be to smooth the bill impact of this step change. However, it expressed the view that
IPART had not adequately considered the additional revenue risk associated with a higher split of
variable charges, or the role customers have in choosing price controls. 205

We heard mixed views on stormwater charges. Some opposed postage stamp pricing for
stormwater 206, while others saw no issue or advocated for a statewide charge 207. One stakeholder
noted that local government areas (LGAs) levy stormwater charges too and warned against
double charging. 208




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 111
                                                                                       Price setting




There were some concerns raised that Sydney Water’s proposed LRMC estimates do not provide
appropriate signals for wastewater investment and usage. They suggested that Sydney Water
should calculate costs of supplying wastewater services in its catchments to align price signals
for investment and provide information to the market about the value of wastewater recycling. 209

One stakeholder proposed allowing multi-unit building owners to buy water and wastewater at a
commercial rate at the property boundary and on-sell to individually metered units at regulated
rates. They suggested this could reduce administration costs improve efficiency and create fairer
pricing between Sydney Water and building owners. 210


8.2.2 Water pricing

Sydney Water’s modelling indicates that the long-run marginal cost of water supply
is around $3.92/kL ($2024-25)
The LRMC includes the costs of future supply augmentation measures and therefore signals the
costs of supplying water to meet demand over the long-term. More information about our
approach to the LRMC is available in Appendix C of our Water Regulation Handbook.

Sydney Water has maintained its current approach of setting the variable component of the
variable water usage charge with respect to its estimates of the LRMC of water. It used several
methods to estimate the LRMC, including the IPART algebraic method (with and without
accounting for spare capacity), marginal incremental cost (Turvey) method and average
incremental cost (AIC) method. This yielded a range from $1.64/kL to $7.15/kL ($2024–25).
Sydney Water ultimately proposed an LRMC of $3.12/kL ($2024-25) based on a 543 GL yield and
a 4.4% real pre-tax discount rate. 211

We identified some modelling errors in Sydney Water’s proposal that suggest some of these
estimates are too low:

1.   Sydney Water’s use of the IPART algebraic method overstated spare capacity (73 GL),
     inflating available supply and lowering the LRMC.

2.   Sydney Water’s use of the Turvey method assumed fixed timing for augmentations like SDP
     and Purified Recycled Water (PRW), making it unresponsive to demand changes and
     resulting in artificially low values.

After adjusting these assumptions, we found that the Turvey method gives an LRMC of around
$3.90/kL ($2024-25) and the algebraic method gives an LRMC of $3.92/kL ($2024-25).


Stakeholders generally supported recovering more of the revenue requirement from
variable usage charges
Given that there was a slight preference among stakeholders for recovering more of the revenue
requirement through the variable usage charge, we have retained this structure. However,
increases to Sydney Water’s expenditure allowance since the Draft Report mean that the revenue
requirement is higher. We considered whether these additional costs should be recovered by
increasing the variable usage charge further, potentially up to the level of Sydney Water’s
estimated LRMC, or increasing the fixed service charge.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                              Page | 112
                                                                                                                            Price setting




Increasing the variable water usage charge further would make typical households
worse off by 2029-30
Increasing the variable usage charge further to $3.99/kL ($2025-26) by 2029-30 has several
pros and cons, but ultimately makes typical households a worse off by 2029-30. Typical
households would pay around 2.9% higher bills by 2029-30, despite having more control over
their usage. Table 8.2 below compares the bill impacts on typical households as the variable
usage charge increases to LRMC level by 2029-30.

Compared to the current usage charge and what Sydney Water proposed, maintaining our draft
decision to increase the variable usage charge to $3.58 ($2025-26) by 2029-30 provides a
balance between improving price signals to support sustainable long-term planning and efficient
water use, and allowing customers some ability to minimise the bill impacts. Around 88% of the
revenue requirement would be recovered through variable usage charges.

Remaining fixed service charges would recover the residual revenue not forecast to be recovered
by the variable usage charge. This varies by connection type whereby residential customers are
assumed to have a 20 mm meter, while non-residential water customers pay based on their actual
meter size in relation to the 20 mm base. Non-residential customers sharing a common meter
share the fixed service charge. The fixed service charge would recover around 12% of the revenue
requirement on average across the 2025 determination period.

While our fixed service charge is higher than what we proposed in our Draft Report, it is lower
than what Sydney Water proposed from 2026-27 onwards. For the typical residential customer,
the fixed service charge would increase from $67.04 (nominal) in 2024-25 to $96.09 ($2025–26)
from 1 October 2025 (excluding SDP adjustments discussed in section 8.3) and remain constant
over the determination period.


We have made no change to Sydney Water’s current price structure for multi-unit
residential buildings
In its submission to our Draft Report, Meriton Group suggested a change to pricing arrangements
that allows building owners to buy from Sydney Water and on-sell water and wastewater
services to individually metered units within the building. Customers would be billed at regulated
rates while the building owner would pay a commercial rate at the property boundary. It stated
that this could reduce the administrative burden and transactions costs incurred by Sydney
Water and building owners and would provide efficiency incentives by adjusting prices in
response to demand. 212

We are not convinced that this would lead to better pricing outcomes for customers living in multi-
unit dwellings. They may be charged for water and wastewater services on the same basis as
standalone houses, which would over-recover the costs of common infrastructure and services.


8.2.3 Wastewater pricing

Sydney Water’s proposed wastewater charges vary by connection size and type:

•   Residential customers would pay a fixed service charge based on a 20 mm connection and a
    75% discharge factor, plus a usage charge based on a deemed usage of 150 kL.

•   Non-residential customers would pay a fixed service charge based on actual meter size and
    estimated discharge factor, and a variable usage charge per kilolitre of estimated usage.

Sydney Water proposed to maintain the current usage charge, adjusted for inflation. It proposed
one change from the 2020 Determination, which is to incorporate the discretionary charge into
the fixed service charge. 213


    metered (i.e. serviced by a common meter) these households pay a usage component based on their unit entitlement
    (this is a percentage apportionment of total water usage of the building).



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                              Page | 114
                                                                                         Price setting




Sydney Water considered short-run marginal cost (SRMC) and LRMC methods to set the variable
usage charge but found them unsuitable due to limited data and weak links between costs and
discharge volumes. Wastewater is not metered, the usage is price inelastic, and major cost
drivers are not volume-based. Instead, it proposes keeping the current variable usage charge
(inflation-adjusted) until a better pricing method is developed.

We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposed wastewater charge structure and agree that
estimating a wastewater LRMC is not meaningful. We note that consideration of what information
should be published to promote competition in the wastewater and recycled water sector was
given in our recent review of Sydney Water’s operating licence. Our recommendations in that
review balanced public information needs with the administrative burden on Sydney Water of
producing detailed and widespread network data that may not be useful to all stakeholders. We
note that Sydney Water’s development servicing plans also provide detailed information of this
nature and competitors can request data about specific network points.


We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposed discharge factor
The discharge factor measures the percentage of a customer’s water consumption that is
discharged to the wastewater network. It effectively converts the size of a water meter to a
wastewater meter (for meter-based fixed service charges) and estimates wastewater discharge
volumes (to apply deemed wastewater usage charges). Discharge factors are used because,
unlike water consumption, wastewater discharges are often not separately metered.

Sydney Water proposed to use a discharge factor of 75 per cent for most residential, non-
residential, unmetered, boarding house and ‘child’ properties in a joint water supply service
arrangement. 214 We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal.


Residential wastewater customers will pay around $2 more to fund Sydney Water’s
potential acquisition of Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater facilities
Hawkesbury City Council currently provides wastewater services to around 8,500 customers,
comprising around 7,500 households and 1,000 businesses. These customers typically pay
wastewater bills of around $1,300 per year.

Sydney Water may acquire Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater business during the 2025
determination period. 215 If this occurs, customers formerly serviced by Hawkesbury City Council
would begin to receive wastewater services from Sydney Water. They would pay the same
wastewater prices as Sydney Water’s other customers.

In our Draft Report, we consulted on:

•   whether Sydney Water should charge a separate price to Hawkesbury City Council
    wastewater customers, if it takes over responsibility for providing wastewater services to
    those customers, and

•   what other factors we should consider.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 115
                                                                                          Price setting




We received over 60 written and verbal submissions from Hawkesbury City Council residents.
These strongly supported Sydney Water’s acquisition of Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater
systems and costs being spread across all customers. Some stakeholders also commented that
Sydney Water was better placed to manage these assets than the council. Hawkesbury City
Council’s submission pointed out that ratepayers currently pay around 3-4 times more than
Sydney Water’s wastewater customers and would benefit from improved river health outcomes
that would not be affordable under the current ring-fenced arrangement. 216

In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water supported integrating Hawkesbury City
Council’s customers into its broader network. It provided forecasts of the capital and operating
costs to bring Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater assets up to current regulated
environmental standard, which we have included an allowance for in the NRR (see Chapter 5). It
would also receive revenue from current Hawkesbury wastewater customers.

The increased costs of integrating Hawkesbury City Council’s wastewater assets into Sydney
Water’s wastewater assets adds around $3 to the wastewater service charge (which adds around
$2 to residential wastewater bills).


8.2.4 Stormwater pricing

Sydney Water provides stormwater services to approximately 630,000 residential and non-
residential customers. 217 Charges are currently fixed, with residential customers paying based on
dwelling type and non-residential customers paying based on lot size and network impact.

Sydney Water proposed three major changes to its stormwater charges including:

1.   a uniform 'postage stamp' stormwater charge based on flood protection, excluding waterway
     health costs

2.   shifting waterway health related capital costs to wastewater customers, on the basis that all
     customers benefit

3.   phasing out the Rouse Hill Land Charge, with future and residual costs covered by
     developers and wastewater customers. 218


Stormwater customers should pay the full cost of providing stormwater services
We have not accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to charge a postage stamp stormwater charge
for flood protection only and shift other costs to wastewater customers. Instead, we have decided
that stormwater charges should reflect full-service costs, including residual scheme costs over
time. This is consistent with Sydney Water’s customer consultation, which found that customers
had mixed views on whether everyone should fund stormwater services, but considered that all
stormwater customers should pay the same regardless of location.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 116
                                                                                           Price setting




We consider that Sydney Water’s proposal is inconsistent with several of the cost allocation
principles that guide our decision-making including:

•   impactor pays – it does not reflect the purpose of the infrastructure for preventing waterway
    degradation from localised urban development
•   beneficiary pays – it does not reflect the full benefits stormwater customers receive and
    overstates the benefits wastewater customers receive
•   cost-reflective pricing – it does not provide efficient price signals because stormwater charges
    do not reflect the full cost of service provision and wastewater charges overstate them.

Sydney Water’s largest investments in stormwater services are in the Rouse Hill region and the
new Mamre Road and Aerotropolis development precincts. The main purpose of stormwater
systems in these areas is to manage increased runoff from new urban and industrial
development, protecting local waterways from pollution and erosion. This is not the same as
providing a waterway health benefit that goes beyond what is required to meet waterway health
targets for new developments.

Shifting stormwater capital costs to wastewater customers could lead to increasingly misaligned
price signals in the longer term, once developer contributions towards these services cease.
Wastewater customers would pay for ongoing maintenance, renewals and return on and of
capital for stormwater assets.

We consider that it is reasonable to recover costs from wastewater customers where stormwater
services are provided to improve water quality, safety and the overall health of waterways
connected to Sydney Water’s stormwater network. For example, the costs of Sydney Water’s
Waterway Health Improvement Program. This program delivers waterway health improvements
to customers that are not directly connected to Sydney Water’s stormwater network. We have
accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to recover these costs from wastewater customers.


Income tax on developer contributions for stormwater schemes should be recovered
from wastewater customers
We have accepted Sydney Water’s proposal to recover income tax on developer contributions
from wastewater customers. These taxes are an indirect cost and may have unintended,
distortionary impacts on stormwater prices.

One of the primary residual costs of Sydney Water’s stormwater schemes is income taxes on
developer charges. Over the next 10 years, the income taxes payable on the Rouse Hill and
Mamre Road-Aerotropolis developer charges for stormwater assets would exceed $940 million
(real $2024–25).

While allocating these costs to stormwater customers may seem more consistent with
beneficiary pays and cost-reflective pricing principles, we note that:

•   developers already pay for the full net cost increase associated with these services

•   because of how the Australian Tax Office treats cash contributions, tax liabilities occur
•   including these taxes in the charges payable by directly connected customers is unlikely to
    improve efficiency signals in the short or longer run, or influence behaviour.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                   Page | 117
                                                                                          Price setting




Allocating these costs to wastewater adds approximately 0.2% or $2 to the typical annual
wastewater bill, compared to 10.3% higher bills (or $13 per year) for stormwater customers.


We have maintained our draft decision to remove the Rouse Hill Land Charge
We have removed the Rouse Hill Land Charge from the start of the 2025 determination period.
Outstanding costs will be recovered through developer and residual stormwater charges.

Sydney Water proposed a phased approach whereby:

•   properties that have begun paying the charge, but have not completed payments before the
    start of the new determination period or the registration of the Rouse Hill Stormwater
    Development Servicing Plan (DSP), would continue paying (resulting in some households
    making payments until 2030–31)
•   new properties that can demonstrate that they have paid the Rouse Hill Stormwater DSP
    charge applicable to that property would be exempt from paying the Rouse Hill land
    charge. 219

We considered this approach unnecessarily complex and inequitable, with some households
continuing to pay the charge until 2030–31. It also does not incentivise Sydney Water to
register the Rouse Hill Stormwater DSP in a timely manner to start collecting developer
charges.

In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water opposed our draft decision stating that it
would unfairly benefit around 4,000 customers who have not paid, while more than 10,000
customers have paid in full. It said billing system changes for a gradual phase out are already in
place so removing the charge would not save costs. It also noted that the developer charges
methodology offers no way to recover these costs and Sydney Water is already motivated to
register the Rouse Hill DSP because it cannot collect developer charges for new connections
until then. 220

We are not persuaded by Sydney Water’s arguments to continue the Rouse Hill Land Charge into
the 2025 determination period. Maintaining alignment with past outcomes is not a sufficient
reason to continue a policy that no longer serves broader customer interests. Setting an end date
for the charge is more consistent with our intention to treat the Rouse Hill Integrated Water Cycle
Management (IWCM) facility as a least cost solution going forward, whereby costs should be
funded by all customers rather than ring-fenced. Under this approach:

•   current outstanding charges would be recovered through stormwater customer charges

•   new costs from 1 October 2025 be recovered from developers.

The net present value of remaining Rouse Hill land charges payable is comparatively small at
around $5.0 million. The revenue from new connections that occur before 1 October 2025 that
are not already included in these forecasts is likely to be immaterial.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 118
                                                                                          Price setting




8.2.5 Unfiltered water charge

Sydney Water supplies unfiltered water services to one industrial customer and proposes to
continue using the current pricing method - applying a discount to the drinking water charge to
reflect the avoided filtration costs. The proposed discount is $0.38/kL ($2025–26). 221 We have
accepted Sydney Water’s method for calculating the unfiltered water charge.


8.2.6 Drought uplift charge

As discussed in Chapter 7, we have decided to retain Sydney Water’s existing drought pricing
mechanism, which sets a dynamic drought water usage price to signal to customers the higher
costs for water businesses to manage drought and incentivise them to manage their water usage
during drought conditions.

When the drought trigger is met, a drought price uplift is added to the variable water usage
charge. The uplift is based on forecast demand reductions (7.7%) and estimated drought response
costs of $53.1 million ($2024–25).

We have calculated this to be $0.41/kL. This is lower than Sydney Water’s proposal of $0.66/kL
because of:

•   assumed volume changes (from the price elasticity response to the change in usage charge)
    and Sydney Water’s incorrect application of the price elasticity of demand in its original
    drought uplift charge calculations
•   the removal of SDP volumetric costs for full production, which are included in the base bulk
    water costs and recovered through non-drought water prices.


8.3     Price adjustments

Our decisions are:

            29. To adjust prices by 1.3% over the 2025 determination period to recover an
                expected $106.1 million shortfall because of our deferred determination start
                of 1 October 2025.



            30. To adjust the fixed water service charge to recover SDP costs incurred in the 2020
                determination period by:
                a. $21.85 in 2025-26 to recover actual costs incurred between 1 April 2024 and 31
                    March 2025
                 b.   $5.46 in 2026-27 to recover forecast costs incurred between 1 April and 30
                      June 2025.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 119
                                                                                          Price setting




8.3.1   Price adjustment for the deferred determination start date

We extended our review of Sydney Water’s prices by 3 months to allow thorough consideration
of its proposal (the ‘deferral period’). As a result, the 2025 Determination will commence on
1 October 2025. In the meantime, Sydney Water will continue charging current prices.

To make neither Sydney Water nor customers financially better or worse off from the delay, we
will true-up the difference between revenue collected under current prices and what would have
been collected under the new determination.

To do this, we:

•   calculated the notional revenue required to recover efficient costs for the 2025 determination
    period (see Chapter 6), and the prices needed to recover that revenue based on our price
    structure decisions for a 5-year determination period starting from 1 July 2025

•   estimated the revenue Sydney Water would recover from customers between 1 July and
    30 September 2025 under existing prices, and the shortfall in revenue due to the 3-month
    delay

•   adjusted prices to recover the estimated revenue shortfall and achieve revenue neutrality
    over the 2025 determination period.

As a result, prices from 1 October 2025 are 1.3% higher to recover an expected $106.1 million shortfall.


8.3.2 Price adjustments for SDP pass through costs incurred under the current
      determination

We have included an uplift to the fixed water service charge in 2025-26 and 2026-27 to account
for SDP pass-through costs incurred in the 2020 determination period.

Under the 2020 determination, Sydney Water was able to pass through its costs of purchasing
water from SDP. It did so retrospectively, by adding a pass-through amount to the fixed water
service charge for costs incurred in the previous year.

In Chapter 7 we explained our decision to discontinue the existing SDP cost pass-through
mechanism for the 2025 determination period. This means from 1 October, Sydney Water will no
longer have a mechanism to recover SDP costs it has already incurred under the 2020
determination.

The SDP costs for 2024-25 were not known when Sydney Water submitted its pricing proposal,
because SDP invoices Sydney Water annually for services provided between April and March in
the previous 12 months. We now know that Sydney Water incurred $50 million between 1 April
2024 to 31 March 2025. The actual costs for the remaining months of the 2020 determination
won’t be known until April 2026. However, given SDP costs have been similar for the last 3 years
and conditions have been stable, we have forecast a similar cost profile.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                               Page | 120
                                                                                                             Price setting




To recover these costs we have added an uplift of:

•    $21.85 to the 2025-26 water service charge for actual costs incurred between April 2024 and
     March 2025 c
•    $5.46 to the 2026-27 water service charge for forecast costs incurred between April 2025
     and June 2025.

This is consistent with how Sydney Water would have recovered costs had the existing pass
through mechanism continued.

We have made no provision for SDP costs incurred between 1 July and 30 September 2025.
While the current determination allows for Sydney Water to pass through these costs, this would
result in Sydney Water being financially better off at the expense of customers because of the
deferred determination start.




c
    This takes into consideration that Sydney Water will recover a quarter of its SDP costs between 1 July 2025 and 30
    September 2025 before the 2025 determination comes into effect.

      Summary of maximum prices


      Maximum variable water usage prices and fixed water service charges will
      increase over the 2025 determination period
      Under our decisions, the variable water usage price will increase by around 34% between
      current prices in 2024–25 and the last year of the determination in 2029–30. a At $3.58 per
      kilolitre in 2029–30, it is also 12% higher than Sydney Water’s proposed variable usage
      charge of $3.12 per kilolitre.

      While the fixed water service charge will increase by around 43% over the same period, b it
      will be around 72% lower than Sydney Water’s proposal by 2029–30. This is partly due to
      us identifying cost efficiencies in Sydney Water’s operations (see Chapters 4 and 5), and
      partly because we have set a higher water usage price. We outline in Chapter 8 why we
      consider this price structure to be in the customers’ interests, including that it gives them
      some ability to minimise the impact of price increases, by using less water.


      Maximum wastewater prices will increase over the 2025 determination period
      Our decision is to set the deemed wastewater usage charge at the level proposed by
      Sydney Water. It will increase by around 6% in the first year and remain constant in real
      terms throughout the rest of the 2025 determination period.

      We set the fixed wastewater service charge to recover Sydney Water’s remaining efficient
      costs, which is about 6% lower by 2029–30 than Sydney Water’s proposal.
      Maximum stormwater prices will increase over the 2025 determination period
      Stormwater prices are fixed charges. Under our decisions, stormwater prices will increase
      by around 31% in the first year and then remain constant in real terms for the remainder of
      the 2025 determination period.

      While in the first year our prices are slightly higher than those proposed by Sydney Water
      (8%), by the end of the 2025 determination period they are around 33% lower than those
      proposed by Sydney Water. This is due to us using lower financing costs than those
      proposed by Sydney Water, as well as identifying efficiency savings Sydney Water could
      achieve. We note our stormwater prices include stormwater costs that Sydney Water
      proposed to shift to wastewater customers, on the basis that all customers benefit from
      waterway health outcomes. We did not accept Sydney Water’s proposal, as we did not
      consider it to be cost-reflective (see Chapter 8).

    This includes inflation for 2025-26.
b
    This includes inflation for 2025-26.



Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                  Page | 123
                                                                                     Maximum prices




Sydney Water currently provides 3 main services to customers:

•   water services

•   wastewater services

•   stormwater services

Under our decisions:

•   Sydney Water’s prices for water services have 2 components:

    — A variable usage price (expressed as $ per kilolitre (kL)) of metered water supplied.
    — A fixed service price (expressed as $ per year), which is set to recover Sydney Water’s
      efficient water-related costs that are not forecast to be recovered by the usage price.

•   Variable usage prices increase during periods of drought, based on dam storage levels (see
    Chapter 8).

•   Sydney Water’s prices for wastewater services have 2 components:

    — A usage charge based on deemed or inferred usage and a price of $1.44/kL. Wastewater
      discharge volumes are not directly metered.
        •   Residential customers pay wastewater usage charges based on a deemed discharge
            volume of 150 kilolitres per year.
        •   Non-residential customers pay wastewater usage charges based on an inferred
            discharge volume, calculated using a customer specific discharge factor multiplied
            by metered water consumption.
    — A fixed service price (expressed as $ per year), which is set to recover Sydney Water’s
      efficient wastewater-related costs that are not forecast to be recovered by the usage
      price.
•   Sydney Water’s price for stormwater services is one fixed charge that applies to around
    630,000 customers, including about 65,000 non-residential customers. Stormwater charges
    are based on a customer’s impact on the network, such as:

    — property type for residential customers
    — land size for non-residential customers.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                             Page | 124
                                                                                        Maximum prices




Sydney Water also provides some recycled water and trade waste services to certain customers.

This chapter sets out the maximum prices for Sydney Water’s regulated services under our
decisions. These maximum prices bring together the decisions made in the previous chapters of
this report, including decisions on operating expenditure (Chapter 4), capital expenditure (Chapter
5), tax, regulatory depreciation, return on assets and other costs (Chapter 6), price controls, cost
pass-throughs and true-ups (Chapter 7) and demand and price structures (Chapter 8). In setting
these maximum prices, we considered how customers can be protected from abuses of
monopoly powers in terms of prices, pricing policies and standard of services, as required under
section 15(1) of the IPART Act (see Appendix A). When setting maximum prices, we allow Sydney
Water to recover only the efficient costs of providing its services, and those services are
delivered to an appropriate standard of quality, reliability and safety. c

In this chapter we also set out our decisions on prices for other monopoly services provided by
Sydney Water. These include late and declined payment fees, as well as trade waste,
miscellaneous and ancillary charges.

We present these maximum prices in $2025-26 terms. We presented our draft prices in our Draft
Report in $2024-25 terms.

Our decisions are:

9.1       Decisions on maximum water, wastewater and stormwater prices

9.1.1     Water charges

The tables below present our decisions on maximum fixed and variable prices for water. These
prices are in $2025-26. Prices from 2026-27 to 2029-30 will be subject to yearly inflation.

Sydney Water proposed to increase the fixed service charge by substantially more than the
variable usage charge. It proposed the fixed service charge increase by around $280 (or 417%)
over the 2025 determination period, compared with a 19% increase in the variable usage charge.
This would have meant that most of its proposed bill increase was through raising the fixed
service charge, which customers pay regardless of how much water they might use.

We outline in Chapter 8 why we consider it is in customers’ interests to adopt a different
approach. Instead, we have set a price structure where Sydney Water recovers most of its water
revenue from the variable usage charge rather than the fixed service charge. This means that the
variable usage charge increases by around 34% over the 2025 determination period, while the
fixed service charge increases by around 43%). We consider this is both fairer, and a better
reflection of the cost of using water. It also gives customers some ability to reduce the impact of
price increases, by using less water.

unadjusted wastewater service charges. We have updated them to reflect Sydney Water’s
efficient operating and capital costs, as well as lower financing costs. In addition, we have
removed stormwater costs associated with waterway health from these charges. As explained in
Chapter 8, we did not accept Sydney Water’s proposal to shift these costs to wastewater
customers. Instead, we have included these stormwater costs in stormwater prices.

As for water prices, the fixed wastewater service charge is set to recover the remainder of
Sydney Water’s revenue requirement and therefore recover its efficient costs.

9.2       Decision on recycled water charges
Sydney Water considers recycling water when assessing options to deliver water and
wastewater services. This approach is consistent with its customer engagement, where
customers expressed support for Sydney Water exploring options – such as recycled water – for
securing future water supplies. 222

We accept Sydney Water has applied IPART’s methodology for pricing recycled water. We have
made a decision to maintain our approach from previous price reviews and continue to defer
setting a maximum price for Sydney Water’s recycled water schemes.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                 Page | 131
                                                                                                                        Maximum prices




9.3        Decisions on late payment, and dishonoured or declined
           payment fees
We set the maximum late payment fees, as well as the dishonoured or declined payment fees,
that Sydney Water may charge in its Customer Contract. 223 Our decision is to keep these fees
unchanged in real terms, as set out in the table below. These fees are in $2025-26 terms. Fees
from 2026-27 to 2029-30 will be subject to yearly inflation.

Sydney Water proposed to increase these fees to cover its proposed increase in corporate
overheads 224. However, the Justice and Equity Centre and the Energy and Water Ombudsman
NSW both opposed the proposed increases. They noted the proposed higher fees would likely
put a greater burden on customers experiencing financial vulnerability, which often arises from
circumstances beyond customers’ control (see Chapter 3).

After noting these stakeholder comments, the minimal revenue impact on Sydney Water and the
lack of a strong justification for Sydney Water's proposed increase (beyond aligning with an
increase in corporate overheads), we have made a decision to keep the current late and declined
payment fees unchanged in real terms. In its submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water
agreed with this decision. 225

      Box 9.1 Sydney Water terms and conditions for late payment fees
      Sydney Water will not charge a late payment fee where:

      •    there is a billing matter being considered by the Energy and Water Ombudsman
            NSW

      •    the customer has made an arrangement with Sydney Water to pay by
            instalments or another payment plan, and the customer is honouring that
            arrangement

      •    part of the bill is being paid using Sydney Water’s payment assistance scheme
      •    Sydney Water is aware that the customer has sought assistance from a
            community welfare organisation that is part of the payment assistance scheme

      •     the customer is registered with Sydney Water’s BillAssist program

      •     the customer has been identified as being in hardship
      •     the customer pays by direct debit, or

      •     the Energy and Water Ombudsman NSW has asked Sydney Water to waive the fee.

      The fee will only be levied:

      •    if the customer has been notified in advance of the late payment fee and the
            circumstances in which it may be levied, and

      •    at least 7 days after the due date.
      Source: IPART Final Report, Review of prices for Sydney Water Corporation from 1 July 2016 to 30 June 2020, p 15; Sydney
      Water, Customer Contract 2024-2028.




We did not include the terms and conditions for late payment fees in the Draft Report. Sydney
Water asked us to re-publish these terms and conditions in the Final Report so customers would
be aware that those experiencing hardship remain supported. 227


9.4       Decisions on trade waste charges and miscellaneous and
          ancillary charges
We set maximum prices Sydney Water can charge its customers for:

•   Trade waste charges, which mainly involve commercial and industrial customers who
    discharge more concentrated waste into Sydney Water’s s wastewater system than regular
    domestic wastewater.

•   Miscellaneous and ancillary charges, which relate to other monopoly services Sydney
    Water provides, such as damaged meter replacements and conveyancing certificates.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                                                Page | 133
                                                                                           Maximum prices




Both trade waste and miscellaneous and ancillary charges account for a minor part of Sydney
Water’s total revenue. In 2024–25, revenue from these charges comprised around 1.5% of its
notional revenue requirement.

Sydney Water proposed changes to its trade waste charges, including updating 33 charges,
introducing 7 new charges and removing 4 existing charges. Similarly for miscellaneous and
ancillary charges, Sydney Water proposed various increases and decreases across service types.
It also proposed introducing 8 new charges and removing 20 existing charges. Most of Sydney
Water’s proposed updates to these charges involve increases in its corporate overheads,
wastewater system operating costs and administration fees.

We have reviewed Sydney Water’s proposal for these prices. We made decisions to set pollutant
trade waste charges around 15% to 20% lower than proposed. We consider our prices more
accurately reflect the costs of treating pollutants in trade waste.

We have largely accepted Sydney Water’s proposed prices for non-pollutant trade waste
charges, as well as its miscellaneous and ancillary charges. In our assessment, Sydney Water’s
price changes include both increases and decreases that appear reasonable and reflect efficient
costs for these items.

Since the Draft Report we have:

•    amended the price path for backflow prevention device annual administration fees, to correct
     for an error in information provided to us

•    not accepted Sydney Water’s proposed increase to the Interim Operating Procedure (IOP)
     charge.

The IOP charge recovers the costs of transporting wastewater via tankers to select treatment
facilities for new developments without access to a centralised wastewater connection. f In its
submission to our Draft Report, Sydney Water requested a 25% increase for this charge. 228 The
main driver of the proposed increase was lowering the asset depreciation period (from 80 years
to 15 years), which Sydney Water suggested was due to unreliable forecast IOP demand beyond
2035. We do not view lowering the depreciation period of IOP assets to be the optimal approach
to deal with the demand uncertainty for IOP services, and therefore, we maintained our draft IOP
charge (which assumes an 80-year asset life), which we have then adjusted for inflation and
changes in Sydney Water’s financing costs since the Draft Report.

The full schedule of trade waste, miscellaneous and ancillary charges are provided in Appendix
E.2 and E.3, Tables E.12 to E.16.




f
    This allows for construction to proceed before permanent infrastructure is in place.

                                Chapter 10

                                Impacts of decisions




                                10
Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                           Page | 135
                                                                                            Draft prices




   Summary of the impact of our prices


   Typical water and wastewater bills will increase by $168 including inflation
   from 1 July 2025 to 30 June 2026, then by $77 plus inflation on average each
   following year until 2030

   Under our decisions, typical household water and wastewater bills will increase by
   $168 (or 13.8%) including inflation in the first year of the 2025 determination period. They
   will then increase by $77 (or 5.1%) on average plus inflation each following year until
   30 June 2030.

   Typical bills will be $44 (or 2.6%) on average lower each year than under Sydney Water’s
   proposed prices. Sydney Water proposed a $253 (or 20.7%) increase including inflation in
   the first year of the 2025 determination period, then a $110 (or 6.8%) plus inflation bill
   increase on average each following year until 30 June 2030.

   Household customers who receive stormwater services and pay stormwater bills to
   Sydney Water will also see the stormwater component of their bill increase by $20 for
   houses and $7 for apartments (or 23.1%) including inflation in the first year of the
   2025 determination period. They will then increase by $2 for houses and $1 for apartments
   (or 1.5%) on average plus inflation each following year until 30 June 2030.

   Non-residential customer bills will increase by between 11.6% and 15.4% including inflation
   in the first year of the 2025 determination period. then increase by between 3.4% and 6.6%
   on average plus inflation each following year until 30 June 2030.


   There are options to improve the effectiveness of existing rebates to help those
   most in need
   The NSW Premier requested IPART to consider the cost-of-living impact of the price
   determination and the effectiveness of existing rebates, including if rebates will adequately
   support customers who may be disproportionately impacted by any price increases.

   We find that the pensioner rebate remains effective in maintaining water and wastewater
   affordability for households that are currently eligible for the existing rebate. However,
   there are low-income households that may still face issues with affordability.

   We make 7 recommendations to the NSW Government on how to improve the
   effectiveness of existing rebates to provide greater support to those that are most in need
   of financial support. This includes freezing the amount of existing rebates in nominal terms
   to enable the NSW Government to reprioritise its existing funding envelope to support a
   broader set of households in need (such as low-income households) or increase the rebate
   amount for other water businesses to improve equity across NSW.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 136
                                                                                                             Draft prices




10.1 Our decisions allow necessary increases to bills
We have set prices that will apply from 1 October 2025 to 30 June 2030. These prices are set in
$2025-26 terms and will increase each year until the end of the 2025 determination period.

In this report, we present bill changes to help customers understand how the prices will impact them.

We present the bills in the first year including inflation, which is the amount the typical customer
would pay. We also present the change in bills from 2026-27 each year until 2029-30. The prices
and bills for these years are in $2025-26 terms and the increases reflect the ‘real’ changes which
exclude yearly inflation the prices are subject to. Customers should expect to pay these prices
plus yearly inflation in these following years.


10.1.1 Residential customers

In discussing typical household bills, a we refer to the combined water and wastewater bill of a
household consuming 200 kilolitres b a year. Some Sydney Water customers also pay stormwater
drainage charges to Sydney Water, which means their bills are higher. c

Under our maximum prices, typical household bills will increase by:

•    $168 (or 13.8%) in the first year of the 2025 determination period
•    $77 (or 5.1%) on average each following year until 30 June 2030.

This is lower than the yearly increases under Sydney Water’s proposed prices of:

•    $253 (or 20.7%) in the first year of the 2025 determination period

•    $110 (or 6.8%) on average each following year until 30 June 2030.

Under our maximum prices, the typical household bill will increase from $1,220 in 2024–25 to:

•    $1,388 in 2025-26, which is $84 (or 5.7%) lower than the $1,472 proposed by Sydney Water
     in 2025-26
•    $1,695 in 2029-30 (the last year of the 2025 determination period), which is $218 (or 11.4%)
     lower than the $1,913 proposed by Sydney Water in 2029-30.

By the end of the 2025 determination period in 2029-30, typical household bills will increase by
22.1% after the first year increase under our prices, as opposed to 30% under Sydney Water’s
proposed prices.

        prices.

        In our Draft Report, we proposed average yearly typical water and wastewater bill
        increases of $61 (or 4.6%), plus inflation. Under the draft prices, typical household
        bills would have increased from $1,220 in 2024-25 to $1,293 in 2025-26, plus
        inflation.

        Our final maximum price decisions will lead to average yearly typical water and
        wastewater bill increases of $87 (or 6.3%), plus inflation. Under the final prices,
        typical household bills will increase from $1,220 in 2024-25 to $1,363 in 2025-26,
        plus inflation.

Water’s proposal. As discussed in Chapter 8, we decided to gradually phase in the increase to
prices each year as opposed to increasing prices in one step. This is consistent with Sydney
Water’s proposal to smooth typical customer bills across the period, rather than passing on all
required bill increases to customers in the first year, which could lead to significant bill shock.

Owner-occupiers will see bill increases under our prices, but lower than what Sydney
Water proposed
Under our maximum prices, the typical household bill would increase by $168 (or 13.8%),
including inflation, in the first year of the determination period, then by $77 (or 5.1%), plus inflation,
on average each following year until 30 June 2030. This is an increase of $307, plus inflation,
from 2025-26 after the first year price increase to the last year of the 2025 determination period
(2029-30).
For other households, water and wastewater bills will increase in the first year of the 2025
determination period by:

•    $131 (or 13.7%) for small households living in an apartment

•    $153 (or 13.8%) for typical apartments

•    $206 (or 13.8%) for large households living in a house
•    $131 (or 13.7%) for pensioner households without a rebate, and $49 (or 13.8%) with a rebate
     under the current rebate structure or $132 (or 37.2%) under the Tribunal recommended rebate
     structure.

After the first year, water and wastewater bills will increase each subsequent year on average for:
•    $63 (or 5.4%) for small households living in an apartment

•    $71 (or 5.2%) for typical apartments

•    $90 (or 4.9%) for large households living in a house
•    $63 (or 5.4%) for pensioner households without a rebate, and $22 (or 5.1%) with a rebate under
     the current rebate structure or $77 (or 13.1%) under the Tribunal recommended rebate
     structure.

These average yearly increases over the next 5 years would mostly be less than bills under
Sydney Water’s proposed prices. The average yearly increase of large and small household bills
would be 1.8 and 3.8 percentage points lower, respectively, than those proposed by Sydney
Water. The average yearly increase of bills for typical pensioner households that receive rebates
is 2.5 percentage points lower than those proposed by Sydney Water.

Typical bills under our maximum prices are on average $44 (or 2.6%) per year lower than bills
under Sydney Water’s proposed prices. Based on our maximum prices, typical household bills
would be 39.0% higher by the end of the 2025 determination period than under current prices, as
opposed to 56.9% under Sydney Water’s proposed prices (see Table E.18).

Sydney Water sends bills to property owners. However, property owners can pass on the water
usage component of their bills to their tenants. a Tenants in this situation, would experience higher
percentage increases in their bills, due to the materially higher variable water usage charge.

Household water bills for renters who pay for water usage will increase by 14% in the first year of

•   $60 for apartments using 160 kL per year
•   $75 for typical houses using 200 kL per year

•   $13 for small apartments or houses using 100 kL per year
•   $21 for apartments using 160 kL per year

17.6% from 2025-26 to 2029–30 after the first year increase.

The bills paid by landlords include the fixed water service charge, fixed wastewater service
charge and a deemed wastewater usage charge. Bills paid by landlords leasing out a rental
property with a separate meter would increase in the first year of the determination by $93
(or 13.6%) for both houses and apartments. After the first year, bills will increase each subsequent
year by $50 (or 5.9%) on average, plus inflation.

The 5.9% increase after the first year is lower than Sydney Water’s proposed 11.2% price increase
(see Table E.20).


10.2 Affordability is a concern for customers
Affordability and high inflation were key concerns among stakeholders for this review. We
recognise that prices increasing for inflation could have substantial impacts on some customers,
including pensioners.


10.2.1 Bills as a proportion of income will increase for low-income households

We have calculated affordability ratios for bills as a proportion of a household’s pre-tax income. A
systematic review of studies analysing water and wastewater affordability used a threshold
between 2 and 5%. 229 We have used a threshold of 3%, as proposed by the UN. 230

Our analysis shows that affordability ratios would remain well within the 3% threshold for most
households over the period. However, bill increases under our maximum prices would
predominantly impact low-income households.




Sydney Water prices 2025-2030                                                                Page | 142
                                                                                            9BImpacts of decisions




Table E.24 to Table E.27 shows affordability ratios under our maximum prices. For households
earning a median income of $115,880 b would see their affordability ratio increase from:

•   1.1% in 2024–25 to 1.5% by 2029–30 for a typical house

•   1.0% in 2024–25 to 1.3% by 2029–30 for a typical apartment

•   1.3% in 2024–25 to 1.8% by 2029–30 for a large household (5 or more people, who own their
    own home, live in a house with a big garden and/or pool and have relatively higher water use).

We estimate that approximately 21% of households (approximately 364,232) in the Sydney Water
service area earn incomes within the lowest income quartile (earning below $63,932 c per year).
We find that these households using:

•   134 kL per year would see their affordability ratios increasing from 1.6% in 2024–25 to 2.3% by
    2029–30.

•   200 kL per year (i.e. typical water usage) would see their affordability ratio will increase from
    1.9% in 2024–25 to 2.7% by 2029–30.
•   300 kL per year would see their affordability ratio will increase from 2.3% in 2024–25 to 3.2%
    by 2029–30.

We also find that low-income owner-occupier households eligible for Health Care Cards will see
bills representing more than 3% of their income over the 2025 determination period, including:

•   Households receiving Couple Parenting Payment from Services Australia (from 3.2% in
    2024-25 to 4.4% by 2029–30)
•   Households receiving JobSeeker Payment from Services Australia (from 4.6% in 2024–25 to
    6.4% by 2029–30 for single households without dependents, from 2.5% in 2024–25 to 3.5% by
    2029–30 for couple households without dependents by 2029–30, and from 3.2% from
    2024-25 to 4.4% by 2029–30 for couple households with 2 children) (See Table E.26)

By comparison, high-income households earning above $279,715 d a year and using 215 kL per
year would see their affordability ratio increase from 0.5% in 2024–25 to 0.6% by 2029–30. For a
high-income household with large water usage (300 kL per year), the affordability ratio will
increase from 0.5% in 2024–25 to 0.7% by 2029–30 (see Table E.24).

       the 3% threshold

With the increase to the variable water usage charge, we found that renters who are separately
metered and pay for the water usage component of the water bill will have an affordability ratio
of less than 3%.
